# Most Gracious Prince and Lord!

Your Serene Highness <note>original: "Ew. Hochfürstl. Durchlaucht." A highly formal honorific used for the ruling families of the German states. In this period, "Durchlaucht" (Serenity) was a specific rank-based address.</note> may deign to receive this book, which I hereby lay at your feet, with that same grace and affability <note>original: "Leutseeligkeit." This term implies a ruler's approachable, kind, and humane character toward their subjects.</note> that is accustomed to accompany all the actions of Your Serene Highness.

It contains a history of the most sorrowful disputes <note>A reference to the Arian controversy, a 4th-century theological conflict over the nature of Christ's divinity that caused deep divisions within the early Christian Empire.</note> that have ever troubled the Christian church. Yet, it is also intended to show that [the essence of faith] lies not in philosophical dogmas <note>original: "Lehrmeynungen." The author is signaling an Enlightenment-era preference for practical morality over the complex metaphysical debates (dogmatism) of the past.</note>, but in the heartfelt worship of God and Jesus Christ, in piety <note>original: "Gottseligkeit."</note> and mutual love,

# Preface.

There is little that needs to be said in advance of this first volume of the *History of Arianism* <note>Arianism refers to the followers of Arius (c. 250–336 AD), who taught that Jesus Christ was a created being, distinct from and subordinate to God the Father. This led to centuries of theological and political conflict.</note> for its better evaluation. According to the author’s own declaration, this history is nothing other than an attempt <note>original: "Versuch." In the Enlightenment context, this suggests a scholarly essay or a preliminary inquiry rather than a definitive textbook.</note>, and it must be judged fairly as such.

# Preface.

As much of a stir as the Arian controversies <note>The Arian controversies were 4th-century disputes over the nature of Jesus Christ’s divinity, specifically whether he was of the same substance as God the Father or a created being.</note> have caused in the Christian world, there is nevertheless only one single specific history of them—namely, that which was written in the previous century by the Jesuit Maimburg <note>Louis Maimbourg (1610–1686) was a famous French Jesuit historian; his works were popular but often criticized for their pro-Catholic bias.</note>. It is written in the manner that histories of heresies <term>Kezzergeschichten: accounts written about religious dissenters or "heretics," which in this period were often highly biased and polemical</term> usually tend to be written by scholars of the Roman Church: specifically, with vehemence and partiality, without precise critical investigation, and moreover, with very little reliability. From such writers who have dealt generally with all heretics, such as Travasa <note>Gaetano Maria Travasa (1707–1785), an Italian ecclesiastical historian.</note> in the Roman Church and C. W. Walch <note>Christian Wilhelm Franz Walch (1726–1784) was a leading German Lutheran theologian known for his massive work on the history of heresies.</note> among the Protestants, a detailed history was not to be expected. A precise and specific his-

<column-break/>

# Preface.

<sig>B 3</sig>

-tory of Arianism would therefore have been welcome to scholars at all times, and perhaps it is most opportune in our times, when there is so much talk of Arians and Socinians <note>Socinians were a sect emerging from the Reformation that, like the Arians, rejected the traditional doctrine of the Trinity, though their specific beliefs about the nature of Christ differed.</note>.

The entire first part of this volume recounts all the specific conceptions regarding the person of Christ from the beginning of Christianity until the time of Arius, and can be considered, to a certain extent, a distinct history. However, just as the second part truly differs from this first part, so too will the following history be continued in like manner; and if the author continues thus, one will be able to reproach him neither for being partial, nor for not [providing] enough- <note>The sentence is interrupted by the page break; the catchword "genug-" suggests the next page begins with "genugtuung" (satisfaction) or "genügend" (sufficiently).</note>

# Attempt
->at a<-
## History of Arianism.

---

### I.
### Introduction.

The history of the first centuries of the Christian Church knows no event that was more important, produced greater revolutions, and carried its consequences through so many centuries, than that which arose in the fourth century through Arius <note>a priest in Alexandria whose teachings on the nature of Christ sparked the controversy</note> and Alexander of Alexandria <note>the Bishop of Alexandria and primary opponent of Arius</note>. Against no single party that has risen among Christians has there been such sharp and prolonged dispute through assemblies, writings, and even through civil authority <note>original: "obrigkeitliche Gewalt," referring to the use of imperial Roman law and power to settle church disputes</note>, as against Arianism. Not one has had so many followers, raised its head so high, and contested the dominion of the Church even against the orthodox <term>Rechtgläubigen: "Right-believers," or those who adhered to the Nicene Creed which defined the Father and Son as being of the same substance</term> themselves, as this one. None has, despite the manifold pressure that—

<summary>This page introduces the Arian controversy of the fourth century, framing it as the most significant and enduring conflict in the history of the early Christian Church. The author highlights the scale of the dispute, which involved not just theological debate but also political intervention and widespread social upheaval.</summary>

<keywords>Arianism, Arius, Alexander of Alexandria, Church History, Orthodoxy, Fourth Century, religious conflict, civil authority</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the history of Arianism, picking up from the previous page's discussion of the immense pressure and challenges the movement faced.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

they experienced, through all centuries down to our own times, [Arianism] has, if not fully preserved itself, yet still found very many friends and followers who have been devoted to its concepts, sometimes in a closer and sometimes in a more distant way; and after fourteen centuries, this event remains one that deserves a great deal of attention. It concerns, indeed, that very doctrine which most distinguishes Christianity from all other religions, and which has an extraordinary influence on its most essential parts. Is it any wonder that the followers of this party defended their doctrine with much liveliness, and that their opponents resisted it with just as much liveliness and steadfastness? But, if one investigates the matter closely, it also concerns a doctrine about which Christians have never thought in perfect unison, neither before nor after the times of Arianism.

To be sure, this has always been denied, and it has been most eagerly asserted that, even if from the beginning the so-called heretics proposed now this, now another opinion on the person of Christ according to their various systems, nevertheless the orthodox <note>original: "rechtgläubige"</note> church always thought the same in regard to this doctrine, as in all other matters. For when people began to place the unity of the church in the fact that everyone agreed most precisely with one another in their concepts of religious tenets, they also believed they would be insulting the church if they were to say that its members differed from one another in their concepts of religious truths. Such disagreements were only to be found among heretics and unbelievers, but were to be forever removed from the orthodox church; and so

<meta>catchword: so</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

just as one thought in the church at one time, so one had thought from the very beginning.

This is a claim that has been generally expressed since the third century of the Christian era. At all Councils <note>assemblies of church leaders called to decide on doctrine and practice</note>, where a new doctrinal definition was invented, one appealed to older authority and pretended with great earnestness that this or that opinion, established by a majority of votes, had already been the opinion of the orthodox church from the beginning, and that the opposing opinion, which was placed under an anathema <term>Anathema: a formal curse or ban issued by the Church, excommunicating a person or denouncing a specific doctrine</term>, was an innovation. Not only the so-called orthodox or those of the "Great Church" have acted this way; all smaller societies that either separated themselves from the Great Church or were excluded by it have done no differently. Already the Nazarenes, Ebionites, and Gnostics <note>various early Christian groups whose views on the divinity or humanity of Jesus were eventually rejected by the mainstream church</note> wanted to possess the original truth, which they claimed had been falsified by others: and if one reads the history of the very latest religious parties—the Lutherans, Calvinists, Anabaptists <note>original: "Taufgesinnten"</note>, Socinians <note>a 16th-century group that rejected the Trinity</note>, and new Arians—each of them has claimed that its doctrinal opinion was the actual ancient one that had existed in the church from the beginning.

The causes for such a procedure are not hard to find. Some people actually believed that their opinion was also that of the orthodox church from the start. Others, however, used this pretense only to gain authority for their own propositions and to make those of their opponents suspicious and hateful as harmful innovations. For it is well known that the people—the common man—hostilities toward no one more than the "innovator" <note>original: "Neuerer," referring to someone who introduces new, potentially heretical ideas</note> in religious matters, and that one cannot recommend oneself to them better than by standing up for the

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<vocab>Arianism, Christianity, Heretic, Orthodox Church, Councils, Anathema, Nazarenes, Ebionites, Gnostics, Lutherans, Calvinists, Anabaptists, Socinians, Arians</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that while many Christian denominations claim their specific doctrines have existed unchanged since the beginning, history shows constant disagreement. He explains that these groups claim "antiquity" to gain authority and to label their opponents as dangerous "innovators."</summary>

<keywords>orthodoxy, heresy, church history, doctrinal development, religious authority, traditionalism vs. innovation</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the "myth of unanimity" in early Church history. The previous page argued that the Church has often falsely claimed that "orthodox" believers always agreed on every doctrine. This page transitions into a specific analysis of the Arian controversy and the necessity of looking at the centuries preceding it to understand how such a deep division occurred.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

identifies those who strive to secure the old religion against every innovation.

But if one is even slightly familiar with the history of the Church and the writings of its earliest teachers, one will soon be convinced that certainly no claim is more unfounded than this: that at all times, those who were regarded as **orthodox** were perfectly unanimous in their understanding of religious tenets. There is almost not a single doctrine in which many very important deviations among the most respected teachers of the Church are not to be found; and this also applies to the doctrine concerning the person of Jesus, which triggered the unfortunate Arian disputes. When these disputes began, those who joined the opinion of <note>Arius (c. 250–336 AD) was a priest in Alexandria whose teachings on the nature of God the Father and the Son sparked a massive theological crisis in the early Church.</note> Arius were accused of proposing a new and completely unheard-of heresy; and as such an innovation, this opinion was condemned at the <term>Synod of Nicaea<gloss>The Council of Nicaea in 325 AD, the first major meeting of Christian bishops to establish a unified statement of belief</gloss></term> and many others held afterward. Now, this in itself counts for nothing. Many very gross errors have been able to claim a very high antiquity, and many great truths were once new and only recently discovered. But one must certainly wonder how this accusation could have been made? Was it ignorance and lack of knowledge of what so many had said before Arius was even born, or was it done to make the opposing party hated and to support the other opinion? Both, I believe, can be considered here; and as easily as the former may be forgiven, the latter has happened so frequently in the Church that one no longer has any reason to be surprised by it.

<meta>catchword: One</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

One will never be able to judge the history of <term>Arianism<gloss>A theological movement which held that Jesus Christ, the Son of God, was created by the Father and was therefore not co-eternal or of the same substance as God the Father</gloss></term> correctly and with complete impartiality without going back to preceding times and examining exactly how people formerly thought regarding the doctrine that gave rise to the wretched quarrels that have most miserably confused both Church and State. There are clear and important advantages to knowing the mindset of Christians of the preceding centuries on this point; one sees clearly how it was possible that two parties, who both confessed one God and one Christ and agreed with each other in all other parts of religion, could oppress one another in such a stubborn manner. From this standpoint alone is one able to judge the reasons upon which both supported their opinions; one clearly recognizes the injustice found on both sides and what it consisted of. And if the history of all the scenes that have occurred in the Church is not merely to inform the reader of what happened, but at the same time to guide the wise Christian to avoid similar mistakes, then this can certainly happen no better than by going back to the times from which both the Nicene and the Arian doctrines can derive their origin.

### II.
### Conceptions of Christ and his Apostles regarding his person. Notions of the same according to Jewish and pagan modes of thought.

Hopefully, no proof is needed that the concepts given to humans regarding religious truths...

<meta>catchword: against</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the Arian controversy was not a sudden "innovation" but had roots in earlier theological variations. He suggests that understanding the centuries prior to Arius is essential for an impartial judgment of the conflict and for learning from the Church's historical mistakes.</summary>
<keywords>Arianism, Arius, Council of Nicaea, orthodoxy, church history, theological disputes, Christology, religious innovation</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration into the nature of divine revelation and the person of Christ. In the previous section, the author argued that "orthodoxy" was never as unified as later historians claimed. Here, he begins to lay the groundwork for understanding the Arian controversy by defining what "revelation" is meant to be: a practical guide for moral improvement rather than a puzzle for speculative intellect.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

must be given in a way that is suited to his concepts and must also be useful to him. Revelations that exceed the human capacity for understanding cannot be given by any divine disclosure; otherwise, that revelation would simultaneously have to perform a miracle to reshape the human intellect and grant it a receptivity of which it is not yet capable in this life. Discoveries without utility, which can contribute nothing to the improvement and peace of mankind, are inherently superfluous and do not align with the purpose and intentions of any revelation. All truths, therefore, that have been revealed by God to humanity are those that have a necessary influence on one's **moral improvement**, and they are presented in a manner suited to human concepts.

The same applies to what the Lord Christ revealed of himself to his disciples. These are matters suited to the human capacity for understanding and have been presented accordingly—things that are absolutely necessary for them to know because they have a necessary influence on their moral conduct. In all of this, the Christian who is concerned with **godliness** <note>original: "Gottseeligkeit" (piety or a life oriented toward God)</note> and his own peace of mind can rest completely satisfied. Whatever exceeds these boundaries—and was therefore not revealed to him—can be regarded as things that are at least currently unnecessary to know or to brood over; they belong to the realm of speculation, and we shall only receive proper enlightenment regarding them in the future.

What Jesus made known to his disciples regarding his own person is this: that he is the **Son of the Most Blessed**, the **only-begotten Son**, whom God sent into this world to fulfill His will and to make the human-
<meta>catchword: human-</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

human race blessed <note>meaning to bring salvation or eternal happiness</note>. That he did not merely wish to present himself as the **Messiah** <term>Messias<gloss>The "Anointed One" promised in Hebrew scripture to deliver the people</gloss></term> under the title "Son of God"—as some modern theologians <note>original: "Gottesgelehrte"</note> suggest—is clear from the fact that he attributes **Peter's confession** on this matter to special insights granted to him by God; for all his disciples already recognized him as the true and promised Messiah. He says of himself that he is so equal to the Father that whoever sees him also sees the Father. Even before the world was founded, he had honor and glory with the Father, and he would return there again. He existed before Abraham and was David's Lord. To be sure, he says the Father is greater than he, and thus he can do nothing of himself but only what he sees the Father doing; he also does not know which day the Father has reserved for His own power. But the Father, who loves the Son, has handed everything over to him, including the office of supreme judge; everything the Father has also belongs to him, and it is the Father's will that the Son should be honored just as the Father is honored. — These are the most prominent definitions that Jesus gave regarding his person and his higher nature, though he otherwise constantly tended to call himself the **Son of Man** <term>Menschensohn<gloss>A title Jesus often used for himself, emphasizing both his humanity and his role in divine judgment</gloss></term> in his discourses.

The concepts presented by the Evangelists and Apostles in their writings are also modeled after these ideas. They call him the **only-begotten Son** who was in the bosom of the Father, the **true God**, the **Word** <note>original: "das Wort" (the Logos)</note> of the Father that was with God in the beginning, the image of the invisible God, the radiance and imprint of His glory, and the image of His essence. They describe him as being far more exalted than all angels, as he—as the Son—possesses the kingdom, majesty, and glory at the right hand of the Fa-

The most exalted spirits are his creatures, whom he brought forth for his service, and through whom everything is also preserved and governed. He did indeed, at the appointed time, truly take on flesh and blood, showed himself visibly to humanity, made the true religion known to them, and through his death and suffering, redeemed mankind from the power of the devil, and opened the pathways to the grace of God and to eternal happiness. But for these very sufferings and humiliations, which he undertook voluntarily, he was crowned with praise and honor by the Father, exalted to the right hand of God, and died, rose again, and became alive once more, so that he might be Lord over the dead and the living. All things are subject to him, and he must reign until he has abolished all rule, and all authority and all power, and placed all his enemies beneath his feet. When this has happened, then the Son himself will also be subjected again to the Father—who subjected all things to him—giving back the kingdom into the hands of the highest God, so that He may be **all in all**. <note>The author is paraphrasing the "Great Subjection" passage from 1 Corinthians 15:24–28, a cornerstone text in Arian and Christological debates.</note>

These representations, which one encounters scattered throughout the apostolic writings, are uncommonly plain, suited to the concepts and comprehension of humans, and contain everything that is necessary for Christians to know regarding the person of the Lord. According to these, one recognizes him as a being infinitely more exalted than all creatures, who should be worshipped and honored not only according to the will and command of God, but also as the Son of God, as the visible image of the invisible God, as Creator,

Creator, Ruler, and Head, he must be honored with the deepest humility. One recognizes him as the one who has earned such infinite merit regarding the human race that everything good we possess and expect—knowledge of God, grace, reconciliation, eternal life—is granted to us only through him and for his sake; and one must love him intimately above all else, and base all one's trust and all one's hopes upon him.

But despite the **simplicity** <note>original: "Simplicität"</note> that prevails in these expressions, it could not fail that very different concepts would be connected to these same words. This lies in human nature itself and in the human faculty of representation, which, even with the greatest precision in expression, is never entirely free from variation; one would hardly find two people in the world whose souls are so similarly tuned that they would connect perfectly identical concepts to one and the same subject described to them. Furthermore, if one looks at the expressions themselves concerning the person of Christ that appear in the apostolic writings, it cannot be denied that they were not chosen with such precision and definiteness that different concepts could not be connected to them. Perhaps it was also not possible to choose others that would allow only one specific concept. But if it were possible and it did not happen, one sees clearly enough from this that it is not a matter of knowing the closer details of this person, but merely a matter of worshipping Christ with the deepest reverence, with the warmest feelings...

Christ is called "God" by his disciples. This is a designation that, at first glance, certainly distinguishes him from all created beings. But despite this, this name is not so specific that it was not also given to the angels; nor is it always a name that should be given exclusively to the Highest Being. In fact, it is sometimes actually applied to angels. For example, the Marcionites <note>The Marcionites were followers of Marcion of Sinope (c. 85–160 AD), who believed the God of the Old Testament was a separate, lesser deity from the God of the New Testament.</note> in the 2nd century called the Creator of the world "God," even though they considered him to be nothing more than a being of a middle nature (1). Even Christ himself, in the conversation he held with the Jews concerning his person in the Gospel of John, explains this expression in such a way that one sees Christ did not intend to make himself known as the Highest God, but rather as the one who was set apart by Him and equipped with divine power to fulfill His will (2).

And this same principle applies to the remaining expressions regarding the person of Christ that occur in the writings of the Apostles; indeed, it is not easy to find an opinion on this point proposed among Christians that has not been supported by passages of Scripture.

<header>### Attempt at a History</header>

beginning [when] Christianity was founded, provided no less cause for all sorts of different ideas.

The concepts that the Jews associated with the person of the Messiah—even where they did not consider him a merely worldly conqueror—diverged very widely from one another. This was true even of the disciples and constant companions of the Lord. Even if one did not find sufficient traces of this in the writings of the Evangelists, it could still be asserted with great probability: those who did not even have correct concepts of the Messianic office and the work of his mission would have had far fewer [correct concepts] of his person. It is only very late, after the resurrection of Jesus, that their understanding in this matter was corrected. Some among the Jews, specifically those of the Pharisaic party, expressed themselves regarding this person in a very sublime manner. They viewed him as a being of a higher kind than angels and men, of which proofs are still sufficiently available today in the writings of the later Jews. According to Rabbi Chanina, the Messiah is the one who created the world. The Book of Zohar <note>The *Zohar* is the foundational work of Jewish mystical thought known as Kabbalah.</note> calls him the Lord of the angels and the visible world, the Son of the highest God, and the <term>Shekhinah</term> <note>Shekhinah: A Hebrew term meaning "dwelling," referring to the divine presence of God.</note> (5). By this same writer, he is also called the most sublime Wisdom of God and is endowed with other great names (6); and not only in the Talmud, but even in the works of Rashi <note>Rashi (1040–1105) was a preeminent medieval French rabbi and author of comprehensive commentaries on the Talmud and Tanakh.</note>, he is called <term>Metatron</term>, in which name, according to their Kabbalistic explanation, the name <term>Shaddai</term> (the Almighty) and even the name Jehovah <note>original: "Jehova"</note>

But despite all these high titles, one would have to know Jewish theology and philosophy poorly if one wanted to think of their <term>Meimra</term> (the Word), their <term>Malakh Panim</term> (the Angel of the Face), or <term>Metatron</term> (the Commander) as anything other than a sublime <term>Aeon</term> <note>Aeon: In ancient mystical systems, an "Aeon" is a power or being that emanates from the ultimate God, often acting as a bridge to the physical world.</note> in the person of the Messiah. He is distinguished from the other Aeons—who are likewise outpourings of the highest deity, of the spiritual fire- and light-essence—by nothing other than his birthright according to his essence and origin. He is the prince and lord of the angels; but in view of the highest deity, he always remains a being subordinate to it. He is the Angel and Prince of the Face: but to speak correctly with the Jews, he is only the first servant and chief chamberlain of God. He is the Shekhinah; but this is nothing other than the first light-outpouring of the deity, indeed the first creature. He is called the "Word" <note>original: "Das Meimra"</note> because the highest deity speaks only through him as its first servant. He is nothing other than one of the three <term>Sephirot</term> <note>Sephirot: The ten attributes or emanations in Kabbalah through which the Infinite reveals itself.</note>, the first radiations of the deity that are the foundation of the remaining seven; these ten <term>Atziluth</term>, or outpourings, constitute the Atziluthic world and were brought forth by the highest deity, to whom they are subject, at the creation of the world (8). <meta>This page continues the author's exploration of how different Jewish sects viewed the Messiah before the rise of Christianity. The previous page discussed the Pharisaic view of the Messiah as a high spiritual being; this page contrasts that with the Sadducees' more human-centered view and begins to examine how Pagan converts brought their own mythological frameworks to the new faith.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>### Attempt at a History</header>

passed over to the Jews, and that the sublime Aeon <note>In Gnostic and early religious philosophy, an "Aeon" refers to a power or being that emanated from the supreme God.</note>, whom they took to be the Messiah, was the very same as what **Ormuzd** <note>Also known as Ahura Mazda, the supreme god of light and goodness in ancient Persian Zoroastrianism.</note> was among the Persians: namely, the most sublime among all Aeons, the master-builder of creation, the first product of God, yet a being distinct from and subordinate to Him. This was, if one may put it so, the opinion of the orthodox among the Jews.

On the other hand, there were also people among them who held far lower concepts of the person of the Messiah. It is well known that the **Sadducees** denied the existence of angels and spirits, and at most considered them, like human souls, to be forces that were separated from the divine essence for a time and later returned to it (9). Given these philosophical concepts, they could have no other idea of the person of the Messiah than that he was a mere man like other men—a prophet equipped with extraordinary gifts to fulfill God's purposes on earth. To him, high titles were attributed in a sense different from those who believed in the existence of angels. Thus, as one can see here, the theology and philosophy of the Jews, according to their various parties, provided a significant occasion for very different concepts of the person of the Lord when any of them turned to Christianity. It is rare for one to step into another religion without carrying over some concepts from the one to which they previously belonged.

(9) <note>original: "Ioseph de Bello Iudaico, Lib. II. c. XII."</note> Josephus, *The Jewish War*, Book 2, Chapter 12.

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>### of Arianism.</header>

**Not** otherwise was the situation among the heathens. The expressions: *Son of God*, *only-begotten Son*, *God*, *Word of the highest Father*, could well supplement concepts for some that indicated a higher nature, removing the Messiah from the ranks of mortal men and placing him in the ranks of the gods. One even finds in the second century, among Christian writers, such comparisons that naturally must have given occasion to form such concepts of the person of the Lord as the heathens had of their gods in their folk religion. For example, **Justin Martyr** says that what the Christians said of the Son of God—that he was the Word of the Father, that he suffered, that he rose again from death, that he was taken up into heaven—was nothing strange: for **Jupiter** also had sons. **Mercury** was his word, Jupiter’s sons had also suffered, **Aesculapius** had also risen from the dead, and **Bacchus**, **Hercules**, and the **Dioscuri** <note>The twin brothers Castor and Pollux in Greek and Roman mythology.</note> had been placed among the stars (10). I believe indeed that neither Justin nor **Tatian**, who brings up similar points in his *Address to the Greeks*, intended to teach the heathens such concepts of the person of Christ as they had of their gods and sons of gods; but these were nonetheless concepts that could not have been far off for many heathens, especially those not very well-educated, when they converted to Christianity. Others, however, recognized in him only an extraordinary wise man, particularly favored by the deity, a prophet and teacher. <term>Diogeneis</term> <note>original Greek: Διογενεῖς</note>, meaning "god-born children," was a

<summary>This page argues that early Christian views of Jesus were shaped by the backgrounds of converts: Jews brought ideas of "Aeons" or human prophets, while Pagans related the "Son of God" to their own myths of demi-gods and heroes like Mercury and Hercules.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of how pre-existing cultural and philosophical frameworks influenced early Christian views of Jesus. While the previous page examined Jewish sectarian views (Pharisees vs. Sadducees), this section explores how broader titles and pagan philosophies—specifically Gnosticism and Platonism—shaped the diverse interpretations of Christ’s nature that would eventually lead to the Arian controversy.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>### Attempt at a History</header>

...designation <note>The author is referring to the term "Son of God" or similar high titles discussed on the previous missing page.</note> that was given not only to the heroes of mythology, but also to kings, priests, poets, prophets, and generally to people who were distinguished from the common crowd by rank, talent, or other means. It was therefore natural that, where Christianity was preached among Jews and Pagans, the specific concepts and ideas regarding the person of the Lord were bound to turn out very differently.

The philosophy that was held in high regard among these very peoples contributed no less to these different ideas than did their former religious ways of thinking. Throughout the entire East, the so-called Oriental philosophy and Gnosis <note>Gnosis refers to "knowledge"—specifically a mystical or secret knowledge claimed by various early Christian sects known as Gnostics.</note> flourished at that time, while in the West, Platonism <note>The philosophy of Plato, which emphasized the existence of abstract forms and a world beyond the physical.</note> enjoyed particular prestige. Both were highly speculative; and the more a philosopher could tell of the spirit world, its nature, and its changes, the greater the wisdom attributed to him. Very early on, scholars from these schools converted to Christianity. Just as the common man does not completely rid himself of his old prejudices when changing his religion, scholars also rarely abandon their systematic opinions entirely. It is therefore understandable that, depending on the nature of the philosophical schools from which these scholars turned to Christianity, the concepts they connected with the teachings of Christianity were shaped accordingly. It is therefore generally true—as Tertullian <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 240 AD).</note> intended to say specifically regarding heresies—that the wisdom of the world was the source of all strange opinions (11).

<margin>
(11) *Tertullian*, On the Prescription of Heretics, chapter 8. <note>original: "Tertull. de Praescript. cap. 8."</note>
</margin>

->One<-

<meta>catchword: One</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>### of Arianism.</header>

One cannot say that the clear expressions regarding the person of Christ contained in the apostolic writings <note>The books of the New Testament, written by the apostles or their associates.</note> should have easily put a stop to such differing opinions. For besides the fact that these expressions are not of such a nature that they exclusively allow for only a single interpretation—and that people were not always satisfied with them, but always wanted to know more than what was said there—the art of interpretation in these early times was anything but capable of allowing one to arrive at correct concepts in such a manner. Along with the Jews and their holy books, their mystical and allegorical <note>A method of interpretation that looks for hidden spiritual meanings rather than literal ones.</note> scriptural explanation also passed over to the Christians. It was therefore no wonder that people arrived at all sorts of strange ideas, or were able to prove from the Scriptures those ideas they had invented themselves.

Even the doctrine of the Monarchy <note>A term for "Monarchianism," a movement that emphasized the absolute oneness (monarchy) of God the Father, often at the expense of the distinct personhood of the Son.</note>, to which former Pagans adhered with no less zeal than former Jews since their conversion to Christianity, has also had its influence here and given occasion to many different ideas about the person of Christ, as the following parts of these treatises will show in more detail. From these manifold occasions for very different opinions, one can already see that they could not have been lacking from the beginning, and that one must not always derive a differing view from such hateful sources and motives as is commonly done when speaking of heretics. Furthermore, the dispersal and lack of communication that existed among the Christian communities in the beginning likely left little hope for a perfect agreement in doctrine.

<header>Attempt at a History</header>

### III.

# Different Concepts of the Person of Christ among Jewish Christians.

The first differences regarding the doctrine of the <term>Person of Christ|The theological study of the nature and identity of Jesus, specifically how his divinity and humanity relate.</term> are found among the Jewish Christians. The **Nazarenes**, Christians in Palestine converted from Judaism who wanted nothing to do with blending with the Gentiles, still viewed the <term>Mosaic laws|The religious and social laws found in the Torah, attributed to Moses.</term> as binding. They also retained the old name that was initially given to the followers of Jesus among the Jews. They had added various anecdotes and additions to the Gospel of Matthew, which was addressed to them; from the remnants of these, one can discern their opinion on the Person of Christ. I do not believe that their opinion on this point constituted their primary distinguishing character from other Christians. That which actually constituted their prejudice—the binding nature of the Mosaic law—and their concepts of Christ were undoubtedly held in common with many other Christians of Jewish descent.

The judgments of the <term>Ancients|Refers to early Christian writers and historians from the first few centuries AD, often called the Church Fathers.</term> regarding the opinion of these people on the Person of Christ are very different. Some say only that they honored Christ as the Son of God <margin>(12) <note>original: "Credunt in Christum filium Dei, in quem et nos credimus."</note> Jerome says: They believe in Christ the son of God, in whom we also believe. Epistle 89 to Augustine.</margin>; but since "Son of God" and "Messiah" were the same thing according to the way of speaking common among the Jews at that time, it is not determined by this to what extent they held him to be the Son of

---

<meta>catchword: God.</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

God. Others, on the contrary, say that they held him to be a mere human <margin>(13) <note>original: "Ναζαραῖοι Χριστὸν τιμῶντες ὡς ἄνθρωπον δίκαιον"</note> Theodoret says: The Nazarenes honor Christ as a righteous man, *Haeret. fabul.* Book II. chap. 2.</margin>. But anyone who knows the way in which the Church Fathers were accustomed to present the opinions of true and alleged <term>heretics|Individuals whose religious beliefs were deemed to be in disagreement with the established doctrines of the mainstream Church.</term> knows that one is not able to trust these reports so absolutely without investigation. Epiphanius, Theodoret, Augustine, and John of Damascus, whose reports are primarily cited, not only contradict one another, but are also too recent and, as one clearly sees, were too unfamiliar with the concepts of the Nazarenes. Origen and Justin Martyr would certainly be more reliable, but they only speak generally of the opinions of some Jewish Christians regarding the Person of Christ, without actually citing the Nazarenes.

The fragments of their Gospel <note>The "Gospel of the Nazarenes," a lost text similar to the Gospel of Matthew, preserved only in quotes by later writers.</note> therefore remain the only reliable source, and from these one clearly sees that they held Christ to be a being of a higher kind, and not a mere human. Two passages from it provide important information on this. In the first, on the occasion of Christ's baptism, it says: "When the Lord ascended out of the water, the entire fountain of the Holy Spirit came down upon him and rested upon him, and said to him: 'In all the prophets, my Son, I have waited for you, that you might come and I might rest upon you: for you are my rest, my firstborn Son, who reigns in eternity'" <margin>(14) Jerome in *Commentary on Isaiah* Chapter XI. 2.</margin>. In the other passage it says: "It took me..." <note>This fragment, cited by Jerome and Origen, usually continues: "...my mother, the Holy Spirit, took me by one of my hairs and carried me to the great mountain Tabor."</note>

---

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

"...me my mother, the Holy Spirit, by one of my main hairs, and carried me onto the great mountain Tabor (15)." <note>This is a famous fragment from the lost "Gospel according to the Hebrews," cited by Origen. In Hebrew, the word for Spirit (Ruach) is feminine, leading to the depiction of the Holy Spirit as a maternal figure.</note>

If one looks only at the first passage, one might perhaps suspect that the Nazarenes actually only took into account the miraculous conception of the Messiah and called him a "Son of the Holy Spirit" for that reason. **Epiphanius** <note>A 4th-century Bishop and writer known for his "Panarion," a catalog of various Christian heresies.</note> says it is uncertain whether the Nazarenes held Christ to be a mere human naturally produced by **Joseph** and **Mary**, or whether they admitted his miraculous conception (16). This latter opinion would thus be proven from this last passage of the Gospel.

But the opinions of the Jews regarding the person of the Messiah clarify both passages of the Gospel and show clearly that the Nazarenes did not hold Christ to be a mere human; nor did they merely take into account his supernatural conception effected by the Holy Spirit. Rather, they held him to be a being of a far higher kind and nature. According to the concepts of the Jews, the Messiah is the first-born son of **Jehovah**, the first creation, the first outflowing <note>original: "Ausfluß"</note> of the Godhead; but <term>Binah|A Hebrew term meaning 'Understanding,' used in Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah) to represent one of the primary emanations of God.</term>, the Holy Spirit, is the source of all spiritual natures and also the **Meimra** <note>original: "Meimra"; derived from the Aramaic word "Memra," meaning "the Word." It was used in Jewish Targums to describe God's agency in the world.</note>, through which specifically the **Meimra**, or the first-born Son of God, has his existence (17). Since the expression **Ruach** <note>original: "Ruach"</note>, Spirit,

Spirit, Holy Spirit, is just as frequently of the feminine as the masculine gender among the Hebrews, and since **Epiphanius** also explicitly counts it among the errors of the Ossenes and Ebionites <note>Early Jewish-Christian sects that emphasized the humanity of Jesus and the observance of Jewish law.</note> that they held the Holy Spirit to be a feminine nature—which was likewise the case with other Jewish Christians (18)—one easily sees how the two passages in the Nazarene Gospel are to be understood. According to the concepts of these Jewish Christians, Christ is the Son of God, exalted above all angels; he is the first product of the Father; but he simultaneously owes his existence to the Holy Spirit, who, in view of his higher origin, can be regarded as his mother.

The concepts that thus existed among these Christians regarding the person of the Lord were derived entirely from the sources of Jewish philosophy, and were anything but those which were generally accepted among Christians in following times. The most exalted expressions—"Son of God," "First-born before all creation," "Reflection of the Father," "Image of his essence"—could, according to this concept, always be attributed to Christ. Indeed, since the <term>principle of emanation|The idea that various levels of reality or divine beings flow out from the one supreme God, like light from a sun.</term> was accepted among the Jews, one could even attribute <term>consubstantiality|original: "Homousie"; the theological concept that the Son is of the same "substance" or "essence" as the Father.</term> to him. Yet he was nonetheless nothing other than the first among the angels, distinguished from them only by being the first-born and by the fact that the others were brought forth through him, and between him

between him and the highest Godhead there still remained an infinitely large gap.

Others among the Jewish-Christians of the earliest times are known by the name of the **Ebionites** or the **Poor**. <note>The name "Ebionite" likely derives from the Hebrew word "Evyonim," meaning "the poor," a title they may have adopted to signify their humble lifestyle or their devotion to the "poor in spirit."</note> This name was perhaps initially given to the followers of the Messiah among the Jews as a mockery, just like the name **Nazarenes**, but was later retained by them. They and the **Nazarenes** were enemies, even though both claimed the obligation of the Mosaic law for Christians. I do not know if the cause of this separation is to be sought in their different concepts of the person of Christ, as claimed by many scholars. To me, at least, this is very unlikely, since in those times it was primarily a matter of holding Jesus of Nazareth for the promised Messiah, without worrying about the closer details of his person: and this truth was accepted equally by Nazarenes and Ebionites. Perhaps the primary reason for their misunderstanding lay in the Ebionites' accommodation toward Greek Jews and even Gentiles, while the Nazarenes, on the other hand, held more strictly to a genuine and unmixed Jewish race. Meanwhile, all the ancients agree that they had other concepts of Christ than the Nazarenes, and held him to be a mere human naturally produced by Joseph and Mary (19). This latter point is given by **Epiphanius** <note>A 4th-century bishop known for his polemical writings against various Christian sects.</note> at least as the origi-

**Tertullian**, **Augustine**, and **Theodoret**, who speak of the Ebionites, are partly too young, partly too distant, for one to be able to attribute perfect accuracy to their accounts. **Epiphanius** could have been better informed. He was from Palestine, where the Ebionites had their actual seat: there were likewise many of them in Cyprus, where he was bishop. But what his reports gain in credibility from this side, they lose again on account of his great gullibility, his exaggerated tendency to always present the concepts of heretics in the most hateful light, and finally, because by his own admission, he also mixed the opinions of the **Elcesaites** and **Sampsaeans** <note>A small, obscure group similar to the Elcesaites who practiced a form of solar worship or veneration.</note> into the accounts of the Ebionite heresy.

An older witness, who could have been just as well informed as **Epiphanius**, and who primarily endeavored to refute the heretics, is indisputably **Irenaeus**. He speaks of the Ebionites in various places. In the first passage, he only says in general that they did not think of the person of the Lord in the same way as **Cerinthus** and **Carpocrates** (20).

# Attempt at a History

In the other two passages, however, he explains himself more clearly regarding the opinion of the **Ebionites**; he contrasts them with the **Gnostics**, and describes them as people who denied the divine nature of Christ and the union of that nature with the human nature (21), and thus considered Christ to be only a mere human produced in a natural way by **Joseph** and **Maria**. This same point is also seen from the fragments of the Gospel of the Ebionites found in the works of **Epiphanius**, from which it also becomes clear how they believed that he first became the Christ, the Messiah, and the beloved Son of God through the divine power of the Holy Spirit imparted to him at the baptism of Christ (22).

General agreement among all the individual members of a party or sect is actually among those things that do not exist; but where it is supposed to take place, a system is necessarily already presupposed. In these early times of the Christian society, a system was not yet to be thought of, and even if it is generally said of the Ebionites that

<columns>2</columns>

held [this one] for an Aeon <note>In Gnostic thought, an Aeon is a divine emanation or eternal being that bridges the gap between the absolute God and the material world.</note> and that one for a natural human produced by **Joseph** and **Maria**, and it is not indicated by **Irenaeus** in what way the Ebionites thought differently. **Pearson** <note>John Pearson (1613–1686), an influential English theologian and scholar of the early Church.</note> believed that one must read *identically* <note>original Latin: "consimiliter"</note> instead of *not similarly* <note>original Latin: "non similiter"</note> in **Irenaeus**, and has received the approval of **Cotelier** and **Grabe**. Others have defended the common reading. Both can stand, if one only first knows in what ways the **Ebionites** thought the same as **Cerinthus**, or departed from him.

that they held Christ only for a mere human, yet there is no lack among them of those who had such concepts of his person as the **Nazarenes**, just as among the latter there was no lack of people who thought like the Ebionites. But just as their concepts of the person of the Messiah depended on the philosophy to which they were devoted, and which they carried over into Christianity, so it happened also with the Ebionites.

In nothing did the Jews at the time when Christianity arose think so differently as about the Messiah. From his office and his purpose, there were already many widely divergent opinions among them; from his person, certainly no fewer. If former Pharisees held him for an exalted Aeon; former Sadducees for a mere human filled with special divine power; then Hellenistic Jews, among whom the Pythagorean and Platonic philosophy was held in high regard and who transferred their borrowed ideas from there into Christianity, had to think differently again. In general, however, it does not seem that in the earliest times people concerned themselves so precisely with the specific and detailed ideas of the person of the Messiah, but rather satisfied themselves with recognizing Jesus of Nazareth as the promised and now appeared Messiah, through whom the knowledge of God would be spread across the earth, and grace and eternal life would be granted to mankind. True and false concepts were very much mixed with one another in these first times, and as much as we can see from the remaining records, among the Jewish-Christians, or with them

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the diversity of early Christian belief. -># Attempt at a History<-

among them, no disputes were conducted regarding the actual nature of the person of the Messiah. Perhaps their minds were too occupied with the question of the obligation to the **Mosaic Law**, which was primarily a matter close to the Jewish heart; or perhaps it was believed that the most important task was first to convince the Jews of the divinity of Jesus' mission and that he was the true Messiah, and that one should not burden minds with questions about the closer nature of this person until that primary goal had been achieved.

The ***Shepherd of Hermas*** <note>An early Christian literary work from the 2nd century, highly popular in the early Church and sometimes considered scriptural.</note> is the only writing from the first century from which one can, to some extent, judge the opinions of those Jewish Christians who belonged neither to the **Nazarenes** nor to the **Ebionites**. To be sure, it does not contain much that is definite regarding the person of Christ, but still enough to become acquainted with the author's conceptions. There, the Father alone is the one all-encompassing and immeasurable God. Certainly, the **Son of God** is spoken of enough; he is older than all creation and was present in the counsel of the Father when the creatures were to be brought forth, and he is the only door through which access to the Father is possible. But although he is equipped with power and majesty, according to the concepts of **Hermas**, he is nonetheless lesser than the Father and is His servant. He is the most distinguished among the six angels and the one who is dearest to the Father. At times, the Son and the **Holy Spirit** are also blended together by him, as where he says: the Son is the Holy Spirit, probably from a blending of Jewish—

<meta>catchword: jüdisch= (Jewish-)</meta>

---

<page-num>35</page-num>
-># of Arianism.<-

—Jewish-Kabbalistic concepts of the <term>Memra<gloss>Aramaic for "Word," often used in Jewish paraphrases of Scripture to denote God's manifestation.</gloss></term> and <term>Chochma<gloss>Hebrew for "Wisdom," personified in the Old Testament and later Jewish thought.</gloss></term>, which was also imparted to all rational creatures, of which traces occur in **Justin** <note>Referring to Justin Martyr, a 2nd-century Christian apologist who used the concept of the "Logos" to explain Christ.</note>. At times, it also seems that the Holy Spirit is regarded as a power of the Son of God (*). But the concepts of the author are far too confused, and he says too little about this, for one not to discover instead that Jewish philosophy was the foundation of his faith in Christ.

### IV.
### Various concepts of the person of Christ among Gentile Christians.

However divided the thoughts regarding the person of Christ were among the Jewish Christians, they were just as naturally divided among those who had turned from paganism to the religion of Jesus. We do not, it is true, have such striking examples of this as with the former, where two significant parties are named by the Church Fathers who differed in their concepts of the person of Christ. But it can nonetheless be accepted as an established truth, since those who transitioned from paganism to Christianity were even more diverse from one another in their principles and doctrines than the Jews.

The pagans, as is well known, had in their fabulous theology entire genealogies of sons of gods, who

...who were begotten in the same natural way as humans. I admit, these concepts were very crude, and only the simple common folk could give them their approval. But when the doctrine of Christ, as the Son of God, was made known to the heathens <note>German: *Heiden*; referring to the non-Jewish practitioners of polytheistic Greco-Roman religion.</note>, should there not have been many new converts who connected this doctrine with the very concepts to which they were already accustomed in their former religion? It required—at least for people of this kind who were just emerging from the darkness of paganism—too much abstraction to accept the concepts of the generation <note>The theological act of "begetting" the Son.</note> and person of the Son of God solely in the way Christianity associated with them, and conversely to completely abandon those to which one was accustomed in paganism. One was satisfied if these new pupils abandoned polytheism and regarded Christ as the only-begotten Son of God. Even Christian teachers in these early times do not seem to have demanded much more. The unknown God (23), to whom the Athenians had erected an altar, was certainly very different from the one and highest Author of all things; nonetheless, Paul makes use of it and says that he is making known to them precisely the one whom they had hitherto ignorantly worshipped. When Aratus <note>A Greek poet (c. 315–240 BC) whose work Paul quoted in the Book of Acts.</note> says that we descend from God (24); the concepts of the heathens were very different from those according to which this could be said by Christians. Nevertheless, Paul cites this passage in his speech to the Athenians for his purpose.

This is not yet enough; rather, Justin Martyr and Tatian in their apologies <note>Defensive writings intended to explain and justify Christianity to the Roman authorities and the public.</note>—which were intended not only to defend the Christians against the accusations made against them by heathens, but also to convince the unbelievers of the truth of the Christian religion—these two teachers present such ideas of the person of Christ that the heathens could not help but think that he was the Son of God in the same sense in which this was said of many of their gods. "That we claim of the first product of God," says Justin, "his reason and word <note>German: *Verstande und Worte*; translating the Greek concept of the *Logos*.</note>, which was produced from him without intermixture, namely of Jesus Christ our master, that he was crucified, died, rose again, and ascended into heaven—in this we say nothing that is new, or that would not also be said among you of the sons of Jupiter." You know which sons of gods your most esteemed writers speak of. Mercury is called the Word, the interpreter, and universal teacher. Aesculapius <note>The Greek god of medicine.</note> was a physician, killed by lightning, and taken up into heaven, etc. (25). It is true, Justin immediately thereafter tears down the pagan gods and sons of gods as they deserve. But what concepts could a former heathen have of the person of the Lord when this same writer adds immediately after: "the Son of God, who is called Jesus, even though he was a human in the ordinary way, is worthy because of his wisdom,"

<meta>catchword: had [gehabt].</meta>

...worthy to be called the Son of God. — But when we say of him that he, as the Word of God, was begotten of God in a manner departing from the usual way, we have this in common with you, who say the same of Mercury <note>original: "Mercurius"</note>, whom you call the Word and the Messenger of God. He thereafter cites various examples from pagan mythology to show that everything the Christians say about the birth of the Lord from a virgin, about his death, resurrection, and miracles, the pagans had also said about their sons of gods (26).

Tatian otherwise speaks very philosophically of God and the Son of God, and even desires that one should not make use of the expression "to be begotten" <note>German: *gezeuget werden*</note> regarding him, because physical concepts could easily be associated with it (27). But notwithstanding this, he nevertheless defends the Christian doctrine of the Incarnation <note>German: *Menschwerdung*; literally "becoming human."</note> of Christ by an instance borrowed from pagan mythology; he says that the pagans should simply compare their fables of Minerva—who for Hector's sake took on the form of Deiphobus—and of Apollo—who for Admetus' sake tended the oxen—and other such fables with the accounts of the Christians; then they would certainly find that they had no reason to mock the Christians for their teaching (28). I do not mean to assert by this, however, that Justin and Tatian themselves held such concepts regarding the person of Christ.

had. According to both of their philosophical systems, they could not have held such conceptions. But I only ask whether it is not more than probable that many Christians, having just emerged from the darkness of paganism, carried their pagan concepts of the sons of gods over into Christianity, since even Christian teachers explained the doctrine of the Son of God in such a manner?

It is true that one finds entirely different conceptions in other writers of these times. Athenagoras expressly distinguishes the Christian doctrine of the Son of God from pagan conceptions. One cannot, he says, consider it ridiculous that I attribute a son to God; for we do not think of God the Father and the Son like the poets in their fables, who depict their gods as no better than men (29). But just as these better and more rational conceptions existed among Christians, so too could those other ones—especially among people who did not think philosophically enough, were not sufficiently educated, had only just departed from paganism, and were filled with the conceptions common there.

It is well known that in early times, Christianity was very frequently regarded among Greeks and Romans as a new kind of philosophy. Not only do Melito and other apologists <note>German: *Schutzredner*; literally "protection-speakers," referring to the early Christian authors who wrote "Apologies" to defend the faith to Roman authorities.</note> present Christianity in this way (30), but the Corinthian Christians in Paul's lifetime already had this same...

<meta>This page continues the author’s investigation into how early Christians and pagan observers reconciled the person of Jesus Christ with existing philosophical categories. The previous page discussed how early apologists like Justin and Tatian used pagan mythological parallels—such as Mercury or Minerva—to explain the Incarnation to a Greco-Roman audience.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: as [wie]</meta>

conception they had of the philosophical schools among the Greeks. From this, the thought followed quite naturally, so to speak, that under Christ—the founder of this new religion or philosophy—one imagined nothing other than a mere sage, albeit one endowed with extraordinary talents. This was how pagan philosophers judged Christ when they placed him alongside Hermes, Orpheus, Pythagoras, Plato, and other great men of antiquity (31). But even Christian philosophers made such comparisons. The lofty titles <note>German: *Prädikate*; referring to attributes like "King of Kings" or "Lord."</note> attributed to Christ in the apostolic writings were not so generally opposed to this concept that it had to disappear entirely on their account. They explained these titles and sought, as well as they could, to reconcile them with this concept. Just as "Son of God" was, for many in the earliest times, a designation actually applied to his human nature, so too was the <term>Logos</term> <note>original Greek: "λογος"; meaning "the Word" or "Reason."</note>—according to the ideas of these people—nothing other than the divine spirit, the wisdom and power of God, which had been bestowed upon the man Christ in a preeminent degree. Through this, he became the sage, so highly exalted above other men in knowledge and action, that he was.

One would, of course, act very wrongly if one were to follow Toland, Sandius, and the Socinians <note>Followers of a 16th-century theological movement that denied the full divinity of Christ, viewing him as a divinely inspired man.</note> in taking this conception of Christ—as a mere human, teacher, prophet, and sage—to be the first, oldest, and authentic view of the person of the Lord that existed among Christians. Nothing can be so easily refuted as this. But one would also,

<margin>(31) Brucker, *Critical History of Philosophy*, Vol. II, p. 373, etc. <note>original Latin: "Brucker Hist. Crit. Philof. Tom. II. p. 373. f. f."</note></margin>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>of Arianismus.</header>
<sig>C 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: human [mens - likely "menschheit"]</meta>

as it seems to me, act just as wrongly if one were to claim that, with the exception of the Ebionites <note>A group of early Jewish Christians who emphasized Jewish law and viewed Jesus as the Messiah but only as a human prophet.</note>, this conception existed nowhere else. If there was no lack of people among the Jewish Christians who harbored this opinion, then there was certainly no lack of such people among the Gentile Christians as well. And that was, it seems to me, the concept one would most readily arrive at if one thought of Christianity as a new kind of philosophy and took into consideration all that the teachers of religion recounted concerning the birth, the life of Christ on earth, his sufferings, and everything belonging to the history of his humanity.

The concept of the Monarchy <note>German: *Monarchie*; here referring to "Monarchianism," a theological movement emphasizing the absolute unity of God the Father.</note>, to which the new converts from paganism adhered with no less zeal than the Jews, also undoubtedly contributed its part to this. Not all new Gentile Christians—especially those not devoted to Platonic philosophy—could form concepts of a Son of God generated from the essence of God. There was a fear that such a representation would infringe too closely upon the doctrine of the unity of God. It was this same concern that, in later times, led the Sabellianists <note>Followers of Sabellius, who taught that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were different "modes" or faces of one single person, rather than three distinct persons.</note> into their error. Christ was therefore regarded, even if as a quite extraordinary person exalted above all other men, nonetheless only as a natural human being by his origin, who was elevated above the mass of common mortals solely through the gifts and powers preeminently communicated to him by God.

In general, however, in the earliest times, people were zealous for nothing so much as to emphatically instill the doctrine of the true humanity of Christ. Clement

<summary>This page argues that while Christ's divinity was the orthodox view, many early Christians (both Jewish and Gentile) initially viewed him as a "supremely wise man" or a divinely gifted human to protect the concept of God's unity (Monarchianism). The author critiques modern skeptics who claim this was the *only* early view, while acknowledging it was a widespread natural reaction to Greek philosophical influence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion of the early Church's struggle to define the nature of Christ. Having previously noted how pagan philosophers viewed Christ as a mere sage, the author now examines how the early Church Fathers (Clement, Ignatius, and Polycarp) fought against Gnosticism—a movement that denied Christ’s physical humanity—and how this defense may have inadvertently led some to view Christ as "merely" human.</meta>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: but otherwise [übri-]</meta>

Clement of Rome, Ignatius, and Polycarp do this with such extraordinary zeal that they even deny any share in Christ, and the blessings acquired by him, to those who deny this truth. Therefore, assurances occur so frequently in these letters that Christ was truly <note>original Greek: *ἀληθῶς* (alēthōs)</note> born of Mary, possessed a human body, ate and drank, was crucified, suffered, and truly died (32). Those who attribute to him only a phantom body <note>This refers to Docetism, the belief that Christ's body was an illusion.</note> are regarded as unbelievers; their teaching is the shameful odor of the teaching of the Prince of this world, a blasphemy and a denial of Christ (33).

It is a very common thing in disputes to swing toward the opposite opinion. The first erring teachers who stood out in the Christian communities, and with whom one had to contend most frequently, were the Gnostics. They had crept into the oriental congregations everywhere and denied the true humanity of Christ. The teachers considered it their duty to oppose these "philosophical Christians" in the strongest possible terms. Was it any wonder if, during the lively assertion that Christ was not a Gnostic <term>Aeon</term> <note>In Gnostic thought, Aeons were divine emanations that bridged the gap between the unknowable God and the physical world.</note>, and that he did not possess a phantom body but wore a real human body, the idea arose that he was called the "Son of God" not because of his nature and origin, but because of the extraordinary powers communicated to him?

---

(32) *Ignatius, Letter to the Smyrnaeans I; to the Ephesians XVIII; to the Trallians IX. Polycarp, Letter number VII. Clement, Second Letter to the Corinthians, number X.*

(33) *Ignatius, Letter to the Smyrnaeans V, VI; to the Ephesians XVII; to the Trallians X.*

<column-break/>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

But that he was otherwise merely a human being? The history of his life on earth was well known; his genealogy was present in the holy books. This same history certainly recounted much that was extraordinary about his birth. However, the pagans told almost the same things about some great men and founders of philosophical sects among them. Thus, it was said of Plato that he was born of a virgin (34). Later writers claimed the same of Pythagoras; and even if Iamblichus rejects the accounts of Epimenides, Eudoxus, and Xenocrates regarding the divine origin of Pythagoras, he still says that there is no doubt that the soul of this philosopher, before its entry into the body, stood under the special supervision of Apollo and was preeminently connected with this deity. Because of his great virtues, through which he drew the eyes and admiration of all men upon himself, he calls him a "Son of God" <note>original Greek: *θεοῦ παῖδα* (theou paida)</note> (35). The concept of Christ as a mere—though preeminently graced—human being was not so foreign among the first Christians in general. Many of the <term>Jewish Christians</term> <note>Early followers of Jesus who maintained Jewish law and often held "low" Christological views, seeing Jesus as a prophet or the human Messiah.</note> were devoted to this view, and it would certainly be very surprising if this opinion had not also paved a way to the <term>Gentile Christians</term> <note>Converts to Christianity from pagan/non-Jewish backgrounds.</note>. Nevertheless, the little that remains to us from Clement of Rome—whom one most likely counts among the Gentile Christians—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Clement of Rome’s Christology, contrasting his simpler, scriptural language with later, more complex dogmatic definitions. It then transitions into Chapter V, which begins an examination of Gnostic views on Christ’s nature.</meta>

can be counted as Christians, a proof that even among them in the earliest times, better concepts of the person of Christ existed. To be sure, one does not find in his writing those exalted expressions regarding Christ which were used later during the more precise analysis of this doctrine, a lack which Photius <note>Photius was a 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and a scholar who reviewed many early Christian texts that are now lost.</note> complains of; rather, it appears from several passages that Clement subordinated the Son to the Father. For instance, he says: the Apostles preached of Christ, but Christ preached of God—thus everything happened in order. Furthermore: we have one God and one Christ, and one Spirit of grace, who is poured out over us. But nevertheless, he calls him the scepter of divine majesty, places him far above all angels, and in doing so refers to the Letter to the Hebrews 1:7, clearly distinguishing his divine nature from his human nature by saying that he was first a Spirit, but later took on flesh, and that one must think of him as God, the judge of the dead and the living (*). Indeed, in both of these letters by Clement, one generally encounters a language and way of thinking that shows the author adhered strictly to the Scriptures; according to them, he held neither unworthy concepts of Christ that would reduce him to a mere human, nor concepts regarding his higher nature that strayed from the truth through philosophical speculations.

### V.
### Gnostic Opinions.

With all these concepts regarding the person of Christ that existed among Jewish and Gentile Christians, the opinions of the philosophical Christians—known among church writers by the name of Gnostics—clashed, some more so and others less.

Perhaps it will displease many readers that I also include here the opinions of such people regarding the person of Christ, who are clearly known as heretics, and whose errors on this point of Christian doctrine have been so frequently contested. But orthodoxy and heterodoxy <note>"Orthodoxy" refers to "correct belief" as defined by the church, while "heterodoxy" refers to "different" or dissenting beliefs.</note> are always a peculiar matter. Many otherwise "right-believing" people have held such ideas on this subject that, if they had been examined with all strictness before an orthodox tribunal, they might hardly have stood their ground. Even if the Gnostics erred, they still laid claim to Christianity; indeed, they even wanted to be better and more accurately instructed Christians than others. Their particular opinions therefore also belong here, all the more so because it is precisely their opinions that gave occasion to others which are to be primarily investigated in this history.

We are speaking here of people who had placed "oriental teaching" <term>ἀνατολικὴν διδασκαλίαν</term> <note>original Greek: *anatolikēn didaskalian*—referring to Eastern or mystical philosophical traditions</note>, or as Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who was a prominent critic of early Christianity.</note> calls it, "ancient philosophy" <term>πάλαιαν φιλοσοφίαν</term> <note>original Greek: *palaian philosphian*</note>, into a certain connection with Christianity. These were people who had accepted Christianity, and perhaps often for better reasons than...

# Attempt at a History

...as had happened with many others (36), but they possessed too great an inclination toward the philosophy to which they had previously been devoted to sacrifice it entirely to Christianity, and were therefore intent on uniting the two. And for this, they found a certain, albeit distant, invitation within Christianity itself.

These were not pagans or Jews alone. The so-called <term>Gnosis</term> <note>From the Greek word for "knowledge," referring to a diverse movement that prioritized secret spiritual insight over blind faith.</note> could not comfortably coexist with either. In paganism, it was opposed by polytheism and the entire folk religion with its ritual worship; in Judaism, it was opposed by the doctrine of the creation of this world, since, to speak with the Gnostics, Judaism focused far too much on this coarse physicality. However, in both religious societies, <term>Gnosticism</term> had gained followers among those who wished to form purer, more sublime, and philosophical ideas about religion. When Christianity emerged, it received a considerable influx of both pagan and Jewish Gnostics. This happened very early, for even in the apostolic writings one finds refutations of some of their doctrines.

Since this philosophy, like most ancient systems, was mostly occupied with metaphysical speculations—

<column-break/>

<page-num>47</page-num>
## of Arianism.

—occupied itself, one can easily imagine that the investigations into the person of the Redeemer occupied a significant place in their philosophical religious system. While other Christians were satisfied with accepting Jesus of Nazareth in faith as the Messiah, the Redeemer of the world, and the beloved Son of the Father, without concerning themselves much with the details of what concepts they should associate with his nature and person—satisfied that he was the sole ground of their salvation and their entire hope—this was far too simple and unlearned for these philosophical Christians. They had, therefore, mixed many metaphysical speculations regarding the higher origin and nature of the Redeemer into their system. These reports and ways of thinking were indeed entirely unknown to all other Christians; but the Gnostics appealed partly to "better" gospels which they claimed to possess, partly to more intimate oral instruction from the Apostles, and finally, they even took some expressions from the other apostolic writings to help support their opinions.

If one examines the Gnostic opinions closely, or rather traces them back to their primary concepts, it cannot be denied that they were closely related to, if not identical with, Jewish ones. Among the Jews of the Pharisaic party, the <term>emanation system</term> <note>The philosophical theory that all things "flow out" from a supreme divine source in various stages or degrees of divinity.</note> had been adopted, and this same system lay at the foundation of Gnosticism. In that Jewish context, the Messiah was certainly the Son of God; but he was so only in the same way as all the sublime intelligences of the spirit world who had their origin in God, and his advantage over these consisted in being more closely related to the highest Godhead and being the Prince of the Angels. Here was—

the Messiah was likewise nothing else: certainly a spiritual offspring of the hidden Father, and His son; but only as are all other spiritual natures, and his advantage over them consists in his being more closely related to the Godhead, therefore possessing more light and perfection, and being one of the most distinguished <term>Aeons</term> <note>In Gnostic thought, Aeons are the various emanations or spiritual beings that radiate from the supreme God.</note> and lord of the spirit world. Among the Jews, the Aeon Messiah was not brought forth from the Father's essence alone in an incomprehensible way, but the Holy Spirit likewise had a share in it, and the <term>Nazarenes</term> <note>An early group of Jewish Christians who maintained Jewish Law.</note> even called the Spirit the mother of Christ <note>This likely refers to the fact that the Hebrew and Aramaic word for "spirit," *Ruach*, is feminine.</note>. Among the Gnostics, a certain spiritual marriage likewise took place, from which the sublime Aeon, the Messiah, was said to have his origin. According to Jewish concepts, the spreading of the knowledge of God, the proclamation of the divine will, the revelation of the high mysteries of God—in short, the actual prophetic office—was the object of the Messiah's mission (37). The Gnostic ideas were very little different from this. According to them, the sublime Aeon was indeed intended to redeem the souls who were imprisoned here in the bondage of evil spirits; but this was to have happened through nothing other than his making the hidden Father known to them, revealing the sublime mysteries of the intellectual world, and indicating to them the means by which the divine souls could be freed from the power of the demons and led up to God, their origin (38).
