I shall have the opportunity in the future to show that such Gnostic and <term>Kabbalistic</term> <note>Relating to the Kabbalah, the ancient Jewish tradition of mystical interpretation of the Bible.</note> ideas also forced their way into places where they were otherwise most strictly banished, and even found their followers and defenders among respected teachers of the orthodox church. The agreement between this Gnostic and Kabbalistic Christian theology will soon become evident to anyone who knows both philosophical systems accurately. The greatest difference actually only occurs where the human nature of Christ and its union with the divine is discussed. All other deviations are to be attributed primarily either to the different genius of the peoples among whom this philosophy flourished, and the different ways of dressing up and presenting their thoughts, or to the freedom that all Gnostics allowed themselves—depending on what their imagination brought with it—to also create for themselves a system of <term>Intelligences</term> <note>In this context, "Intelligences" refers to disembodied spiritual beings or divine intellects.</note>. # Attempt at a History
<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

Pythagorean and Platonic ideas. These were merely different modifications of the ancient **Eastern teachings** <note>original: *ἀνατολικῆς διδασκαλίας*</note>, just as the Gnostic and Cabalistic <note>Refers to the Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism.</note> ideas were. One finds the **unknown father** <note>original: *ἄγνωστον πατέρα*</note>—the unknown God, the hidden God—just as much among the Platonists and Pythagoreans as among the Gnostics and Cabalists. Among the latter no less than the former, the principle prevails that this highest, hidden God is the father of the entire intellectual world <note>The "intellectual world" or *mundus intelligibilis* refers to a realm of pure thought or spirit, beyond the physical senses.</note>. Where they diverge concerns almost exclusively the spiritual marriages and births within the intellectual world and what is associated with them. This is why the Gnostics claimed that Plato had not been accurately enough informed about the intellectual world, or, as they expressed it, had not fathomed the "depth of the intellectual essence" (39). The Platonic ideas of the **Word** <note>original: *λόγος* (Logos)</note> and the **Spirit** <note>original: *πνεῦμα* (Pneuma)</note> are present among the Gnostics just as much as the Cabalists' ideas of the **Meimra** <note>Aramaic for "Word," used in Jewish Targums to describe God's creative agency.</note> and the **Holy Spirit** <note>original: *Ruach hakkodesch*</note>. Jews and Gnostics claimed that the revelation of divine knowledge was the means by which souls, held here in the bonds of matter and under the rule of demons, could be led back to God and their former happy state. And the Pythagoreans and Platonists thought exactly the same; according to their concepts, divine souls had been chained to this body as penance for former transgressions, but through divine knowledge and through meditation upon the same, they would be enabled to free themselves from these bonds

<page-num>51</page-num>

bonds of servitude and soar upward to the deity (40).

Given such an agreement, it is no wonder that <term>Gnosis</term> <note>From the Greek word for "knowledge"; a mystical insight into the nature of the divine.</note> early on gained many adherents among both Jews and Pagans—specifically when the former were devoted to the principles of the Pharisaic party, and the latter to Pythagorean and Platonic philosophy. Upon a close examination of the Gnostic system, one also soon recognizes the reason why its followers, whether they were Pagans or Jews, so easily and so early agreed to the adoption of Christianity. For that which so greatly displeased the Gnostics about Paganism—namely the polytheism of the folk religion—was also rejected by Christianity; and that which Gnosticizing Jews found to criticize in their ancestral religion—namely many parts of the external service and the frequent emphasis on coarse physicality—was likewise contested by the Christians.

However great this agreement might always be, and though it was actually one and the same source from which Cabalistic, Gnostic, and Pythagorean-Platonic philosophy were derived—and the Christian concepts connected with this philosophy therefore had to be fairly similar—there still remained an important difference. This arose partly from the different clothing and modification of the philosophy itself, and partly

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>52</page-num>

...partly had its origin in the various connections made between these ideas and Christian doctrines. To this was added the fact that, in this purely speculative philosophy, the imagination of each individual was given free rein; consequently, the philosophical-Christian system of the Gnostics <note>The Gnostics were a diverse group of early Christian thinkers who emphasized secret "knowledge" (gnosis) as the path to salvation rather than just faith.</note> necessarily turned out very differently according to the varying imaginative capacity of each leader of a particular party.

Even before Cerinthus <note>Cerinthus was a prominent Gnostic teacher active in the late 1st century, often portrayed in early tradition as an opponent of the Apostle John.</note> and his following, there were "friends of Gnosis" among the newly emerged Christians who sought to bring the opinions learned there into a certain union with the teachings of Christianity, even if they did not constitute a separate party among the Christians. This is clearly seen from many passages in the apostolic writings, although one must not interpret every passage as referring to actual Gnostics, as has generally been done by commentators. Cerinthus and his disciples are the first and—as far as we reliably know—the only ones who, in the first century of the Christian era, formed a distinct philosophical-Christian system and separated themselves from other Christians by forming their own party. I can say only as much about the system of these people as is necessarily connected with my purpose. Cerinthus and his followers were, like all philosophical Christians, strict defenders of the unity of God. But they differed from other so-called Gnostics in that they were more inclined toward Judaism and sought to strike a certain union between it, their philosophy, and Christianity. This is the main character-

Cerinthus and his followers separated "Christ" from "Jesus." The latter, in their view, was a mere man who had been begotten by Joseph and Mary in a natural way and had distinguished himself before other men through virtue and godliness (41). Christ, on the other hand, was a sublime Aeon <note>In Gnostic thought, "Aeons" are divine beings or emanations that radiate from the ultimate Godhead.</note>. But it is difficult to discover at which level of the spirit world they imagined him to be. As far as one can conclude, however, the first product of the hidden Father or the primordial deity is the Only-begotten <note>original: *Μονογενής* (Monogenes)</note>. From him, the Word <note>original: *Λόγος* (Logos)</note> is said to have been produced, and from both, Christ was distinct as another Aeon, who descended upon Jesus at baptism in the form of a dove, united with him, revealed the knowledge of God to him, and imparted the power to perform miracles (42). # Attempt at a History
<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...flowed out from the hidden Father as the primal deity, so was Christ, as the **Only-Begotten** <note>original: "Μονογενὴς"</note> or Firstborn, the first and most sublime outflow from God. If Irenaeus's <note>Irenaeus (c. 130–202 AD) was a bishop and theologian who wrote extensively against Gnosticism.</note> conception is correct, then the **Word** <note>original: "Λόgos"</note> was in turn the product of the Only-Begotten. To other Gnostic systems this is certainly quite consistent; but I also doubt Irenaeus's accuracy here, as Cerinthus still took many things from Judaism into account, and in that system the **Word** <note>original: "Meimra," a term used in Aramaic translations of the Old Testament to refer to God’s creative word or presence.</note> was the first offspring or outflow of the **Primal Ein Soph** <note>original: "Ur-Haensoph," referring to the Infinite or "Without End," the Jewish mystical concept of God prior to self-manifestation.</note>. But however this may be, one can see what concepts Cerinthus formed regarding the person of Christ and what a sublime rank he assigned to him.

The task of Christ's mission, according to Cerinthus—as well as other Gnostics and many Jewish and Gentile Christians of those times—consisted in making known the hidden God, in order to redeem human souls from the wretched power of the demons through this knowledge. This brought upon him the hatred of the Jewish clergy, who captured and executed Jesus. At that moment, the **Aeon** <note>In Gnostic thought, Aeons are divine beings or emanations that make up the spiritual world.</note> Christ—who was entirely incapable of suffering—left him and returned to the blessed Fullness <note>The "Pleroma," or the totality of divine powers.</note> from which he had descended; but Jesus was raised again from the dead. Since, according to Eusebius, Cerinthus taught that after the resurrection of the dead, Jesus would unite with the Aeon Christ and return once more from the Fullness to establish a glorious kingdom in Jerusalem (43); it seems that he also accepted the Gospel narrative of the Lord's Ascension,

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

...and believed that after the resurrection of Jesus, the Aeon Christ reunited with him and took him (perhaps with the glorification of his body) up with him into the Fullness.

This is how Cerinthus and his followers thought of the person of the Lord, seeking in this way to bring their philosophical principles into some union with the truths of Christianity. If one looks past the enormous separation these philosophical Jewish-Christians made between Jesus and Christ, the Cerinthians were, among all Gnostics, the ones who accepted the most from the Gospel accounts concerning the life of the Lord as a man. Others in these early times must not have thought this way, but rather attributed only a **phantom body** <note>A "docetic" body, appearing to be flesh but actually being spiritual matter.</note> to Christ, understanding everything told by the Evangelists and Apostles regarding his birth, death, resurrection, ascension, and life on earth as having happened only in appearance. Ignatius <note>Ignatius of Antioch (died c. 108 AD), an early Christian martyr and bishop.</note> already contests this opinion of those later called **Docetists** <note>From the Greek word "dokein," meaning "to seem." These were early Christians who believed Jesus's physical body and suffering were mere illusions.</note> in various passages of his letters. "What does it help me," he says, "if someone praises me and blasphemes my Lord by denying that he bore a true body?" And in another place: "He truly suffered, as he also truly raised himself, and not, as some unbelievers say, that he suffered only in appearance. — So too, after his resurrection, he ate and drank with his disciples as a true man" (44).

<sig>D 4</sig> <meta>catchword: Might</meta>

<margin>(44) Ignatius in the *Epistle to the Smyrnaeans*, numbers II, V, and *to the Trallians*, number IX, and *to the Magnesians*, number XI.</margin>

<summary>This page explores Cerinthus's belief that the divine "Aeon Christ" departed from the human Jesus before the crucifixion, later reuniting for the resurrection. It also contrasts this view with Docetism, which claimed Jesus never had a physical body at all.</summary>
<keywords>Cerinthus, Gnosticism, Aeon Christ, Docetism, Ignatius of Antioch, Eusebius, Meimra, Pleroma, Christology</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Gnostic teacher Cerinthus and the varying interpretations of Jesus’s nature. The previous page ended by noting that the divine "Aeon" Christ was incapable of suffering and thus separated from the human Jesus before the crucifixion. This page explores why different cultural backgrounds—Jewish versus Pagan—led to these distinct theological perspectives.</meta>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

If I might venture a conjecture here, it seems to me that this opinion regarding the person of the Lord occurred primarily among those Gnostic Christians who had formerly belonged to Paganism. While they held Christ to be a higher intelligence, they compared what they heard of his human life and fate with the stories pagans were accustomed to telling in their mythological fables about the appearances of gods on earth in human form. Among former Jews of the Gnostic party, this could not happen so easily, as the actual life of Jesus on earth was too well known among them for one to have been able to take everything that the friends and disciples of Jesus related of it for a phantom or mere appearance. <note>The idea that Jesus's physical body was an illusion is known as Docetism, from the Greek word *dokein*, meaning "to seem."</note> Hence arose that strange distinction of Christ from Jesus, which is mentioned in the system of the Cerinthians.

Thus, even in the earliest times, when Christianity had only just emerged, the concepts of the person of Jesus were already quite diverse, despite the fact that everyone together regarded him as the Messiah, as their particular head, and as the ground of their entire salvation. Can one wonder that this diversity became even greater in subsequent times and was increased with new additions?

### VI.
### Various Conceptions among the Orthodox in the Second Century.

One can take it as very certain that the same manifold conceptions that occurred in the first cen-

<meta>catchword: cen-</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

tury regarding the person of the Lord also continued to persist in the second. Nazarenes and Ebionites <note>The Nazarenes and Ebionites were early groups of Jewish Christians who maintained Jewish Law and often held more "human" views of Jesus's origins.</note> were prominent sects or Christian parties at that time, at least in the Orient. Just as former Jews and Pagans, when they confessed the Christian truth, had previously taken their former ideas over into Christianity, so this certainly still happened now. Thus, the main idea also remained in the various Gnostic systems: that Jesus was an exalted **Aeon** <term>Aeon: In Gnostic philosophy, these are divine emanations or beings that bridge the gap between the unknowable God and the material world.</term> from the spirit world, who, like other spiritual natures, flowed out from the **Primal Deity**; and where differences were found here, they concerned only the rank to which they assigned this exalted Aeon, as will be shown in the following.

But in this century, various pagan scholars converted to Christianity and became teachers and defenders of it, such as Justin Martyr, Athenagoras, Theophilus, Melito, Tatian, and several others. It was therefore natural that from this side, too, many different conceptions regarding the person of Christ had to arise among the Christians. It is not merely the common man who remains true to his once-conceived prejudices; the learned are no less subject to this malady. Just as the Gnostics formed their conceptions of Christ according to the ideas they had of the spirit world, so these scholars, who now came to Christianity, formed their conceptions of Christ according to the philosophy to which they had been devoted before they had accepted the Christian religion.

<sig>D 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>This page discusses how the cultural backgrounds of early converts—Jewish, Pagan, or Philosophical—shaped their conflicting views on Jesus's divinity and humanity. It highlights the transition into the second century, where prominent scholars began interpreting Christ through the lens of Greek philosophy.</summary>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

But before I speak of this, I must first cite the conceptions found in **Ignatius** and **Polycarp**, in whose letters one finds no trace of them having taken any philosophical system into consideration. **Ignatius** asserts the divine nature of Christ in such a clear and emphatic way that one cannot doubt he held Christ to be true God. He says of him that he was human according to the flesh, but at the same time the Son of God according to his divinity; in the former respect he was made, but in the latter he was not; he was God and man at the same time, and born of Mary as well as of God. Even if he descended from David according to the flesh and was the Son of Man, he was nonetheless also the Son of God, and thus God revealed in human form (45). Regarding this union of Christ’s divine nature with the human, he places the birth and the death of the Lord among the most sublime and hidden mysteries at the very end. The passage is admittedly obscure where he speaks in a very mystical manner of three "mysteries of the outcry" <note>This refers to a famous passage in Ignatius’s Letter to the Ephesians (chapter 19) regarding the virginity of Mary, her giving birth, and the death of the Lord—three mysteries hidden from the ruler of this world.</note> that were accomplished in the silence of God. But one can still recognize that **Ignatius** formed very sublime concepts of the person of the Lord. He even goes so far in his conception that he calls the blood of Christ, using a rather harsh expression, the "blood of God," and the sufferings of the Lord the "sufferings of God" (46). **Ignatius** generally shows himself here to be a great opponent of the Gnostic opinions regarding the person of the Lord.

---

(45) Ignatius, Letter to the Smyrnaeans I. Letter to the Ephesians VII. XX.
(46) Letter to the Ephesians, No. I. To the Romans, No. VI.

<column-break/>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

Lord. He defends everything pertaining to the true humanity of Christ so emphatically against the views of the Docetists <note>The Docetists were an early sect who believed Jesus’s physical body and his suffering were mere illusions, from the Greek word *dokein*, meaning "to seem."</note> that he does not hesitate to call this party simply "unbelievers," and to give up everything for lost if Christ had only carried a human body, suffered, and died in appearance only. He distinguishes his views on the divine nature of Christ just as clearly from the views of the Gnostics. God, he says, has revealed himself through Jesus Christ his Son, who is his eternal Word, which did not proceed from "Sige" or silence <note>In Gnostic theology, "Sige" (Silence) was a divine Aeon from which the Word was said to emanate; Ignatius argues Christ’s origin is not a Gnostic emanation.</note>. He was, as he says shortly before, with the Father before time and appeared at the end. He proceeded from the one Father and also returns again to the same (47). This is how **Ignatius** explains the person of the Lord, and his views distinguish him immensely both from those of the two types of Jewish-Christians and from the views of the actual Gnostics. Nevertheless, one would be immensely mistaken if, because of these and other passages, one wanted to attribute to **Ignatius** the very same views that were later established at the Council of Nicaea <note>The Council of Nicaea in 325 AD standardized the doctrine that the Son is "of the same substance" as the Father.</note> and generally regarded as the orthodox doctrine of the church.

It is true, he calls Christ God, who was with the Father before time; he calls him the <term>Logos</term> <note>original: "λόγον"</note> — the reason, the word of the Father, who is from God and from Mary (48). But how little determined

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

These expressions must be examined precisely! On the other hand, one sees very clearly from other passages that he subordinates the Son to the Father. He is the imitator of the Father, who follows the Father, and who does nothing without the Father or of his own accord (49). Where he places the Son together with the Father, the latter is always called "God" and the former "Lord"; whereas, when the Son is thought of alone, he is called "our God." Finally, he also calls Christ the Son of God according to the will and the power of God (50).

From the single letter of **Polycarp**, there are even fewer sufficiently clear definitions to be gathered than from the smaller letters of **Ignatius**, which were not written at all for the purpose of dogmatic clarification. Nevertheless, one can see enough to know that he held Christ to be the Son of God in a more sublime sense than was the case among various Gnostic and <term>Judaizing Christians</term> <note>Judaizing Christians were early followers of Jesus who maintained that Christians must still follow Mosaic Law, such as circumcision and dietary restrictions.</note> of the time. From the prayer which **Polycarp** offered at his execution, it appears that he seems to have held the Father alone to be the most high God. However, this passage has its difficulties due to different readings, and it will likely never be determined with certainty whether the changes in the text were made by an "orthodox" or an "Arian" hand (51).

<page-num>62</page-num>

had a precedence over other <term>Aeons</term> <note>In Gnostic theology, Aeons were various levels of divine beings or emanations that filled the gap between the supreme God and the material world.</note>. Therefore, one finds the coexistence of the Son asserted most emphatically by **Irenaeus** everywhere. Everything that is said regarding the revelations of God in the Old Testament cannot be said of the Father alone, but actually applies to the Son, who revealed himself to the patriarchs. For this reason, according to the concepts of this church teacher, the apostasy of the Jews from God arises from the fact that they wanted to know God, or the Father, without the Son or the Word (55). The manner of his production, however, he regards as a matter that is beyond the capacity of all, even the most exalted creatures, and is known only to the Father and the Son himself. Enough, God is entirely intellect, entirely word, says **Irenaeus**; what he thinks, he also speaks, and what he speaks, he also thinks. His thought is Word, and his Word intellect, and the all-encompassing intellect is the Father himself (56). This passage also has its difficulties; but one still sees how far the Son of God is placed here above all created beings, and how precisely and essentially he is connected with the Father.

But notwithstanding these strong expressions for the eternal coexistence of the Son, **Irenaeus** does not derive the existence of the Son from a natural necessity, but—as much as one can gather from all the passages—solely from the will and the power of God. find more reliable and less dangerous explanations than these; namely, that we recognize through him as the only true teacher that the Father is over all (59). Therefore, everything that the Son possesses, he derives from the Father, who is the God over all. Since he is now exalted over all—as God, King, Judge, and Lord over all—he received this from the Father because he was obedient to his will even unto death on the cross (60).

Some of the ancients, as will become clearer in the following with more detail, held the concept that the Word did not originally have its own selfhood, but was the Reason dwelling within the Father, the <term>Logos endiathetos</term> <note>original: "Λόγος ἐνδιάθετος"; a Greek philosophical term used by early theologians to describe the "internal word" or reason of God before it was "spoken" or brought forth as a distinct person.</note>, which was only later generated as a distinct person when creatures were to be brought forth. One might also conclude this from some of the previously cited passages of **Irenaeus**. What he says about the manner of the production or generation of the Son—which is known only to the Father and the Son alone—is not of such a nature that it would exclude this idea (61). But one must admit that **Irenaeus** never expressed himself clearly enough on this point in his writings. While he never says that the Son only received his own personality at the moment when creatures were to be brought forth, he also does not say that the Son never existed other than with his own personality.

Just as **Irenaeus** attributes the <term>Monarchy</term> <note>In this context, 'Monarchy' refers to the Father being the sole source and supreme head of the Godhead, a common emphasis in early Christian theology to protect monotheism.</note> to the Father in the strictest sense, and sub-

Many passages occur in **Irenaeus** regarding this. In his view, the Son and Spirit are not beings who acted according to their own will in the production of things, or, as one might otherwise say, through a concurrence of will—whereby what the Father willed, the Son willed as well. They were instruments and servants of God, executors of his own will. He says, therefore: God has his hands with him at all times, namely his Word and his Wisdom, or his Son and his Spirit, through whom and in whom he created everything according to his own free will (62). In another place, he calls both "servants of God." The Father, he says, has his great and unspeakable ministry: for everything serves his own offspring and image, that is the Son and the Holy Spirit, the Word and the Wisdom, to whom in turn all angels are subject and serve (63). When, therefore, **Irenaeus** explains the passage in Ephesians 4:6, where it is said of the Father: *who is over you all and through you all and in you all*; he is the first through himself, the second through his Son, and the last through the Holy Spirit. And this is the thought found almost universally among the church teachers of older times, as has already been noted by **Valesius** (64).

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

If one carefully examines everything found in the writings of this <term>Church Father</term> <note>Likely Irenaeus of Lyons, based on the preceding pages.</note> regarding the divine persons, it is certain that he did not consider the Son to be a creature of the Father, but rather the eternal Word of the Father, who has always existed with Him, through whom the Father brought forth all things, and to whom, as well as to the Holy Spirit, everything is subject. He also essentially distinguishes the Son from all spiritual natures brought forth through him, as the latter are creatures, while the Son is the generation, emission, and utterance of the Father. However, it remains uncertain when the Son received his own existence and personhood. Yet he declares clearly that this is to be derived solely from the will and power of the Father. He asserts that the Father is properly the God over all, or the most high God, to whom the Son and the Holy Spirit are subordinate, and from whom they have also received all divine attributes.

But besides these concepts and ideas, other views appear in this century among the orthodox teachers of the Church, of which it is clear that they owe their entire existence to the philosophy to which these teachers were devoted and its union with Christianity. Among these, **Justin Martyr, Tatian, Athenagoras, Theophilus of Antioch,** and **Melito**, all of whom adhered to the Platonic school, occupy a prominent place. Not a single one among these

<margin>

Note on Eusebius, *Church History*, p. 5. 6.
</margin>

<meta>catchword: these</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>

these authors considered the Son to be a creature brought forth out of nothing, and even less a mere human being equipped with divine power. The philosophy they followed already led them to infinitely higher concepts. **Plato** had already asserted a certain kind of trinity, or as his later followers expressed it, a **first, second,** and **third god** <note>original: "πρῶτον, δεύτερον und τρίτον θεόν" (prōton, deuteron, triton theon).</note>. Among these, the second, or **Word** <note>original: "λόγος" (logos).</note>, was an eternal emanation of the hidden Father. The ideas encountered regarding this in the writings of the Neo-Platonists are indeed very obscure and differ so much in many respects that it is difficult to form perfectly correct and universally accepted ideas of their entire doctrine of the Trinity (65). It is nevertheless certain that they assumed a triad in the godhead, of which each <term>hypostasis</term> <note>original: "Hypostasis"; a Greek term for a distinct individual reality or "person" within the divine essence.</note> belongs quite inseparably to the divine being. The divine being, says Plato (according to Porphyry), extends to three hypostases. The first of these is the highest God, **the Good**. The second is the **World Creator**; the third is the **World Soul** (66). These divine, primordial hypostases are so closely connected with one another that they are different only in that each is something else, or as Plotinus says, **distinguished only by otherness** <note>original: "τῇ ἑτερότητι μόνον κεχωρίσθαι" (tē heterotēti monon kechōristhai).</note> (67). According to Plato's conception, the entire spirit world is nothing other than an emanation from God; but only two among these are, in part, of his divinity

...become partakers, of whom he specifically wished to make use as co-regents of the world and servants of his will. The first of these two is the **Providence of the Father** <note>The author is discussing the Platonic "Triad" or Trinity as interpreted by early scholars.</note>. This brings into order that which the Father has commanded; the second is the **Understanding of God**. This maintains everything that has been ordered by the first. All other spiritual natures have received only a certain portion of the substance and perfections of the Father, only as much as was appropriate for them. This is how **Chalcidius** <note>A 4th-century philosopher known for his Latin translation and commentary on Plato's *Timaeus*.</note> presents the doctrine of the **Triad** <note>Original: *Trias*; a group of three, referring here to the Platonic Trinity.</note> of Plato (*). In the following, I will have the opportunity to develop the ideas of these philosophers more precisely. However, since the most learned among the Church Fathers of this century were devoted to **Platonism** and sought to connect their Christian religious concepts with it after their conversion to Christianity, one clearly sees that their conception of the person of Christ was elevated far above that of other Christians. They did not merely consider him not to be a common man, a creature, or an exalted **Aeon** <note>A term often used in Gnostic philosophy to describe eternal beings or emanations that radiate from the divine source.</note> of the intellectual world, but also most closely connected him to the Father in essence and dignity. Nevertheless, their concepts are certainly not such that one has cause to appeal to their testimony as proof of the orthodox opinion; rather, one sees clearly enough the Platonic doctrine presented in Christian dress.

The first thing one discovers in these writers is the **emanation system**, or the doctrine of the outflow of all spiritual things from God: for

<margin>(*) Chalcidius on Plato’s Timaeus. §. 186.</margin>

besides the Son and Spirit, there are still other powers that descend from God. But among all, the Son and the Spirit are the first and most prominent. According to this system, **Athenagoras** <note>A 2nd-century Christian apologist who addressed the Roman Emperors.</note> calls the Son an "Idea," a power flowing out of the Father, the first product of the Father, who is an outflow of the Father just as a ray is from the sun. Few among the Church Fathers of the first centuries speak as precisely of the **Trinity** as Athenagoras. The Son, he says, is in the Father, and the Father in the Son in the unity and power of the Spirit (68). But if one examines his words closely, the Son is the <term>Logos</term> <note>Original: *λόγος*; meaning "Word," "Reason," or "Speech."</note>, the understanding of the Father, actually nothing other than that attribute of the Father by which He is understanding and wise (<term>internal reason</term> <note>Original: *λογος ἐνδιάθετος*; a technical term for reason as it exists within the mind before being expressed as speech.</note>). For this reason, he also says of the Son that he is not a creature who was first called from non-being into existence, because one cannot assume that God was not always wise; but this eternal wisdom of God went forth from Him and received its own personality, or <term>hypostasis</term> <note>Original: *ὑπόστασιν*; the distinct, individual existence of a person within the Trinity.</note> (69). The author notes that while these views appear orthodox, they are deeply rooted in Greek philosophy rather than purely biblical tradition.</summary>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...[Athenago]ras <note>The text completes the name Athenagoras from the previous page.</note> certainly gives the Son his existence from eternity, and according to him, there was never a time when he did not exist as the **Logos** <term>Logos: A Greek term meaning both "Word" and "Reason." Early Christians used it to describe Jesus Christ as the eternal wisdom of God.</term>: for God has been **rational** <note>original: λογικος (logikos)</note> from eternity; but as a distinct person, as the Son of the Father, he only began to exist when the things to be created were to be ordered and brought forth through him.

According to this conception, a powerful change certainly takes place within the Godhead; but this is also the conception one finds in **Justin the Martyr** <note>An early Christian apologist (c. 100–165 AD) who sought to reconcile Gospel teachings with Greek philosophy.</note>, where he calls the Son the "Mind" and the **first offspring of God** <note>original: πρωτον γεννημα του Θεου (proton gennema tou Theou)</note>. He describes the Son as a rational power which the Father, according to his free counsel and will, allowed to proceed from himself before all creatures, just as the word we speak proceeds from us, or as one light is ignited from another (70). In the beginning, before all creatures, Justin says, God generated a rational power from himself, which is called sometimes Son, sometimes Angel, sometimes God, and sometimes Lord and Word (*). Before this production occurred, the Logos was indeed in the Father; but when creation was to take place, he, as it were, **brought forth** <note>original: προβαλλειν (proballein)</note> this Logos, which had been hidden within him until then, which then communicated itself to the whole creation and formed rational creatures.

<margin>
Since all material things, like nature and earth, lay as a substrate, the thicker parts mixed with the lighter ones, he came forth to be their idea and energizing power. <note>Translated from the Greek: ὡς των ὑλικων ξυμπαντων ὁποια φυσεως και γης σχειας ὑποκειμενων δικην μεμιγμενων των παχυμερεστερων προς τα κωφοτερα ἐπ' αὐτοις, ἰδεα και ἐνεργεια εἶναι προελθων.</note>
</margin>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

<meta>The catchword "habe" connects the end of page 70 to the start of 71.</meta>

had. That is the **Word within you** <note>original: Λογος ἐν ὑμιν (Logos en hymin)</note>, Christ in us. The generation of the Son was therefore not necessary, not eternal—even though it occurred before time—but rather, according to Justin's conception, it depended on the free will of the Father whether the Logos hidden within him should receive its own personality or not. Before this happened, God was Father only in possibility, but not in reality. He became Father only as soon as he let the Logos proceed from himself as a separate person in order to create the world through him.

This is also the opinion of **Hippolytus** <note>Hippolytus of Rome (c. 170–235 AD), an important theologian whose works clarify early views on the Trinity.</note>. Nothing, he says, is as old as God. Nothing existed besides him, and he alone was "many." He was not without understanding, without wisdom, without power, without counsel. Everything was in him and he was everything. When he willed and how he willed, he begat his Word at the time determined by him, through which he made all things. However, he begat this Word to be the leader, counselor, and master-builder of those things which were made. This Word, which he had within himself and which would otherwise have been invisible to the created world, he made visible by sending out the first Word and giving birth to **Light from Light**, presenting his Mind as Lord to the creature—a Mind that had previously been visible only to him (71). Here, the Logos is certainly connected to the Father in the closest possible way; he is no "creature," as **Arius** <note>Arius (c. 256–336 AD) taught that the Son was a created being, a view later condemned as heresy.</note> later portrayed him, but rather the Lord and author of all things to be created. He is from eternity, and there can be no time...

(71) Hippolytus against Noetus chapter X. p. 13. Fabricius edition.
</margin>

<summary>This page explores the Christology of Justin Martyr and Hippolytus, explaining their view that the Logos existed eternally within God as reason but only became a distinct "Person" or "Son" at the moment of creation. It distinguishes these early views from the later Arian heresy by emphasizing that the Logos is the divine author of creation rather than a created being.</summary>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...time could be conceived when he did not exist: but this eternity is fundamentally not an eternity at all, since he does not exist as Son from eternity, but only as a property, an **inner Word** <term>Logos endiathetos: A Greek theological term for "the word within." It refers to reason or thought as it exists inside the mind before it is spoken aloud.</term>, who only then obtained personality when God willed to bring forth the world; his generation or production was not necessary, but depended on the free will of the Father.

The conceptions of **Tatian** <note>A student of Justin Martyr and an early Christian apologist (c. 120–180 AD) known for his "Address to the Greeks."</note>, while he still belonged to the Catholic Church, are perfectly identical to those of Justin Martyr. God was in the beginning, he says, but the beginning of the Word was power. For before the creation of things, the Ruler of all was alone. However, in regard to his power—everything was with him. With him was the Word, which existed in him, but by his will sprang forth from his unity. This Word is the first-born work of the Spirit. This arose through division but not through separation, and received its own effect without diminishing that from which it comes. He then compares the production of the Word to the lighting of several torches from one, and adds: so also has the Word gone forth from the power of the Father, without the Father being thereby deprived of the Word, or becoming **void of reason** <note>original: ἀλόγως (alogōs); a play on words meaning both "without reason" and "without the Word."</note> (72).

This very same conception is also found in **Theophilus**, Bishop of Antioch. God, he said,

He made use of this Word as a master-craftsman of the things to be created, and made everything through him (73). And soon after it says: before anything was yet produced, he had this one (shortly before he calls the Son the Word, the understanding, which had always been in the heart of the Father, and here contrasts his generation with the generation of the sons of the gods in the fables of the poets) who is his understanding, his wisdom, as his counselor with him. But when he wanted to make that which he had resolved upon, he generated this producible Word as the first-born of all creation, not so that he was thereby deprived of the Word, but that he generated the Word and was constantly in company with the Word. In support of this opinion, Theophilus appeals to the beginning of the Gospel of John, which he seeks to explain from these principles (74).

**Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220 AD) from Carthage who was instrumental in shaping Western theology and Latin Christian terminology.</note> also ascribes a similar origin to the Son. Initially, the Son was present in the Father as the wisdom of the Father; but when God said, "Let there be light!", he then received his own personality, went forth from the Father, and was thus born of him (75). of the expression **emanation** <term>original: *Prolatio* (προβολὴ). In early theology, this refers to the "bringing forth" or "extension" of the Son from the Father, like a ray from the sun.</term> and the same comparisons. The Son comes from the Father like the stream from the spring, like the shoot from the root, like the rays from the sun. Some passages correspond so literally with those of other Church Fathers that one might think he had them before him and merely translated them (76). In the book against **Hermogenes** <note>An artist and teacher in the late 2nd century who argued that God created the world out of pre-existing matter.</note> he says: **God has always been God, but not always Father and Judge.** He could not be Father until he had begotten the Son, and not Judge until transgression was present. But there was a time when neither transgression nor the Son existed, through which God was to become Judge and Father (77). From this he even derives a proof that the Son himself had a beginning, and that matter cannot be without a beginning, since being without a beginning belongs to God the Father alone. Yet he does not for that reason deny the Son eternity: but he did not have this eternity as the Son, but rather as the **Wisdom** <note>In this context, Wisdom is the personified "Sophia," seen as a divine attribute that eventually becomes the person of the Son.</note> that was in the Father from eternity, but still without its own personality; as wisdom, word, and intellect of the Father, he is eternal: but as the Son, he first took a beginning when the world was to be created.

The very definitions used by all the teachers of the second century cited so far—when they called the Son the **uttered Word** <gloss>Greek: Λόγον προφορικὸν</gloss>, the **internal Word** <gloss>Greek: ἐνδιάθετον</gloss>, or an **emanation and outflow of the perfect** <gloss>Greek: προβολὴν ἀπόῤῥοιαν τοῦ τελείου; Latin: derivatio et portio totius</gloss>—these are not only rejected by Athanasius, but he also asserts what Tatian and Theophilus of Antioch had expressly denied: namely, that the Father, by the birthing of the Son, would have had to be robbed of his intellect and wisdom, becoming **without reason** <gloss>Greek: ἄλογος</gloss> and **unwise** <gloss>Greek: ἄσοφος</gloss>, if the Son had been nothing other than the wisdom dwelling within the Father before his generation into personality (81).

<meta>This page continues the examination of the early Church Fathers' attempts to describe the relationship between God the Father and the Son. It specifically explores how Tertullian’s view of God as a "corporeal" or physical being influenced his language, and how this compared to the views of Origen and the Platonist-leaning Fathers like Justin Martyr and Clement of Alexandria.</meta>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

Tertullian’s idea that God is corporeal <note>In this context, "corporeal" does not mean a human body, but rather that God has a form of "substance" or "matter" rather than being pure, abstract nothingness.</note> certainly played no small part in his concept, and Origen therefore rejected the term **emanation** <note>original: προβολή (probolē). A term used to describe how the Son "protruded" or "issued" from the Father.</note>. But one finds not only the same comparisons used by Tertullian regarding the bringing forth of the Son—such as the sun and its rays, the lighting of one torch from another, a spring and the stream flowing from it—but also the same term, **emanation**, among writers of this period like Justin. These writers, as former Platonists, held the divine nature to be incorporeal <note>original: ἀσώματος (asōmatos); literally "without a body."</note>. Aside from the fact that these ideas found support in certain passages of Scripture cited at the time, it is certain that the terms **incorporeal** and **spirit** <note>original: πνευμα (pneuma)</note> did not always exclude all forms of materiality. Clement of Alexandria explains the "incorporeal" nature that Xenophanes attributes to God by saying that He does not have a body *like* a human body; and even Origen, who rejected the term **emanation** because it properly applied to physical things, says that every spirit, if the word is taken in its most literal sense, is a body (82).

Given these concepts of incorporeality and spirit, one cannot attribute Tertullian’s thought—that the Father is the whole substance of the Godhead while the Son is a portion of it—solely to his idea of God as a physical being. Rather, one can assume with the greatest prob-

-ability <meta>This completes the word "probability" from the catchword "schein-" on the previous page.</meta> that this very same conception of the Son was also held by the aforementioned Church teachers, who, as former Platonists, otherwise accepted no physicality in God. Meanwhile, in the times before Origen, there was no lack of people among the Christians—even from the orthodox party—who disapproved of the expression that Christ was an **emanation** of the Father. Indeed, Tertullian defends this expression as one that, while also used by heretics, was not for that reason heretical; rather, he claimed the heretics had actually borrowed it from the Catholics (83). This latter claim might not be entirely correct; instead, both the heretics and the orthodox likely took it from the philosophy to which they were devoted, as they did with many of their specific ideas regarding the person of the Lord.

The high origin that these Church teachers attributed to the Son of God is visible from these passages. But the precedence that Christ held over all creatures did not rest—if one penetrates their entire system of thought—on his origin from the essence of the Father and his being **of the same substance** <note>original: ὁμοούσιος (homoousios). This became the standard orthodox term after the Council of Nicaea in 325 AD.</note>, as the Council of Nicaea later established. Rather, it rested on the idea that before his own personal existence, he was the Wisdom residing in God from eternity, or the **Internal Word** <note>original: Λογος ἐνδιάθετος (Logos endiathetos). In Stoic philosophy, this refers to thought or reason held within the mind before it is spoken aloud.</note>. For according to the views of these Church teachers, all spiritual natures were nothing other than outflows of the divine essence. In this regard, the doctrine of being "of the same substance" has a similar—

It highlights that the pre-Nicene understanding of Christ's divinity often viewed him as an eternal attribute of God that eventually became a distinct person.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the early Church Fathers defined the substance of God and the Son. It transitions from discussing the materiality of the spirit to how terms like "consubstantiality" (homoousia) were applied to humans and angels before being strictly reserved for the Trinity.</meta>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>Pencil annotation at top: "Angel: Valkyries". Large 'X' marks in left margin near lines 10 and 15.</meta>

the same condition exists as with other terms which we, according to our modern concepts, understand quite differently than they were understood by the Church Fathers. Thus, Origen calls the Son "God" and explains that he is named so because he received his power from the Father, just as the angels are called "gods" because they received their power from the Son. — The same thing happens here with **consubstantiality** <note>original: Homousie; from the Greek homoousia, meaning "of the same substance." This became a central term in the later Nicene Creed.</note>. Justin places the army of the remaining obedient and God-like good angels between the Son (who proceeded from the Father) and the prophetic Spirit. Tatian connects the origin of human souls with the origin of the Word <note>original: Logos</note> and derives their immortality from the fact that they have received a "portion" <note>original: Μοῖραν (moiran)</note> of God; this is the very thing said of human souls that Tertullian had said of the Son of God. Justin says that human souls are indeed a "part" <note>original: Μέρος (meros)</note> of God, and that a certain "kinship" <note>original: συγγένεια τις (syngeneia tis)</note> exists between them and God, as they are divine and a part of the royal Mind. Tertullian gives them this same origin, with the only difference being that he considers them a secondary outflow from God—a breath of the Spirit of God. This was a fine distinction, for the invention of which Hermogenes alone perhaps provided the occasion (84). All of this was perfectly in accordance with the concepts of Platonist philosophy, in which human souls were given an equally sublime origin (85). It is therefore not at all surprising

when one reads that the angels are called **consubstantial with God** <note>original: ὁμοούσιοι τῷ Θεῷ (homoousioi tō Theō)</note> and are termed rays and reflections of the eternal light; for they are outpourings of the divine nature just as rays of light flow from the sun. In regard to this origin, the Son is therefore also called the first and highest among the **Aeons** <note>In early Christian and Gnostic thought, Aeons were various levels of spiritual beings or emanations that radiate from the divine source.</note>, just as, conversely, the Aeons or spiritual natures also bear the name **Word** or **Logos** (86). **Consubstantiality** <note>original: Homousie</note> therefore belonged, according to the concepts of these Church Fathers, to the other spiritual natures proceeding from God no less than to the Son. However, he was distinguished from them in that he was not only the first and highest of all who proceeded from the divine essence, but also, prior to his own personality, he constituted the Wisdom of God. Furthermore, all other spiritual natures, after their departure from the divine essence, constituted separate substances; whereas the departure of the Son ended within the divine essence, in such a way that the Son—even after receiving his own selfhood and personality—still remained the Wisdom of the Father, just as he had been before. Even the expression "begetting" <note>original: Zeugung</note>, by which people later sought to distinguish the production of the Son from that of other spiritual natures, was—though well enough known—not yet one that would have been sanctioned or accepted by those Christians who [founded] their Christian [concepts...]

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: two</meta>

<insert>God [is] our help</insert>

[hum]an concepts according to the philosophical school from which they originated: for by these, the expression "generation" or "birthing" was used only in reference to physical things.

It is characteristic of all pre-Nicene Church teachers <note>Those writing before the Council of Nicaea in 325 AD.</note> that they attribute deity to the Father in a preeminent sense, and therefore generally call him the one, the highest God; whereas they attribute to the Son, strictly speaking, only a derived deity—a deity of the second rank, as it were—and thus specifically call him "our God." This was based partly on passages such as John 17:3, 1 Corinthians 8:6, and others of that kind; but primarily, they took into consideration that the Father alone possessed "being-in-and-of-himself," while the Son owed his existence to the will of the Father and was therefore not <term>Autotheos</term> <note>original: Αὐτόθεος; Greek for "God of Himself," meaning a being who is God by his own nature and not derived from another.</note>. Tertullian therefore says: we believe in Jesus Christ the Lord, our God, but who is the Son of God—namely, of that God who alone is the only God, the author of all things (*).

From these ideas, which were adopted by the Church Fathers regarding the origin and essence of the Son, one can already see that they did not consider a perfect equality among the divine persons; rather, the "Subordination System" <note>The theological view that the Son and Holy Spirit are subordinate to God the Father in nature and status.</note> was the one universally accepted by them. Eusebius said in his time that those who claimed two perfectly equal divine persons would necessarily

assume two gods, and nothing was further from the cited Church Fathers than to make the Son and the Holy Spirit equal to the Father in power and divine majesty. This can be regarded as almost the universal opinion held prior to the Council of Nicaea. I will first cite a passage here from Hippolytus, who deserves attention primarily because he calls Christ "the God over all," <term>the God over all</term> <note>original: ὁ ἐπὶ πάντων θεός (ho epi panton theos)</note>, a title which is otherwise properly used only of the Father. But when one reads his entire passage, it soon becomes clear that he, like others of his time, accepts subordination. The Lord, he says, is truly the God over all, for he says that everything has been handed over to him by his Father. He, therefore, who is God over all, was born and became flesh. He is also God, highly praised forever, as John testifies of him when he says: "He who is, and who was, and who is to be, the almighty God." He thus has dominion over all things because the Father appointed him ruler of all. — If, therefore, all things are subject to Jesus Christ, except for Him who subjected them to him, then he has dominion over all, but the Father rules over him, so that in all things one God may be manifest, to whom all things are subject along with Christ, to whom the Father subjected everything except himself (87). # Attempt at a History
<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: self-</meta>

...diverges from many others among the Church Fathers, who adopt a different punctuation for the very reason that the phrase <term>the God over all, blessed</term> <note>original: ὁ ἐπὶ πάντων θεὸς εὐλογητὸς; a phrase from Romans 9:5 often debated in Trinitarian theology.</note> could not belong to the Son, but only to the Father alone. However, according to his explanation, this status—being the highest God and all-ruler—can only be said of Christ insofar as everything has been handed over to him by the Father.

Justin’s concepts of <term>Subordination</term> <note>The theological view that the Son and Holy Spirit are secondary in rank or nature to God the Father.</note> are already evident from what he says about the person of Christ himself, whom he so often presents as the servant and messenger of the Father. But they are also revealed in the fact that he does not derive the worship of the Son from the Son being God by his own essence—a reason that likely could not hold if one looks back at what Justin and other Platonists assume about the origin of the intellectual world—but rather that he derives this worship of the Son solely from the will of God. "Whoever loves God with all their heart," he says, "and with all their strength... will worship no other God," namely, other than the one, the Father, "and will, according to the will of God, because God wills it, worship the Angel who is loved by his Lord and God (*)."

This same Subordination is also maintained by Tertullian, and he therefore defends the doctrine of <term>Monarchy</term> <note>From the Greek "monarchia," meaning "sole rule." In this context, it refers to the belief that there is only one ultimate source or "principle" in the Godhead: the Father.</note> against the accusation made by some of the orthodox that they were introducing three gods. He compares the Monarchy of God with the monarchy of the Emperors, whose sons were accepted as co-regents, and says further: "A destruction of the Monarchy only truly occurs if another self-

<margin>(*) Justin, *Dialogue with Trypho*, p. 321.</margin>

<page-num>83</page-num>

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

governing and independent power, which is a rival to the first, is introduced alongside it. — But since I otherwise derive the Son from nowhere else but from the substance of the Father, who also received all his power from the Father and can do nothing without the Father's will; how can I then abolish faith in the Monarchy, which I maintain in the Son, to whom it was handed over by the Father?" He cites the same passage from 1 Corinthians 15:27-28 that is cited by Hippolytus, and draws from it the conclusion that the doctrine of the Son of God does not stand in the way of the Christians' doctrine of Monarchy at all, since the Son is always lesser than the Father; just as he owes his existence to the Father, he is also subject to the Father and is actually to be regarded as his co-regent. 

Tertullian even reproaches Praxeas <note>A late 2nd-century teacher who argued that the Father and the Son were so much the same that the Father himself suffered on the cross, a view called Patripassianism.</note> for calling the Lord Christ "the almighty God," because he would thereby be confused with the Father himself, to whom alone this title actually belongs (88). He does say that this name and others like it, which otherwise belong specifically to the Father, are also due to the Son, but he simultaneously adds the reason and respect in which they are given to him: namely, insofar as he is the Word of the almighty God and has received all power from the same (89). Tertullian demonstrates this same distinction where he says: "The one through whom [everything was made] is another [than the one who made it]..."

The cause from which everything comes, says Philo, is God; the instrument, however, through which everything was prepared, is the Word of God (90).

From this very fact, that the Son has his existence from the Father, <term>Tertullian</term>—or rather <term>Novatian</term> <note>Novatian was a 3rd-century Roman theologian; his treatise "On the Trinity" was historically often misattributed to Tertullian.</note>—also derives the <term>minority</term> <note>Meaning the lower rank or subordinate status of the Son in relation to the Father.</note> of the Son. It is necessary, he says, that the Son be lesser than the Father, for he knows that he exists in him, since he has his origin from him and is begotten by him. Furthermore: although Christ knew that he, as one who comes from God the Father, was God, he nevertheless never compared himself with God the Father or placed himself on an equal footing with him, for he always remembered that he was from the Father, and that what he is, he only has because the Father gave it to him. — Rather, he was subject and subordinate to every command and will of the Father (91).

...passages <term>Colossians 1:15</term> and <term>Hebrews 1:3</term> <note>These verses describe Christ as the "image of the invisible God" and the "radiance of God’s glory," which early theologians interpreted as evidence of his secondary status.</note> find their origin, and gave occasion to the singular thought found in <term>Apollinaris</term> <note>Apollinaris of Laodicea (c. 310–390 AD) was a bishop who proposed that Christ had a human body but a divine mind, rather than a human soul.</note> that Christ, even before he was born of Mary, was already clothed in a heavenly body; a thought that likely has its basis in nothing other than the Kabbalistic doctrine of <term>Adam Kadmon</term> <note>In Jewish mysticism, Adam Kadmon is the "Primal Man," the first spiritual manifestation of the Divine through which all other creation flows.</note>.

This term reflects the concept of the Demiurge or an artisan who executes the plans of a higher architect.</note>. <term>Theophilus</term> <note>Theophilus of Antioch (2nd century), an early Christian apologist who was among the first to use the word "Trinity" (Trias).</note> therefore says: he used this Word as a servant during creation and made everything through it (94). Through the Son, writes <term>Novatian</term> <note>A 3rd-century Roman theologian and scholar.</note>, all things were made, and nothing was made without him, as one who has always been and still is obedient to the Father. One point I must add to this concerns the distinction between the Son and the Holy Spirit as made by the Church Fathers of this century. There are certainly enough passages where they regarded the Son and the Holy Spirit as two distinct persons. However, there is no shortage of other passages by these same writers from which one might conclude that they did not distinguish between the two. For instance, **Justin Martyr** <note>Justin Martyr (c. 100–165 AD) was an early Christian apologist who sought to reconcile Christian faith with Greek philosophy.</note> says that the Holy Spirit and the power of God that came over Mary—and which also inspired the prophets—was the <term>Logos</term> <note>From the Greek for "Word" or "Reason," used in theology to describe the second person of the Trinity.</note>, the firstborn of God. **Theophilus, Bishop of Antioch**, expresses himself in the same way when he calls the *Logos* the Spirit of God, the Beginning, the Wisdom, and the Power of the Most High, who inspired the prophets. **Tertullian** says the same and to some extent explains this complication, where the Son is at one moment distinguished from the Spirit and at the next confused with him. Regarding the passage in <term>Luke 1:35</term>, he says: the angel does not call the Spirit of God "God" directly, intending thereby to indicate a portion of the Whole which has fallen to the Son. This Spirit of God and the Word are one and the same. When John says: "The Word became flesh," we recognize the Spirit under the name "Word," and again (in Luke 1) we recognize the Word under the name "Spirit." For the Spirit is the substance of the Word, and the Word is the effect

of the Spirit, and both are one (*). If one compares this with what others later said regarding the production of the Spirit through the Son, this matter may perhaps also become clear.

### VII.
### Ideas of some Non-Catholics.

<margin>X</margin>
Christianity unquestionably gained many advantages from the joining of so many scholars who turned to Christianity in the second century; however, it is also undeniable that they brought the bickering of the philosophical schools over into Christianity with them. Accustomed to sublime speculations, they indulged in them even when contemplating the mysteries of religion, and sought to make their opinions and concepts valid to others as well. In this century, the doctrine of the person of the Son of God already experienced this; it later gave occasion to such great and religion-disgracing disputes. Besides the actual <term>Gnostics</term> <note>A diverse group of early religious movements that emphasized secret "gnosis" (knowledge) as the way to salvation, often viewing the material world as evil.</note>, **Artemon** <note>Artemon was a 3rd-century teacher in Rome who taught that Jesus was a mere man, though born of a virgin, who possessed a superior degree of divine influence.</note> and various others appeared in these times, who diverged from the Catholics in their ideas on this point, separated themselves from others for that reason, and were excluded by them.

Tertullian says very correctly that the wisdom of this world gave rise to heresies, since under the pretext of [interpreting] the holy books correctly...

# Attempt at a History
<meta>catchword: compares</meta>

to explain, mixing its light with the darkness of profane philosophy. He speaks of **Marcion** <note>Marcion of Sinope (c. 85–160 AD) was a significant early Christian teacher who rejected the Old Testament and believed the God of the Law was different from the God of the Gospel.</note> and his judgment is very well-founded. However, it is also certain that this applies no less to others who can by no means be counted among the Gnostic Christians.

**Novatian** <note>Novatian (c. 200–258 AD) was a scholar and priest in Rome whose treatise *On the Trinity* sought to navigate between various early "heresies."</note> expresses himself regarding the origin of the opinions prevailing in his time—which diverged from the views of the Catholics—concerning the person of the Lord in the following manner: both those, he says, who consider Christ to be one and the same as the Father, as well as those who consider him to be merely a human being, have derived their error and perverse opinion from the fact that when they found in the Scriptures that there is only one God, they believed they could not maintain this without either considering Christ to be merely a human being or considering him to be the same as the Father. — Those who believe the latter put forward the following: if there is only one God, and Christ is also God, then Christ must be the Father, because there is only one God. If this is not so, then necessarily two gods are introduced, contrary to the words of Scripture. But those who consider Christ to be only a human being conclude the opposite: if the Father is one and the Son is another, and both—the Father and Christ—are God, then there is no longer one God but two. If, however, there is only one God, then Christ is only a man, and the Father is rightly understood to be that one God (97). Novatian compares

<page-num>91</page-num>
# of Arianism.
<meta>catchword: sent</meta>

compares these two opposing opinions with the two thieves between whom Christ the Lord was crucified. According to his conception, the doctrine of the **Monarchy** <term>Monarchianism</term> <note>From the Greek *monarchia* ("single rule"), this was a theological movement that emphasized the absolute unity of God, often struggling to define the distinct personality of the Son.</note>, which is so emphatically asserted in Scripture, actually gave occasion to these erroneous opinions about the person of Christ. That would certainly not be the first time, and it is more than certain that both the followers of **Praxeas** as well as **Artemon** <note>Artemon was a 3rd-century teacher who taught that Jesus was a "mere man" (psilanthropism), though uniquely chosen by God.</note> sought to support their opinions through the authority of the Holy Scriptures. This had to be done by every party that accepted these books as the source of all Christian knowledge.

I wish here first to speak only of the conceptions of **Praxeas** <note>Praxeas (fl. late 2nd century) taught that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were simply different "modes" of the same person; his critics mockingly called this "Patripassianism," suggesting the Father himself suffered on the cross.</note> and his followers, as much as is useful for my purpose. A detailed account belongs in a history of heresies. Praxeas, who lived at the end of this century, is generally placed at the head of those who, in their opinions of Christ, separated themselves from other Christians and formed their own party. He is generally regarded as the author of the subsequent so-called **Sabellian opinions** <note>Named after Sabellius (c. 215 AD), this view—also called Modalism—suggests that God is one person who appears in three different roles or "masks."</note>. But if one investigates the matter closely, Praxeas was probably not the first who thought this way; for **Justin** <note>Justin Martyr, already mentioned as an early apologist.</note> already contests the very same conceptions attributed to Praxeas, and it is visible from his contestation that it was not an incorrect understanding of the Holy Scriptures, but rather the concepts of the person of the Lord—accepted by the orthodox but actually derived from their philosophy—which were the source from which those of Praxeas were actually drawn. There are some, says Justin, who pretend that the [one] sent out from the Father of all things...

# Attempt at a History
<meta>catchword: no-</meta>

...pervading power, which appeared to *Moses*, *Jacob*, or *Abraham*, is called an angel when it comes to humans to make the Father's will known; but it is called glory when it appears in an extraordinary vision. Furthermore, it is called a man or a human being when it shows itself in such forms as the Father wills. It is called the Word <term>Logos: the divine Reason or Word of God</term>, however, because it makes the will of the Father known to mankind. They argue that this power does not separate itself from the Father, just as the light of the sun on earth is not a thing separate from the sun in the heavens, but rather departs along with the setting of the sun. In the same way, they say that the Father, when he wills, allows this power to spring forth from him, and when it pleases him again, he draws it back into himself. They say he also creates the angels in the same manner (98). When one compares the opinion that *Justin* presents here with the teachings of *Praxeas* <note>Praxeas was a late 2nd-century teacher who held "Monarchian" views, believing that the Father and the Son were the same person, which led to the accusation that he believed the Father suffered on the cross.</note>, it is evident that even before the latter, there were people among the Christians who held these same opinions. That which *Justin* says here at the end regarding his opponents' claims about the angels was the very thing that, in much earlier times, distinguished the Pharisees and Sadducees from one another among the Jews regarding the doctrine of angels and spirits. The former held angels and spirits to be emanations of God that maintained their own selfhood, while the latter held them only to be powers that were sent out and withdrawn again.

<page-num>93</page-num>
# of Arianism.
<meta>catchword: After</meta>

is to be sought nowhere else than in the system of the so-called orthodox church teachers themselves. This will become clear to everyone just by hearing how *Justin* presents his own opinion, which is opposed to the ideas just cited. Just as the angels, he says, are self-existing and eternally remaining beings, and cannot return through dissolution into that from which they proceeded, so it is also with that Power which the Holy Scriptures call God and Angel. It is not, like the light of the sun, a mere name, but also something distinct in number. It is begotten of the Father by his power and will, not by cutting off, as if the essence of the Father had been divided—as all other things can be divided and broken into pieces, thereby becoming something different than they were before the division—but rather as one light is kindled from another, which we then see as a distinct light, without that from which it was kindled suffering any decrease; rather, it remains the same, no matter how many are kindled from it.

Since people had once ventured so far as to explain the manner of the origin of the Son of God, and the philosophical mode of explanation had gained the most prestige, it could not fail that they would soon arrive at all sorts of strange ideas. One of the most natural was certainly to consider the Son as one person with the Father, despite how many convincing passages from the writings of the New Testament could be set against it.

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>94</page-num>

According to the views of the Church Fathers, the intellectual world <note>The "intellectual world" refers to the realm of spirits, angels, and divine reason, as opposed to the physical, material world.</note> had, fundamentally, an origin no different from that of the Son, and they did not even shrink from attributing <term>Homousie: Consubstantiality, or being of the "same substance" as God</term> to the angels and spirits. They were even called <note>original: "ἀπαυγάσματα τοῦ ἀϊδίου φωτός"</note> "radiations of the eternal light," just as the Son was called the radiance of the Father. The only difference regarding their essence and origin was that the Son was the firstborn, the "eldest of all Aeons" <note>Aeons: Divine beings or emanations that represent different aspects of God; here referring to the most ancient spiritual entities.</note>, as *Methodius* says (99), and had become a participant in the entire divine essence, whereas the other spiritual natures had received only a specific part; although even *Tertullian* says of the Son that he received only a portion of the Godhead (100). Was it any wonder that many found these ideas of the Son unworthy and were drawn to other opinions that seemed to them to have some basis in Scripture?

The Platonizing Church teachers, who made the angels as well as the Son out to be emanations of the divine essence, certainly made a very important distinction when they said that the proceeding of the Son ended within the divine essence, whereas the spiritual natures constituted separate substances. But even this distinction—which certainly distinguished the Son significantly from all other intelligences—what could it lead to first and foremost, other than not considering the Son to be a self-subsisting substance or

And what ultimately did not happen in this way could certainly not remain far removed from the comparisons of the Church teachers and their doctrine of the <term>Logos endiathetos: The "internal word" or reason existing within God before being expressed.</term> Difficulties were found everywhere, and in the end, nothing seemed to untie the complicated knot better than assuming only a single person in the Godhead, and understanding nothing other than the intellect under the "Word," and nothing other than the power of God under the "Spirit." Regarding the Holy Spirit and the Son, one sees that the orthodox Church teachers of these times did not distinguish them from one another with the necessary precision. Thus, *Theophilus* says of the Word that it was the Spirit of God which inspired the prophets, and through which He spoke as through instruments (1). Even *Justin* himself speaks very confusedly when he says that Christ, according to the will and counsel of God the Father of all things, became man through the power of the Word (2).

Furthermore, the doctrine of the <term>Monarchy: The theological emphasis on the absolute oneness of God, often used to argue against the distinct personality of the Son and Spirit.</term> was, in those times when one had to deal with pagans, a doctrine regarding which even the smallest appearance of contradiction was considered significant. *Eusebius* says, certainly not without reason, that <term>Sabellianism: The belief that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not distinct persons, but merely three different "modes" or faces of the one God.</term> actually owed its existence to the fear of dividing the single divine essence. This is also clearly seen in *Tertullian* (3). But this very same thing certainly also had other

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>96</page-num>

...others led themselves to explain it in such a way that their conception certainly comes very close to that which was later adopted by **Praxeas, Noetus, Sabellius**, and their followers. Thus, **Athenagoras** says: the Son of God is the **Word** <note>original: "Wort" / "Logos"</note> of the Father in conception and in power. For from him and through him all things were created, as the Father and the Son are one. For the Son is in the Father and the Father is in the Son in the unity and power of the Holy Spirit. Specifically, the understanding and the Word of the Father is the Son of God. He wishes to explain this even more clearly thereafter and says: the Son is the first product of the Father, not as something that came to be or was brought forth (for God, as an eternal mind, had the Word within himself, since he has been rational from eternity), but he went forth from him as the **Idea** <note>original: "Idea"</note> and power according to which the things still lying in confusion <note>Referring to the primordial chaos before creation.</note> were to be formed (4). This entire conception borders—as does the whole opinion of the <term>Logos endiathetos: The "internal word" or reason dwelling within God before being expressed as the "spoken word" (Logos prophorikos) at creation</term>—so closely on the doctrine later presented by **Praxeas**, that it is no wonder if there were people among the Christians who doubted the distinct and unique personality of the Son.

The accusations that **Tertullian** makes against **Praxeas**, when he says: *he put the Paraclete to flight and crucified the Father* <note>original: "Paracletum fugavit et patrem crucifixit." ...refuted at the same time as Artemon. From this, it is concluded that the opinion of the latter was identical to the view of Theodotus. However, even his views remain uncertain. A passage by **Novatian** <note>An early Christian theologian (c. 200–258 AD) who wrote an important treatise on the Trinity.</note> cited earlier might be the most decisive here, as he distinguishes the followers of **Praxeas**, who held Christ to be one with the Father, from those who viewed him as a mere human—if only we knew whether **Theodotus** and **Artemon** were intended to be understood among the latter. Perhaps, however, this is the aim when the unnamed author in **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), often called the "Father of Church History."</note> says of these two that they countered the pronouncements of Scripture with their syllogisms (11) and were essentially geometers—earthly men who spoke earthily and failed to recognize the one who came from heaven. This likely means nothing other than that they set aside the higher speculation regarding the deity and spiritual natures, which were already being pushed to great heights in these times by Platonists and Christians formed by Platonic philosophy. They did not consider Christ to be the Son of God or a higher intelligence who came from heaven, but only a human endowed with extraordinary advantages. Thus, it is not **Plato**, but **Aristotle**, **Euclid**, and **Theophrastus** who are cited among the favorite authors of these people.
