According to the account of **Irenaeus**, **Basilides** claimed that not **Christ**, but **Simon of Cyrene** was crucified. According to **Clement of Alexandria**, on the other hand, it appears that he admitted the true humanity, as well as the suffering and death of Christ (12).

The philosophical-Christian system of **Basilides** had, if the usual conceptions of it hold, presumably the closest relationship to the Kabbalistic <note>Referring to Jewish mystical traditions regarding the nature of the divine and the universe.</note> system among all Gnostic ones. According to him, the entire intellectual world was an outflow from the highest God; but the highest and most prominent among them were, according to **Irenaeus**, five—but according to **Clement of Alexandria**, more likely seven—<term>Aeons: Divine beings or emanations that represent various attributes of God</term>, who with the Father constituted the blessed <term>Ogdoad: A group of eight divine beings</term> <note>original: "Ogdoas"</note>. Among these seven, the **Nous** or **Mind** <note>original: "Nus"</note> and the **Logos** or the Word were the first; however, one always owed its existence to the other. # Attempt at a History
<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: how</meta>

Opponents have always been diligent in presenting him from the most absurd side. Perhaps, however, these first seven **Aeons** <note>In Gnostic thought, these are divine emanations or "ages" that represent powers or attributes proceeding from the high God.</note>, which are cited as the immediate or closest emanations, were nothing other than personified attributes of the divine nature. These would have emerged from that nature to bring forth the intellectual world through their union, without, however, being separated or detached from it (13). This was essentially the same case with the ten **Sephiroth** <note>The ten attributes or emanations in Jewish Kabbalah through which the Infinite reveals itself.</note> of the Kabbalists, which were actually nothing other than attributes of the deity. **Basilides** <note>An early Christian Gnostic teacher in Alexandria (active c. 117–138 AD).</note> personified his own, and what is said about the descent of these so-called Aeons from one another is perhaps nothing other than a misunderstanding. This happened because people attributed to the **Ogdoad** <note>The "group of eight," referring to the first level of divine emanations.</note> what actually only took place in regard to the intelligences produced by it. The system of Basilides, if viewed with completely impartial eyes, was actually not much worse than that of many orthodox church teachers. The real difference was likely only this: he personified seven attributes, whereas those teachers allowed this for only two. His first two Aeons, the **Nus** <note>original: "Nus", meaning Mind or Intellect.</note> and **Logos** <note>original: "Logos", meaning Word or Reason.</note>, were taken from the same source from which those others had taken their own Mind and their **internal Word** <note>original: "Logos endiathetos", a philosophical term for reason as it exists within the mind before being expressed.</note>. Furthermore, it is by no means provable that Basilides abandoned history to pursue mere events that took place in the spirit world, as

Therefore, baptism was actually celebrated among the **Basilidians** as the **Theophany** <note>The manifestation or "appearance of God."</note>, or the appearance of the Son of God in the flesh (14). The error of Basilides regarding the person of the Lord consisted actually in his denial of the incarnation of the sublime Aeon and its union into one person with the human nature (**hypostatic union**) <note>original: "unio hypostatica", the theological doctrine that Christ is one person with two natures, divine and human.</note>.

To this system stood that of **Carpocrates** <note>An early 2nd-century Gnostic leader in Alexandria.</note>, which, if one can believe the accounts of the Church Fathers, was in some sense its direct opposite, although from another side it was related to it. As far as one can see from the contradictory reports, Carpocrates held Christ to be a human naturally fathered by Joseph and Mary, and he therefore placed him alongside other great men of antiquity, such as **Pythagoras**, **Plato**, and **Aristotle**. Hence the Church Fathers say that among all the Gnostics, he thought most lowly of Christ. But the soul connected with the body of Christ was superior to the souls of other men, and a higher **power** <note>original: "$\delta\upsilon\nu\alpha\mu\iota\varsigma$" (dynamis).</note>, an Aeon, enabled him to overcome the spirits who created the world. It contrasts this with Carpocrates, who viewed Jesus as a naturally born man possessing superior spiritual power to overcome the creators of the material world.</summary>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: that</meta>

how he fell away from the highest God is unknown, as is the entire metaphysical theology of the **Carpocratians** <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic sect who believed that Jesus was the biological son of Joseph but possessed a soul of such purity that he could remember the divine realm and overcome the rulers of this world.</note>. **Epiphanius** <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403 AD), a prominent Church Father and "heresy hunter" who documented various early Christian sects.</note> even speaks of **powers** <note>original: δυνάμεσι (dynameis)</note> in the plural, which were sent into the soul of Christ (15).

Among all Gnostic systems of this century, that of **Valentinus** <note>One of the most influential Gnostic teachers of the mid-2nd century, who taught that the material world was created by a lower deity (the Demiurge) and that salvation comes through secret knowledge (gnosis) of the divine realm.</note> is the most detailed, but also at the same time the most complicated. But despite all the allegories with which it is interwoven, Valentinus nonetheless gives the Son a very exalted origin.

According to the usual ideas of the Church Fathers, not a single Gnostic Christian had such enormous concepts of the person of the Lord as Valentinus. For according to them, the **Nus** <note>original: "Nus" (Nous), the Greek term for Mind or Intellect</note>—who was a product of the Primal Father and Silence, and was the first among the 30 **Aeons** <note>In Gnosticism, Aeons are divine emanations that make up the "Pleroma" or divine fullness.</note> of the blessed Fullness—and Christ, and Jesus, were three distinct Aeons from one another. But it is only more than certain that these representations of the Valentinian system are little to be trusted; and despite all the complicated ideas of Aeon-generations, one clearly sees that **Logos** <note>The Word</note>, Jesus, Christ, and **Soter** <note>The Savior</note> were only different names for one and the same Aeon (16). One would have to have the books of the Valentinians themselves if one wanted to form correct concepts of their doctrine of Aeons, which was likely nothing other than hieroglyphs and allegories, just as the entire Gospel history was viewed by them as an allegory. But despite all these obscurities, one still sees,

<margin>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
<sig>G 5</sig>

that Valentinus attributed to the Son an origin exalted above all other spiritual substances. For when the Primal Father wished to reveal himself to the spiritual natures, he brought forth from himself the **Understanding** <note>Nous</note>, the pure Spirit, who is the only-begotten Son of the Father. This production actually happened by the Father contemplating his own nature and perfections, and the product, the **projection** <note>original: προβολὴ (probole), meaning an emanation or a "throwing forth" of divine substance</note> of this contemplation, was the only-begotten Son, who was the substantial image of the Father and the imprint of his essence (17). According to this representation, the Son had gone forth from the essence of the Father, his projection, the Understanding of God exalted to a special personality, and in him again was the Logos or the Word. **Beausobre** <note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a French Protestant ecclesiastical historian famous for his work on Manichaeism and early Christian heresies.</note> had the thought that the Valentinians did not consider the Logos to be a person different from the Son, but rather for his intellect and wisdom, and this opinion has much probability (18). But whatever the case may be regarding that, the Son was still not a creature, not a lowly Aeon, but a projection that had gone forth from the Godhead itself; and Valentinus therefore also called him God, and attributed to him the production of all things (19). And just as he had already made the hidden Father known to the angels in the intellectual world, he was afterwards also sent into this world to make the true knowledge of God known to souls and to free them from the service of demons. # Attempt at a History
<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: cion</meta>

Regarding the nature of Christ and his humanity, much obscurity still surrounds the system of **Valentinus** <note>A mid-2nd century Gnostic teacher in Rome</note>. This obscurity likely stems from the representation given by **Irenaeus** <note>Bishop of Lugdunum and author of "Against Heresies," the primary source for Gnostic beliefs</note> of Valentinus's opinions, in which Jesus and Christ were considered distinct **Aeons** <note>Divine beings or emanations that make up the Pleroma, the fullness of God</note>. However, it is generally accepted that Valentinus attributed to Christ a visible, yet finer and more ethereal body, which he brought with him from heaven. This latter idea is not entirely surprising: for **Apollinaris** <note>Apollinaris of Laodicea (d. 390 AD), who taught that Christ had a human body but a divine mind</note>, one of the most learned Church Fathers of this century, held the thought that Christ was already human and united with an ethereal body even before his birth from Mary. This is an opinion for which the basis is perhaps to be sought nowhere else but in the **Kabbalistic** <note>Jewish mystical tradition</note> doctrine of the **Adam Kadmon** <note>The "Original Man" or "Primordial Man," a spiritual prototype of humanity</note>. 

This was also **Bardesanes's** <note>A 2nd-century Syriac Gnostic and poet</note> conception of Christ. As far as I know, his doctrine of the divine nature of Christ was not censured by the teachers of the Church; he undoubtedly regarded Christ as nothing other than an exalted Aeon, a <term>projection</term> <note>original: προβολὴν (probolēn), an emanation or "putting forth" of a divine being</note> and outflowing from the Father. However, he denied Christ’s true humanity and birth from Mary, attributing to Christ a heavenly body, a <term>celestial body</term> <note>original: σῶμα οὐράνιον (sōma ouranion)</note>, similar to those the angels assumed when they appeared to Abraham and ate and drank with him (20). According to **Athanasius** <note>The 4th-century Bishop of Alexandria and champion of Nicene orthodoxy</note>, this was also the conception **Marcion** <note>A prominent 2nd-century teacher who rejected the Old Testament and believed the material world was evil</note> held in this century regarding the person of Christ. But **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author from Carthage, known as the "Father of Latin Theology"</note> deserves more credit here, from whom we see that Mar-

---

(20) Beausobre, *History of the Manicheans*. II. p. 137. <note>original: "Hist. des Manichéens"</note>

<column-break/>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

-cion attributed only an **apparent body** <note>original: Scheinkörper; a body that looks real but has no physical substance</note> to Christ, and regarded everything said of Christ’s life on earth—his birth, suffering, and death—as merely an illusion (21). Meanwhile, **Apelles** <note>A disciple of Marcion who modified his master's stricter dualism</note>, one of Marcion’s most prominent followers, later deviated from him on this point. Instead, following the lead of Valentinus, Bardesanes, and others, he assumed that Christ did not have an apparent body, but a real body made of heavenly matter, which he then cast off again during his ascension (22). 

In passing, I note here that **Pantaenus** <note>An early Christian theologian and head of the Catechetical School of Alexandria around 180 AD</note>, an orthodox teacher of the church, held this very same thought: that Christ left his body in the sun (23). In general, the so-called **Docetists** <note>From the Greek word "dokein" (to seem); Christians who believed Christ's human form was an illusion</note> were divided into two parties regarding Christ's human nature: some attributed to Christ a mere apparent body, while others attributed to him a heavenly body. But whichever opinion prevailed, the primary reason for it (aside from negative views on physical matter) was nothing other than the fact that people held concepts of the nature united with the Lord's humanity that were too exalted to allow for the belief that God, or such an exalted Aeon, could be born, suffer, and die. If only the Gnostics had not declared themselves so strongly against the true humanity of the Lord and, where they spoke of his higher origin,

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

had they not lost themselves too far in the realm of metaphysical speculations, which originated from Eastern—and primarily, as with the **Ophites** <note>A Gnostic sect known for their reverence of the serpent as a symbol of hidden knowledge</note>, from **Kabbalistic** <note>Jewish mystical tradition</note> philosophy—they might perhaps never have been considered heretics by other Christians because of this doctrine. For fundamentally, their teaching of Christ—as the eternal **Logos** <note>The "Word" or divine reason</note>, as the only-begotten Son of the hidden Father, and as a <term>probole</term> <note>original: "Probole," a Greek term for an emission or "projection" of divine substance</note> or emanation of the divine essence—bore a very close resemblance to the concepts accepted among the orthodox church teachers, especially those who adhered to **Platonism**. And it is certain that these Gnostic Christians were exceptionally numerous.

### VIII.

### Various Conceptions in the Third Century.

The same various conceptions regarding the person of the Lord that had existed among Christians in the second century continued into the third; they were, in a sense, even increased as philosophy gained more followers among the Christians. This was, namely, the century in which several great men appeared among the Christians who were also famous among the pagans for their learning, such as **Clement of Alexandria**, **Origen**, **Gregory the Miracle-Worker**, **Dionysius of Alexandria**, **Methodius**, **Lucian**, **Lactantius**, and others. These men certainly performed immensely important services for Christianity, but

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<page-break/>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

but they also harbored their own peculiar opinions, both in other doctrines and in the one concerning the person of Christ.

It can certainly be said that the previously mentioned concepts always remained the foundation; however, where such a fertile field for speculation was opened to the human spirit as in the philosophy to which the scholars of these times were primarily devoted, it is easily expected that many strange side-conceptions would not be lacking.

The doctrine of the **subordination of the Son** <note>The belief that the Son is rank-ordered below God the Father, rather than being co-equal</note> was, in this century as in the previous one, still the generally accepted doctrine in the church. Thus, while Clement of Alexandria calls the Son the "Lord of all things," he simultaneously says of him that he carries out the will of the All-Ruler as his "servant" (24). Origen judges similarly when he remarks on the passage in John 1:3, saying that the expression "through" <note>Greek: *dia*</note> is used for the second cause, not the first. "All things were made *through* the Word; not *by* the Word, for the word 'by' <note>Greek: *hypo*</note> is said of him who is greater and higher than the Word. And who else is this but the Father?" (25) In general, Origen regards it as a rash expression if one says of the Son of God that he is "God over all," thereby placing him on equal footing with the Father. It may be, he says, that some

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: this</meta>

...some of us—since among the multitude of believers a diversity of opinions is certainly to be expected—are so hasty as to say that the Redeemer is God over all. We, however, do not hold this opinion, but rather believe the word he himself spoke to us: *The Father, who sent me, is greater than I* (26). As one can see from this, there were Christians who held the thought that the Son was perfectly equal to the Father in power and dignity, and was the God over all; however, the general conception, as **Origen** <note>A hugely influential early Christian scholar from Alexandria (c. 185–253 AD) whose complex views on the nature of Christ later became controversial</note> says, was that the Son was lesser than the Father and subject to him. This is also evident from the letter of the **Synod of Antioch** <note>A council of bishops held in 268 AD to address the teachings of Paul of Samosata</note> to **Paul of Samosata** <note>The Bishop of Antioch who taught that Jesus was a man who became divine, rather than the eternal Word</note>. The Son, it says there, is sometimes called Angel, sometimes Lord, and sometimes also God. But it would be impious to believe that the "God over all" could be called an "Angel"; rather, the Son is the Angel of the Father, who is himself also Lord and God: for it is written: *the Angel of Great Counsel* (27).

In the same way, the Church Fathers are also unanimous in this: that everything the Son possesses was only bestowed upon him by the Father and came to him from the same. All power of the Lord, says **Clement of Alexandria** <note>A prominent Christian teacher (c. 150–215 AD) who sought to unite Greek philosophy with Christian faith</note>, therefore properly belongs to the **Almighty** <term>Allbeherrscher</term> <note>original: "Allbeherrscher," translating the Greek *Pantokrator*, or "Ruler of All"</note>, and he is, so to speak, the paternal power. # Attempt at a History
<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

Taken as a whole, the Church Fathers of this period still held the same conceptions that were prevalent in the previous century, in which **Platonism** <note>A school of philosophy based on the teachings of Plato, which heavily influenced early Christian thought regarding the spiritual and material worlds</note> had played such an extraordinary part. Even **Clement of Alexandria** and others refer to it in various places. Nevertheless, the deviations and specific ideas are very visible and striking here and there. **Origen** <note>One of the most prolific and influential early Christian theologians, known for his allegorical interpretation of Scripture</note> admittedly rejected the expression <term>projection <note>original Greek: *προβολὴ* (probole), a term used to describe how divine beings "flowed out" from the source of divinity</note></term> regarding Christ, as it was a term used by the heretics (**Gnostics**, especially the **Valentinians** <note>Followers of Valentinus, a major Gnostic teacher who taught that the divine world consisted of many "emanations"</note>); but nonetheless, he accepted the doctrine of **emanation**, and in describing the proceeding of the Son from the Father, he made use of the same comparison of the sun and the rays flowing from it, which **Justin Martyr** and other Church Fathers had used. But it is remarkable that he assumes this generation of the Son to be from eternity, and does not have it occur only when the world was to be created through the Son, as **Justin**, **Hippolytus**, **Tertullian**, and other Church teachers had assumed; rather, according to him, there was never a time when God had not begotten his Son. These words: *You are my Son, today I have begotten you*, Origen says, are spoken by God, with whom it is always "today." For just as there has never been a morning with God, so too will there be no evening with him: rather, the time that extends equally with his life without beginning and without end is for him the "day" or the "today" on which the Son was begotten,

</margin>

To be sure, those who held the Son to be the <term>immanent Word <note>original Greek: *Λογος ἐνδιάθετος* (Logos endiathetos), the idea of the Word existing as an internal thought of God before being expressed</note></term> also maintained his eternity in a certain sense, though not his eternal generation, which they allowed to proceed shortly before the creation of the world. But this doctrine of an *immanent Word* endowed with its own personality only at the creation of the world was not Origen's conception; rather, the Son had always existed as a Son in his own personality with the Father. The system of emanation he adopted certainly gave rise to this conception: just as the sun, he says, is never without rays, so too God has never been without the Son; he has always been begotten by God and has been the radiance of his glory. This brilliance is not born once and then ceases to be born, but rather it has always been generated, and still is (32). But it is also certain that his opinion on the eternal existence of the spiritual natures produced through the Son played no less a part in this. # Attempt at a History
<page-num>114</page-num>

<meta>catchword: God's</meta>

...been, but that even before it, infinitely many worlds had been present, as it would be absurd to think that God had been in a state of inactivity before the creation of the world (33). His concepts regarding the origin and essence of spiritual natures were added to this, and since everything, both the visible and the invisible, was created through the Son, one can easily see that Origen could assume nothing other than an eternal generation of the Son.

But notwithstanding this eternal generation of the Son, which was asserted by Origen and is regarded by Bull <note>George Bull (1634–1710), an English bishop who defended the orthodoxy of the pre-Nicene Fathers</note> as an opinion in which Origen agrees with the orthodox church (34), the concepts of this church teacher regarding the Son still remain very slight, and very different from the later Nicene doctrine <note>The set of beliefs established at the Council of Nicaea in 325 AD, which defined the Son as being of the same substance as the Father</note>. This is because Origen, as Huetius <note>Pierre Daniel Huet (1630–1721), a French scholar who wrote extensively on Origen</note> has already noted, also attributed eternity to creatures, and just as he describes their production, he also describes the production of the Son as an act of the Father's free will. He asserts the eternal generation of the Son; but he asserts nothing less than that the Son is co-eternal <note>original Greek: *συναΐδιος* (synaidios)</note> with the Father; just as he asserts the eternal proceeding of the Holy Spirit, and yet says that the Son is older than the Holy Spirit (35). In another passage, he explicitly calls the Son a creature. This Son, he says, who was brought forth according to the will of the Father, the image of the invisible

Jerome, and Epiphanius, and others after the Council of Nicaea have therefore regarded Origen as a predecessor of Arius <note>The priest whose teachings led to the Arian controversy, asserting that the Son was a created being</note>, whereas Ruffinus and others sought to defend him. Both sides were certainly in the wrong, and it is equally wrong when Huetius places the distinction Origen made between Christ and other creatures in the idea that the generation of the Son happened from eternity, while the creatures were brought forth in time. According to the conceptions of this church teacher, the difference did not consist in their origin: for according to the **emanation system** <note>A philosophical concept where all things flow out from a primary divine source</note>, the Son shared this origin with all spiritual natures. Nor did it consist in the eternal generation of the Son versus the temporal production of the creatures: for these too were brought forth from eternity; rather, it consisted partly in the fact that the Son was the first and oldest among all things besides God, and partly in the fact that He was formed after the Father, while they were only brought forth according to His pattern, and also that the Son is still being continually generated by the Father (37). Huetius sought to save Origen's orthodoxy through another passage, or at least to give his expressions a milder sense, where he says: in the Gospel it is taught that everything

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Origen's theology, specifically addressing how he reconciles the eternity of the Son with the Father’s status as "Almighty." It connects the previous page's discussion of the Son's eternal generation to the status of other created beings.</meta>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...made through him, and nothing without him. <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 1:3</note>
From this it is evident that the designation of God as the Almighty is no older than the designation of Father: for through the Son, the Father is almighty (38). But one only needs to know the writer to be convinced that this essentially contributes nothing to his justification. Origen here certainly admits the existence of the Son before the existence of all creatures; and since God is never to be thought of without having been almighty, and all things were made through the Son, God can never be thought of without the Son. <margin>X</margin> This is indeed the reasoning of this Church Father; but for that reason, the priority given here to the Logos <note>The "Word" or the Son of God</note> consists only in his being older than the other produced things.
For it is known that Origen, like Hermogenes <note>A 2nd-century Christian philosopher who held that matter was co-eternal with God</note>, asserts that God could not have been called the Pantokrator <note>original Greek: "Pantokrator," meaning Ruler of All or Almighty</note> from eternity if there had been no creatures over whom he ruled (39). Origen even goes so far as to assert that the words in John—"all things were made through the same"—were intended to indicate nothing other than that, as he was begotten by the Father, all things were also begotten simultaneously with him, so that the generation of the Son simultaneously effects the creation of all things (40). The actual difference between the Son and the Aeons <note>In early Christian and Gnostic thought, these are various orders of spiritual beings or emanations from God</note> is based not so much on the different manner of their production—that the Son was begotten from the essence of the Father, whereas the Aeons were created.

---

(38) Huet’s *Origeniana*. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Book I, ch. 2.
Book II. p. 44. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; (40) The same [Huet], Homily II on various

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

<insert>through the Son to page 115</insert>

...been; rather, the difference consists in the fact that the Son was brought forth immediately, as the author of the so-called Apostolic Constitutions <note>A 4th-century collection of ecclesiastical law</note> says, whereas the other things were created mediately through the Son. The Son is, as it were, the original, while the Aeons are a copy of him.
For this reason, the author of the *Recognitions* <note>The Pseudo-Clementine Recognitions, an early Christian philosophical romance</note> also denies that the Holy Spirit can be called the "Son of God," because he was made through another who was also made—namely, through the Son (41). That Origen had this very same thought can be seen from the passages cited by Huet (42). <margin>*</margin>

The Homoousia <note>The theological concept that the Son is "of the same substance" as the Father</note> was accepted by Origen, as it was by all Platonists, and has always been a consequence of the system of emanation. The opponents of Origen have indeed drawn the conclusion from some passages of his works that he believed the Son was of a different substance than the Father; but many other passages occurring in his writings argue against this accusation (43). Even Athanasius <note>The primary defender of Trinitarian orthodoxy against Arianism</note> himself did not doubt Origen's orthodoxy regarding this doctrine.
But when one surveys his system, nothing is more certain than that his conception was very different from the subsequent Nicene one. He admits the Homoousia: but regardless, according to his opinion, the Son remains inferior to the Father even in essence, just as the rays of the sun are infinitely far...

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...are far distinct from the sun itself, even if they have their existence from it. The angels and intelligences <note>In early philosophy, "intelligences" refers to non-corporeal, rational beings or spirits</note> are also emanations and of the same substance <note>original Greek: ὁμοούσιοι (homoousioi), a key theological term meaning "of the same essence"</note> (44). But for that reason, they are not God and possess no unlimited perfection; rather, their production depended on the free will of God, and during that production, only as much perfection was communicated to them as was appropriate for them. The author of the so-called Clementine Homilies <note>A 2nd or 3rd-century collection of Christian writings attributed to Pope Clement I</note> therefore says: human bodies have immortal souls that have drawn in the divine spirit: but although they have proceeded from God and are of the same essence, they are not therefore gods (45). He also applies this to the Son, of whom he certainly says that if he were to have nothing more than the angels, he would not be distinct from them or have no advantage over them; yet he is also not God in the sense that the Father is, and only ever called himself the Son of God: for that which is begotten can be placed in no comparison whatsoever with that which is unbegotten, and two infinites could not possibly coexist with one another. Origen's mode of representation was very little different from this. Certainly, the Son has a great advantage over the angels, even if they, like him, have proceeded from the very same divine substance: for they have him to thank directly for their existence and are copies of him, just as he is a copy of the Father; therefore he is the Son of God, and what they possess of divinity, they have through

through him, who assigned them as much of it as was appropriate to them, according to their specification, as it were. But his production was not necessary, but rested merely on the free will of the Father. He proceeded from the substance of the Father, and Origen calls it both an impious doctrine if one does not consider the Son to be a person distinct from the Father, and if one denies his divinity and attributes to him an essence different from the Father's. He is still always begotten by the Father and draws the Father's divinity to himself (46). But to him was, in a certain sense, only a portion of the divine substance communicated, as Tertullian <note>An early Christian author (c. 155 – c. 220 AD) who was among the first to use the Latin word "trinitas" (trinity)</note> had already claimed. In this regard, he says that the goodness of the Father relates to that of the Son as the goodness of the Son relates to the other creatures standing beneath him, and he is properly only the image of the Father’s goodness. From this, he understands the expression in Luke 18:19: *No one is good but the one God* (47). And likewise, he also attributes to him a lesser knowledge than to the Father (48).

What Origen taught regarding the pre-existence of the soul of Christ—which flowed from his philosophical opinion on the pre-existence of human souls—and the union of the same with the Logos <note>The "Word" or the divine reason of God</note> even before his incarnation, and how the Logos was thereby enabled to subsequently take on human nature, I shall pass over here. the internal and the expressed <note>original: "dem innern und hervorgebrachten," or Greek: "Λόγος ἐνδιάθετος" (Logos endiathetos) and "Λόγος προφορικός" (Logos prophorikos). This philosophical distinction describes the Word as a silent thought within God vs. the Word spoken aloud to create the world.</note>, it can be explained what Justin says: that the Son became man through the power of the Logos (49).

Origen, however—who is so notorious among the ancients for his strange ideas, although very few of those who denounced him as a heretic were his equal in scholarship—is not the only one who, in this century, held such opinions about the person of the Lord that differed from preceding and succeeding church teachers. Rather, one finds the same in Clement of Alexandria, Gregory of Neocaesarea, and others who stood in a much better "odor of orthodoxy" <note>A phrase meaning they were generally considered to have "correct" or standard beliefs.</note>. Petavius has cited various passages from Clement of Alexandria which are meant to testify to his orthodox conceptions of the person of Christ; such as where he says of Christ that he is truly God and equal to the Almighty, that the Word is one with the Father, possesses the same goodness, and is also without time or beginning (50). Bull has also sought to explain the paradoxes still criticized in him, in order to present Clement as one who thought in perfect harmony with the Nicene Fathers (51). # Attempt at a History
<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

since otherwise the first would also be the second (53), of such a writer one cannot say that he ascribes eternity to the Son in the sense that the orthodox party did later. The Son remains eternal, but not **co-eternal with the Father** <note>original: "συναΐδιος πατρί" (synaidios patri)</note>: the latter is the cause that transcends even the Son, and the oldest of all things. There is no mention here of an **internal Word** <note>original: "Λογος ἐνδιάθετος" (Logos endiathetos) – the philosophical idea of the Word as a silent thought within God before being spoken aloud.</note>, and it seems that here Clement's ideas were not different from those of his student, Origen. Clement also holds the same thought later presented by Origen: that just as the Logos proceeded from God, he simultaneously caused the creation (54).

Many grand expressions regarding the divinity of the Son appear here and there in the writings of this Church teacher, such as when he says that the Son is in the Father, that both are one, namely God, and appeals to the passage in John 1:1: so he also gives thanks to the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, and adds that everything should give thanks to the One, in whom all things are, through whom all is one, and through whom also that which is eternal exists, of whom all are members, etc. This also includes his calling the Son the sole God of all men, the great, the holy God, of which Bull <note>George Bull (1634–1710), an English bishop and theologian who defended the "orthodoxy" of the pre-Nicene fathers.</note> has cited sufficient passages to prove the Nicene **consubstantiality** <note>original: "Homousie" (Homoousia) – the doctrine that the Father and Son are of the same essence.</note> from them (55).

But despite these strong expressions, this Church Father is still very far from the doctrine later established at Nicaea. Certainly, the Son is not **of a different substance** <note>original: "ἑτερούσιος" (heteroousios)</note>, different in essence from the Father, but according to his philosophical system, all intelligences are likewise not [of a different substance]. For all the grand predicates he assigns to the Son are not to be derived from a unity of essence, but from the sole will of God. On earth, says Clement, man is the most excellent; in heaven, it is the angel. But the nature of the Son is the most perfect, the most holy—that which is closest to the sole Almighty, the most sublime, which orders everything according to the Father's will—It cannot be divided, does not go from one place to another, is everywhere and is nowhere enclosed. — It is entirely intellect, paternal light, entirely eye, sees everything, hears everything — and all this is through Him who subjected all things to the Son (56). Anyone who considers the entire description with some accuracy will, despite the sublime expressions used, well understand the entire conception of this Church teacher, and how far it differs from the later Nicene as well as the Arian views. The angels, according to his conception, are more perfect than humans, not as being **of a different substance** <note>original: "Heteroousioi"</note>, but because they are placed one step higher, as it were closer and purer to the Divinity. The Son likewise is the most sublime and perfect thing that can be imagined (and here it immediately occurs to everyone why...

(does he not call him God in general?) not because he possesses his existence from a different essence than the angels; nor because he was begotten from the essence of the Father; but because he is even closer to the **Sovereign of All** than the angels, and everything is subjected to him through the will of the Father. This is a conception that is equally far removed from both the Arian and Athanasian systems of doctrine <note>Arians believed Christ was a created being, while Athanasians argued he was of the same substance as the Father. The author suggests Clement occupies a middle ground or a different category altogether.</note>. Thus, Clement also says of the Son that he was **made equal to the Master of all** <note>original Greek: "ἐξισωθεὶς τῷ δεσπότῃ τῶν ὅλων" (exisōtheis tō despotē tōn holōn)</note>. This is an expression in which Bull <note>George Bull (1634–1710), an English bishop and theologian who defended the orthodoxy of the pre-Nicene Fathers.</note> likewise believed he found the clearest proof for the Nicene doctrine (57). But the reason given at the same time—**because he was his Son** <note>original Greek: "ὅτι ἦν υἱὸς αὐτοῦ" (hoti ēn huios autou)</note>—certainly does not place this equality in the fact that the Son has his origin from the essence of the Father, as Clement and other Alexandrians of his time thought of the angels in a similar way; rather, this equality, or more accurately this "making equal," has its basis in the will of the Father, who himself made the Son equal to him in order to complete his work through him.

Essentially, the grand names also say just as little when he calls him **truly God** <note>original Greek: "ὄντως θεὸν" (ontōs theon)</note>, **the only God of all men** <note>original Greek: "μόνον τῶν πάντων ἀνθρώπων θεόν" (monon tōn pantōn anthrōpōn theon)</note>, **great God** <note>original Greek: "μέγαν θεόν" (megan theon)</note>, **holy God Jesus** <note>original Greek: "ἅγιον θεόν Ἰησοῦν" (hagion theon Iēsoun)</note>, **God and Lord of all** <note>original Greek: "θεὸν καὶ κύριον τῶν ὅλων" (theon kai kyrion tōn holōn)</note>, and so forth, or that he does not observe the peculiar distinction between **god** <note>original Greek: "θεός" (theos)</note> and **the God** <note>original Greek: "ὁ θεὸς" (ho theos)</note> later specified by Origen (58) <note>Origen argued that only the Father is "The God" (with the definite article), while the Son is "god" (without the article), indicating a derived divinity.</note>. For all of this does not rest upon the essence of the Son, and is therefore necessary, but rather upon the will and—

This depended partly on the will of God, who ordained it so, and partly because he always stands in the closest connection with the Father, is not cut off or separated, does not go from one place to another, but is always everywhere and is enclosed by nothing—entirely intellect, entirely fatherly light, and so on. This is almost exactly what Origen says, and wherein he distinguishes the Son from all other spiritual substances, namely: that he is still continually being begotten by the Father and draws his divinity to himself.

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

In the *Hypotyposes* <note>A lost work by Clement titled "Sketches" or "Outlines."</note> of Clement, according to Photius <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and influential scholar.</note>, there were said to be many passages that were Arian in nature, which Roussel <note>likely referring to Albert Roussel or a similar scholar; the German text says "die Russin," possibly a typo for a name or "the Russians," but in this context, it refers to a scholar's interpretation.</note> considered to be corrupted passages. However, no matter how often he may have said that the Son was made by the Father, a creature, nothing is more certain than that the conceptions of this Church Father were equally far removed from both the Arian and the Nicene definitions <note>The Nicene definition established that the Son is "of the same substance" as the Father, while Arians argued he was a created being.</note>.

Regarding Gregory of Neocaesarea, Basil says that he called the Son a **creature** and a **thing made** (60) <note>original Greek: κτίσμα (ktisma) and ποίημα (poiēma)</note>; expressions to which, as Basil says, heretics (Arians) appealed at that time. But this was certainly done with injustice, and perhaps Gregory’s conception was no different from that of Clement and Origen, his teacher, as can be seen from the passage cited by Basil from Gregory’s *Expositio fidei* <note>Exposition of the Faith</note>, which can hardly be reconciled with the Arian system of doctrine (61).

A closer alignment with Arian ideas might be found in what Dionysius of Alexandria said, where he says of the Son that he is the creature of the Father, and as different from the essence of the Father as the **farmer is from the grapevine**, and that as a creature, he did not exist before he was made. Since both—the Arians and Athanasius—called upon the authority of this church teacher to support their opinions,

call upon him, and sought to attribute their ideas to him, one can already suspect from this that his views were not as certain as both parties claimed. To be sure, the desire to distance oneself from one error has very often given birth to new ones. Arius and his followers also testified that they did not teach with Valentinus that the Son was an "emanation" of the Father, or with Mani that he was a "co-essential part" of the Father <note>Valentinus and Mani were leaders of Gnostic and Manichaean movements; the author is showing that Arians tried to frame themselves as middle-ground orthodox thinkers.</note>. Yet, the Arians would hardly have been able to appeal to Dionysius as they did if his conception of the person of the Son had been entirely of the nature presented by Athanasius. It is more than probable that there was something erroneous in Dionysius’s opinions, and his conception of Christ differed from the Nicene one, as has already been noted by Mosheim (63). Basil undoubtedly judged him most correctly when he said: "I do not approve of everything in this man; rather, there is much that I reject. <page-num>128</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...not out of malice of heart, but out of exaggerated heat, to oppose Sabellius <note>Sabellius was a 3rd-century priest who taught that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were merely three different "modes" or masks of a single divine person, a view known as Modalism.</note>. — It would have been enough if he had shown that the Son was not one and the same subject as the Father: — But he claimed not only a distinction of persons, but also a difference of essence, a reduction of power, and a difference in the glory of the Son (64). He adds that Dionysius, therefore, expresses himself very differently in various passages of his works; at times he does away with the "co-essentiality" <note>The idea that the Father and Son share the same divine nature.</note> when dealing with the Sabellians, and at other times he admits it when speaking to those who think as he does. According to this, it would certainly remain very doubtful what Dionysius's actual conceptions were. But if one considers that the Alexandrians in general were inclined to explain Christian principles through Greek philosophy—and Dionysius no less than Clement and Origen, whose pupil he was—then one can already form sufficient concepts of this bishop's opinion, which at its core was likely just as far removed from the Arian as from the Nicene system of doctrine <note>The Nicene system insisted on the Son being "of the same substance" (homoousios) with the Father, while Arians saw him as a created being.</note>. Dionysius may well consider the Son to be a <term>creature</term> <note>original: "Κτίσμα" (Ktisma)</note> of the Father and attribute to him an essence different from the Father; yet he does not thereby demote him to the level of other creatures, or derive his existence from nothing: he is out of the essence of the Father and an <term>emanation</term> <note>original: "Ἀπόῤῥοια" (Aporrhoia), meaning an outflow or radiation.</note> of the same. He may claim the co-essentiality of the Son as much as he likes, calling him—along with Athana-

<margin>(64) Basil the Great, Epistle 41. Works, Vol. III, p. 60.</margin>

<meta>catchword: nasio</meta>

<header>of Arianism.</header>

-sius—Light from Light, without beginning, and calling him the one who is always begotten and is from the Father: yet he still thinks differently about it than Athanasius, who cites him in support of his own opinion. Everything depends on what Dionysius considered to be the essence of God. This was, as can be seen from the passage cited by Eusebius from the book *Against Sabellius*, <term>unbegottenness</term> <note>original: "Ἀγεννησία" (Agennēsia), the quality of having no beginning or source.</note>, the state of being-of-oneself. What consequence Dionysius must have drawn from this regarding the essence of the Son has already been shown by Mr. Dr. Semmler <note>Johann Salomo Semmler (1725–1791), a German pioneer of the historical-critical method in theology.</note>: namely, none other than this—that the essence of the Son is different from the Father, because the former <note>The Father.</note>, but not the Son, possesses <term>unbegottenness</term> (65).

From Methodius, Photius has cited a passage from his book *Against Origen*, where he speaks of two powers: one that brings everything forth out of nothing, which is the Father, and one that orders what has been brought forth out of nothing <note>The Son.</note>. Petavius <note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a prominent Jesuit theologian.</note> rightly criticizes this, noting that he calls the Son and the Father "two powers," even though both are only one power (66). But it is well known that the older Church Fathers were anything but precise in the choice of their expressions. With more justification, the previously cited passage can undoubtedly be included here, where he calls the Son the oldest among the Aeons <note>In this context, "Aeons" refers to eternal ages or divine emanations.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether early Church Fathers held views that predated or aligned with Arianism. <page-num>130</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

It is a well-known fact that [Lucian] suffered martyrdom in the year 311. But whether his concepts of the person of Christ were equal to those of Arius <note>Arius taught that Jesus was a created being, not co-eternal with God the Father.</note> is certainly very much to be doubted. It is much the same here as with Dionysius of Alexandria; for while Alexander of Alexandria identifies Lucian as a successor to Paul of Samosata <note>A 3rd-century bishop whose views on the nature of Christ were condemned as heretical.</note> (from whom the error was allegedly passed down to Arius), Hilary <note>Hilary of Poitiers, a staunch defender of the Trinity.</note>, on the other hand, considers Lucian’s confession of faith—even though the term "co-essential" <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος" (homoousios); the key term used at Nicaea to describe the Son being of the same substance as the Father.</note> is missing from it—to be one that explains the deity of the Son of God perhaps more emphatically and clearly than even the Nicene creed itself (67). It all depends on how one interprets it. This much is certain, however: the expressions used in this creed are much milder than those later employed by the Arians. It is likely that Lucian’s thoughts on the person of Christ did not differ from those of Clement of Alexandria, Origen, and other Alexandrians, whose teachings (as Dr. Semler <note>Johann Salomo Semler, an influential 18th-century German biblical critic.</note> has very rightly noted) were widely spread at that time and constituted the scholarly knowledge among the presbyters (68). If his letters, mentioned by Suidas <note>A 10th-century Greek encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world.</note>, were still extant, one would be able to judge this more reliably.

<meta>This page continues the examination of early Church Fathers' views on the nature of Christ, specifically focusing on the Latin author Lactantius and his distinction between the Son and the angels. It follows the discussion of how early theologians struggled to define the "consubstantiality" of the Son before the formal doctrine of the Trinity was solidified.</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...inasmuch as it is consubstantiality <note>original: "Homousie"; the concept of being of the same substance or essence.</note>; but the Son of God has this in common with all the angels, who are also the Spirit of God and, like him, emanations from God. The preeminence of the Son is based partly on the fact that he was produced first, and subsequently all others were made through him; but also partly on the ridiculous distinction that he makes: because Spirit and Speech are produced from different parts—the Spirit or breath coming from the nostrils, while Speech comes from the mouth—there is thus a great difference between this Son of God and the rest of the angels. The latter came forth from God silently, but the Son came forth from the mouth of God with voice and sound (71).

Looking at these and other passages cited by Petavius <note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a Jesuit theologian known for his work on the history of dogma.</note>, Lactantius was not far from the opinion regarding an **internal Word** <note>original: "Λόγος ἐνδιάθετος" (Logos endiathetos); a philosophical term for a thought or word before it is spoken aloud.</note>, which various Greek Church Fathers and, among the Latins, Tertullian held. In this regard, he always admits the eternity of the Word. But as a Son, as a distinct person, he only received his existence through a certain extension of the divine essence (a concept that will appear more frequently in what follows) when the world was to be brought forth. He remains always a <term>thing created</term> <note>original: "Factitium"; something produced or made, rather than being uncreated like the Father.</note> by the Father, just as the other angels or spirits are his products, and his preeminence over them consists in the fact that he is the first, perfectly similar to the Father, that his manner of production occurred differently, and that these spirits (the breath of God) were only made into angels through him.

<note>(71) Lactantius, *On True Wisdom*, Book IV, Chapter 8.</note>
<meta>catchword: These</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

These matters could be detailed much further. However, these passages are sufficient to show every attentive reader what kind of opinions concerning the person of the Lord were held among the orthodox teachers of the church in this century. These opinions, despite all their diversity, had their origin nowhere else than in the sources of <term>Platonism</term> <note>The philosophy of Plato, which in this era emphasized a hierarchy of being between the ultimate God and the physical world.</note> of the Alexandrian school. When combined with Christianity, this was what was then called learned knowledge and "secret theology" among the Church Fathers. It occupied itself with these speculations not only regarding God and his attributes, but also regarding the <term>Aeons</term> <note>In various theological systems of the time, these were emanations or powers that flowed out from the divine essence.</note> and emanations of the divine being, and everything belonging to the intellectual world of the Platonists.

---

### IX.
## Conceptions of the Non-Catholics.

The very same reflection on the divine nature, and the effort to explain the manner in which the Son was produced by the Father—which generated such diverse opinions among the orthodox teachers of the church—also gave rise elsewhere to many strange opinions. Their adherents, depending on how zealously they persisted in their ideas, grew into distinct parties. For in this century, people made it a special business to speculate on these things, and the further one ventured into them, the more one made oneself worthy of the name of a profound theologian.

<meta>catchword: worthy.</meta>

<meta>catchword: out of</meta>

worthy. This was a malady that, in all probability, passed from the Gnostics <note>A diverse group of early religious movements that emphasized "gnosis" (secret knowledge) as the key to salvation.</note> to the Catholic <note>In this context, "Catholic" refers to the "universal" or "orthodox" Church as it was then establishing itself, rather than the modern Roman Catholic denomination.</note> teachers. Since souls, says Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD), a towering and controversial figure in early Christian theology.</note>, cannot reach their peculiar perfection in any other way than through the knowledge of divine things, the first object of their investigation is everything concerning the person of the Father. This is followed by investigations concerning his only Son, such as what his nature is and in what manner he is the Son of God (72).

It was these investigations that produced so many strange opinions among the Catholic teachers of these times—opinions that were actually merely philosophical speculations and entirely foreign to Christianity. These were indeed the source from which so many paradoxical concepts of the so-called <term>heretics</term> <note>original: "Kezzer" (modern: Ketzer). The author uses this term to denote those labeled as deviants by the emerging church hierarchy.</note> had their origin, although it cannot be denied by some of them that the foundation for this had been laid in much earlier times.

Besides the strange opinions of the Gnostic Christians and the Platonizing Church teachers, there had already been two opinions in the previous century. One of these did not consider Christ to be God, but only a mere man equipped with extraordinary gifts and advantages; the other, by contrast, did not distinguish him from the Father as a separate person at all. Before I speak of this, I must mention a singular opinion that perhaps would have gained just as many followers as others and formed a special party, if either its author had been less yielding, or if he had been dealt with in such a way that so often produces sects and stubborn heretics in the Church. This was the opinion put forward in this century by Beryllus, Bishop of Bostra, in Arabia.

Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), the first great historian of the Christian Church.</note> gives the following account of this. He says that Beryllus departed from the ecclesiastical rule and sought to introduce foreign doctrines alongside it. Namely, he had the audacity to say that our Savior and Lord, before he became man, had not existed as a distinct person: nor did he have a divinity of his own, but merely the paternal divinity dwelling within him (73). ...but rather a completely pure soul, which had been taken from the essence of the Father (74). This agrees with what Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380–439 AD), a historian of the early Christian Church.</note> relates: that at the synod held against Beryllus, the doctrine of the true human soul of Christ was confirmed. The account provided by Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the "Father of Church History," who had access to the original library at Caesarea.</note>—who had the official records before him and agrees with what Origen says—is undoubtedly the most correct. He notes that those who asserted that Christ only received his own personality at his incarnation, having previously existed only in the intellect of the Father with the Father's divinity dwelling within him, could not be received into the church. According to this account, the view of Beryllus bordered on both the opinion of Praxeas, Noetus, and Sabellius, as well as that of Artemon, Theodotus, and the Ebionites <note>A group of Jewish Christians who regarded Jesus as the Messiah but not as divine.</note>.

The doctrine held by some orthodox teachers of an <term>Internal Word</term> <note>original: Λόγος ἐνδιάθετος (Logos endiathetos). In Greek philosophy, this refers to a thought or reason still within the mind, before it is spoken aloud.</note> was undoubtedly the foundation of this particular system of teaching. Before this <term>Word</term> <note>original: Λόgos</note> had proceeded from the Father, it had no essence <note>original: οὐσίαν (ousian)</note> of its own, but existed within the Father. However, while these orthodox teachers allowed for the elevation of this <term>Word</term> to its own personality to precede the creation of all things, Beryllus allowed it to happen only when Christ became human. When the <term>Word</term> united with the human Christ, he then received his own essence <note>original: οὐσίαν</note>, or a <term>circumscription of essence</term> <note>original: οὐσίας περιγραφήν (ousias perigraphēn); a technical term for a distinct, defined individual existence.</note>, his own selfhood, or personality. But he did not have his own divinity; rather, the divinity of the Father was in him, and it revealed its extraordinary power through him.

Here, the opinion of Beryllus bordered on that of Sabellius, and on the other side, on that of Artemon and Theodotus. Whether he believed that this union of the <term>Word</term> <note>original: Λόγος</note> with the human nature occurred at the conception and birth of the Lord, or at the baptism of Christ, is uncertain. If we knew this, we would be even better informed about his entire conception, in which much is still obscure today, since not only the records of the synod held against him but also all of his works, which were praised by Eusebius (75), have been lost.

Very closely related to this opinion was that presented in the first half of the third century by Noetus in Asia, and a few years later by Sabellius in Africa. This was actually the same view that Praxeas had presented in the previous century.

If one looks away from the distorted representations that the Church Fathers—as is common with those who think differently—made of the teachings of Noetus and Sabellius, then the conception of these men was this: they denied the distinction of the three persons in the divine essence, and viewed the Word and Holy Spirit only as special attributes and effects of the divine essence.

Sabellius condemned those who denied that the Father was the Son and Spirit, since he [was accused] by his opponents...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into early Christian theological disputes, moving from Beryllus of Bostra to the more influential system of Sabellius. The author explores how the desire to protect the "Monarchy" (the absolute unity of God) led some thinkers to view the Trinity not as three distinct persons, but as different manifestations of a single divine essence.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...opponents blamed him for denying the Son and the Holy Spirit; but he maintained that Father, Son, and Spirit were only three different names or modes of manifestation of God, which therefore did not constitute three particular persons, but only a single <term>hypostasis</term> <note>original: ὑπόστασιν. In this context, Sabellius uses the term to mean a single underlying substance or individual existence, rather than the three distinct "persons" later defined in orthodox theology.</note>. This was—as far as one can see from the accounts of Athanasius, Epiphanius, Gregory of Nazianzus, and others—the main idea in the Sabellian system.

If one directs their attention to the occasion for this opinion, it was, as I have already said earlier in the case of Praxeas, none other than the great zeal for the doctrine of the **Monarchy** <note>The theological concept emphasizing that there is only one first principle or "ruler" (God), often used by those who feared that a Trinity implied three separate Gods.</note>.

When the Sabellians, says Epiphanius, meet an orthodox believer, they ask: "My friend! Do we believe in one God, or do we believe in three (76)?" The fear that by accepting three persons in the divine essence, one might distance themselves from the great doctrine of a single God, was the sole cause; it was much like how some, according to Gregory of Nazianzus, asserted that through the doctrine of the Holy Spirit as a distinct person, a new God—one not grounded in Scripture—was being introduced (77). But it was not this concern alone, but certain ideas, comparisons, and expressions found among some philosophical teachers of the orthodox church, with which they sought to make clear the manner of the gen-

<meta>catchword: gen-</meta>

---

<margin>(76) *Epiphanius, Against Heresies* LXII. p. 514.</margin>
<margin>(77) *Gregory of Nazianzus,* Oration on the Holy Spirit, Works p. 219. Basel edition.</margin>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

generation of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, and their relationship to the Father, that played no small part in this, as I have already noted. Added to this is a third factor, through which the differently and confusedly presented doctrinal concepts of this party are clarified in no small measure. To be sure, according to the concepts of the philosophical Christians of those times, God was the simplest and most indivisible unity that could be thought; but for that reason, they did not deny a certain **extension** of the divine essence. Even Dionysius of Alexandria, who otherwise fell into exaggeration out of his vehemence in opposing the Sabellians, says—when he wishes to defend himself against those who blame him for distinguishing the Son too much from the Father: "In the same way, we extend the inseparable unity into the **Trinity**, and contract the indivisible Trinity back into the unity (78)." The generation of the Son and the Holy Spirit was viewed to a certain extent as an extension of the divine essence, through which, of course, two particular hypostases emerged, and thus a <term>Trias</term> <note>A Greek term for Trinity.</note> came from the unity; however, their proceeding was not a separation, so the Son is still always begotten of the Father and draws his divinity to himself, just as the Holy Spirit still always proceeds from the Father. According to Athanasius, Sabellius had actually said: "Just as there are various gifts, but the same Spirit, so too the Father is the same; but he is *extended* into the Son and Spirit (79)."

<summary>This page explains the Sabellian view of the Trinity as a single divine essence that "extends" into three modes (Father, Son, Spirit) to preserve the unity of God. It highlights the philosophical tension between God as an indivisible unity and the theological necessity of the Trinity.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into early Christian theological disputes, moving from the general concept of the "Monarchy" of God to the specific system of Sabellius. The author contrasts the "orthodox" view—where the Son and Spirit have distinct existences rooted in the Father—with Sabellius's view of them as mere powers or modes of a single divine being.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

However, while the other teachers of the Church gave a distinct individual selfhood to the Son and the Spirit—who had emerged in this way from the unity, from the Father (who always remained, as it were, the sun and the primal source of the Godhead)—even though they always remained connected with the Father and proceeded from Him; *Sabellius* denied this distinction. He viewed the Son and Spirit only as powers of the divine essence, which were called now "Son," now "Spirit." Perhaps *Sabellius's* conception differed from those of the Catholic <note>In this historical context, "Catholic" refers to the "universal" or "orthodox" church of the early centuries, rather than the modern Roman Catholic Church.</note> teachers in one other respect as well. *Mosheim* <note>Johann Lorenz von Mosheim (1693–1755), a highly influential German Lutheran church historian.</note> at least held this view, and what *Epiphanius* <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403), a bishop known for his "Panarion," a massive work cataloging and refuting various heresies.</note> says about it—if he were only reliable enough—confirms this opinion (80). According to this view, the Son and Holy Spirit were not merely powers that proceeded from the Godhead to a certain extent; rather, Father, Son, and Spirit were three powers that constituted the divine essence in the same way that body, soul, and spirit constitute a human being, or the heating power, the lighting power, and the round shape constitute the sun.

Whichever of these interpretations holds true, it is certain that *Sabellius* did indeed distinguish the Father, Son, and Spirit from one another. Consequently, the accusation that he believed the Father became human, was crucified, etc., was nothing more than an unfortunate consequence drawn by his opponents (81):

People among the Christians had thought in almost the same way as early as *Justin's* <note>Justin Martyr (c. 100–165 AD), an early Christian apologist.</note> time. According to this view, Christ was indeed the Son of God, but not from eternity; rather, he only became so when he became human, at which point he received his own personality. This presumably gave the ancients the opportunity to make the Ebionites <note>The Ebionites were an early Jewish-Christian sect who emphasized the humanity of Jesus and followed Jewish law.</note> into predecessors of Sabellius.

One thing I still note: it is difficult to understand how Sabellius was able to reconcile the passages of Scripture with his system where the **Word** <note>original: "Λόγος" (Logos)</note> is given as the only-begotten Son of the Father, if he truly held the Father to be likewise only a power of the Godhead, like the Son. Therefore Epiphanius <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403 AD) was a bishop known for his fierce opposition to anything he considered heresy.</note> also objects to Sabellius, stating that the Son is not **a co-brother to the Father** <note>original: "συναδελφὸς τῷ πατρί" (synadelphos tō patri)</note>. Perhaps this was only a logical consequence drawn by Epiphanius, and Sabellius actually held the Father, as the original source of the Godhead, to be its only **hypostasis** <term>hypostasis: a theological term referring to a distinct, individual personhood within the Godhead</term>, and viewed the Son and the Holy Spirit as powers of that same Father. As far as this system otherwise distances itself from the Gnostic and Platonic philosophy among the Christians, it still has aspects where it borders on both, such as with the **system of irradiation** <note>original: "Irradiationssystem" — a theory where divine power flows out from God like rays from the sun, rather than God existing as distinct persons.</note> that was adopted by some older Jewish parties (82).

-al sects, Paul, Bishop of Samosata, also occupies a remarkable place.

The ideas that this bishop formed regarding the person of the Lord are described very differently by older and newer writers (83), but upon close examination, they were ideas that stood to some extent in the middle between those of Sabellius and Artemon <note>Artemon was a 3rd-century teacher who taught that Jesus was a mere man, though born of a virgin and superior to the prophets.</note>. He assumed only a single person in the divine essence, and this was the Father. But yet, from eternity, the Word was in God. What concepts he formed of the Holy Spirit is not clear; the disputes with Paul of Samosata actually only concerned the person of the Son. Meanwhile, one can conclude with the greatest probability that he understood it as nothing other than a divine power and attribute. For the eternal Word was, according to his conception, nothing other than the wisdom dwelling within God—the divine understanding—which is to the Godhead what reason is to a human being. Here, then, the doctrine of the ancients regarding an **internal word** <note>original: "Λόγος ἐνδιάθετος" (Logos endiathetos) — the Stoic and early Christian concept of "thought" or "reason" as it exists within the mind before being spoken.</note> came into play. But he denied that this inner word of the Father had become a particular person or received its own selfhood. Therefore came the accusation that he denied **God the Word** <note>original: "λόγον θεὸν" (logon theon)</note> and maintained that Christ was not **God before the ages** <note>original: "πρὸ αἰώνων θεὸς" (pro aiōnōn theos)</note>, namely insofar as he was spoken of as a particular hypostasis.

Paul did indeed accept an **uttered word** <note>original: "Λόγον προφορικόν" (Logon prophorikon) — the concept of the "Word" being spoken aloud or externalized into the world.</note>, but this was nothing other than the effect of divine under-

<meta>catchword: expansion</meta>

Here, then, the ideas of this man bordered very closely on those of **Sabellius**. According to his opinion, **Christ** only began to exist when he was born of **Mary**; he only then became the **Son**. But what was born of **Mary** was not the **Word**, not **God**, but a mere **human**, with whom the **Word**, or the **Wisdom of God**, united, and through which he surpassed all others in knowledge and sublime virtue; in this respect, he was **God**, the **Son of God**. Here the opinion of this man also approached that of **Artemon** <note>Artemon was a 3rd-century teacher in Rome who taught that Jesus was a mere man endowed with divine power</note>, as far as one can say with certainty about the latter's ideas. Hence the frequent reproaches made against **Paul**, that through him the errors of **Artemon** and the **Jews**—who regarded **Christ** only as a mere **human**—were being renewed. For even though **Paul** assumed a union of the **Word** with the **human Jesus**, the **Word** was not a distinct **hypostasis** <term>hypostasis: a technical term for a distinct, individual personhood or "subsistence" within the Trinity</term>, but only divine **wisdom** and **power**, which fell to his lot in a higher measure than to other prophets.

These were the ideas of this man, as far as they can be gathered from **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the "Father of Church History"</note> and from the synodal letter found in his writings from the **bishops** assembled at **Antioch**, and from **Athanasius, Epiphanius, Theodoret**, and other writers.

This is a comparison which, given the great difference between the opinions of **Paul** and **Nestorius**, likely owes its origin primarily to the desire to make the latter quite hateful (84). This idea was, of course, condemned at the Council of **Antioch**; meanwhile, followers of this opinion persisted into the following century, as there was generally no lack of people among the Christians who thought as **Paul of Samosata** did. Whether the Antiochene Fathers, however, held exactly the same ideas about the person of the Lord that were later established at **Nicaea** <note>The Council of Nicaea (325 AD) established the orthodox doctrine of the Trinity</note> is very uncertain. From their rejection of the term **of the same substance** <note>original: "Homousios" (ὁμοούσιος) — the core term of the Nicene Creed meaning that the Son is of the identical essence as the Father</note>, the **Arians** in former times, and in more recent times **Sanden, Whiston**, and **Petavius** themselves, have concluded the opposite (85).

Another distinct way of thinking that arose in the third century concerning the person of the Lord was that of the **Manichaeans**, which had its origin in a combination of **Persian teaching** <note>original: "Parsenlehre" — refers to Zoroastrianism or the religion of the ancient Persians</note> with Christianity, just as Gnostic philosophy had given the Gnostic Christians, and Platonic philosophy the Church Fathers, the opportunity for their specific ideas about the Son.

<meta>The previous page concluded the discussion on Paul of Samosata, who viewed Christ as a mere human united with the divine Word or Wisdom, which Paul saw as an expansion of God’s power rather than a distinct person. This page transitions to Mani, the founder of Manichaeism, exploring how he synthesized Persian philosophy with Christian concepts of the Trinity, setting the stage for how these ideas were later used in arguments against Arianism.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
### Attempt at a History

**Mani**, the founder of this particular sect, which arose in the **Orient** and spread widely from there—still having many followers in the **West** as late as the twelfth century—had generally made it a law for himself to place the theology and philosophy of the **Persians** (among whom he arose) into such a connection with the teachings of **Christianity** that not only could both coexist, but the latter would even receive a higher degree of perfection through those insights than was found among the common **Christians**. This was a sickness common to all **Gnostics**, and it had passed from them even to the **Orthodox**. If the latter found agreements in their philosophy with **Christianity**, **Mani** found similar ones in his own, which in any case had much in common with the **Platonic** and **Pythagorean** philosophy and was derived from the same source. This is visible in all other parts of the **Manichaean** system, and one should therefore not be surprised to find here, where the person of **Christ** is being discussed, pieces in which **Mani** agrees with those among the **Orthodox** teachers who sought to reflect most deeply on the divine nature (86).

**Mani** was, in the truest sense, a **Trinitarian**. He assumed a supreme being which, in his opinion, was indeed immaterial, but was a pure essence of fire and light, and was that being from

This was the **Father**, or according to the **Persian** system, **Time without boundaries** <note>original: "Zeit ohne Grenzen" — a reference to Zurvan, the Zoroastrian personification of infinite time</note>, the **Eternal**, as **Anquetil** says according to the **Zend books** <note>original: "Zendbücher" — referring to the Zend-Avesta, the sacred texts of Zoroastrianism</note> (87).

The description that **Mani** gives of this primordial being, the **Father**, as recorded by **Augustine**, is uncommonly sublime and—leaving aside some enigmatic descriptions in the Oriental taste—perfectly **Orthodox** (88). This being was the simplest thing that could be conceived, and therefore indivisible, but was nonetheless capable of **extension**, as others before **Mani** had already thought. But since **Mani** sought to unite **Persian** doctrine and **Christian** concepts, he also spoke of the **Son** and **Holy Spirit**, whom he truly distinguished from one another in name.

It is difficult to determine what concepts **Mani** actually associated with this.
