On the contrary, **orthodox** believers have sought to find a correspondence between **Manichaean** and **Arian** ideas. **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a former Manichaean who became a leading Christian theologian and critic of the sect.</note> says at one moment that the **Manichaeans** held a belief regarding the **Trinity** similar to the **orthodox**, and never presumed to deny that the **Father**, **Son**, and **Holy Spirit** were of the same essence; yet at another moment, he speaks of their system of doctrine as if they made a great distinction between these persons and even separated them by location. **Beausobre** <note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a French Protestant theologian famous for his critical history of Manichaeism.</note> has shown on many occasions how little one can rely on **Augustine’s** testimony. **Mosheim** <note>Johann Lorenz von Mosheim (1693–1755), a German Lutheran church historian.</note> and **Beausobre** have already judged quite correctly that **Mani** found a **Trinity** already present in the **Persian** <note>Original: "Parsenlehre" referring to the religion of the Parsees or Zoroastrians.</note> teachings, and that what he says on this matter must be explained from this source (90). This applies especially where the person of **Christ** is discussed, where the **Persian** doctrine of **Ormuzd** <note>The Zoroastrian supreme god of light and goodness, also known as Ahura Mazda.</note> served as the foundation in **Mani's** system. According to the **Zend-Avesta**, **Ormuzd** was begotten from the purest light and came from the one who is its source. To be sure, he is always the result of the mixing of the **Primal Being** and **Primal Fire**, as are perhaps all **Intelligences** <note>Spiritual or celestial beings.</note>; yet he is still **born Light from Light**, the epitome of all perfections, the limited image of the **Eternal**, the **Firstborn**, who was brought forth from the **Primal Being** of time without limits by the speaking of his own **Word**. However, whether he arose through **emanation** from the **Primal Being** or was created remains uncertain (91).

**Mani** found these concepts in the **Persian** doctrine of **Ormuzd** and applied them to the **Christian** doctrine of the **Son of God**, just as the **orthodox** had applied the **Platonic** doctrine of the **Logos** <note>The "Word" or divine reason, a concept from Greek philosophy used to explain Christ’s divinity.</note> to the same. Perhaps the explanations of philosophical **Christians**, which were especially influential in the **East**, confirmed **Mani** in his thoughts; and perhaps his system would never have been so rejected if it had contained only this, and not other things at the same time which were far more intolerable.

When one looks at these matters free from prejudice, as they lie before us, this comparison can hardly surprise anyone: at the very least, there were certain striking features in the **Persian** doctrine of **Ormuzd** and in the **Christian** doctrine of the **Son of God** that seemed to allow for such a union. What the former taught of **Ormuzd**—that he was the **Firstborn**, **Light from Light**, the image of the **Infinite**, the epitome of all perfections, and proceeded through the speaking of his own **Word**—was precisely what the **Christians** also said of the **Son of God**. Just as **Ormuzd** is the creator and author of all that is good and holy, the father, producer, and ruler of the **Amshaspands** <note>The "Amesha Spenta," or divine sparks/archangels in Zoroastrianism.</note> and all other **Intelligences**, and the actual **representative** of the **Primal Being**; so too was the **Son**, according to the ideas of the **Christians**. From this, one can see with reasonable accuracy the concepts **Mani** formed regarding the person of **Christ**. **Beausobre** <note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a French Protestant scholar of Manichaeism.</note> took great pains to prove the orthodoxy of the **Manichaeans** on this point; others have denied this entirely. Between these two claims lies a middle path that brings us closer to the truth. The **Arians** <note>Followers of Arius who believed Christ was a created being, not of the same substance as God the Father.</note> stated in their letter to **Alexander** <note>Bishop Alexander of Alexandria, a primary opponent of Arius.</note>: "We do not teach as **Valentinus** <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher.</note> did, that the Son is a <term>Probole</term> <note>original: "Probole," a Greek term for a "projection" or "outflowing" of the divine</note>, an emanation of the Father; nor like **Mani**, that he is a consubstantial <note>Being of the same fundamental essence or substance.</note> part of the Father" (92). If **Mani** did indeed teach the latter, then it is clear that his ideas shared much with those of many **orthodox** believers; but it is then equally impossible that the **Arians**—as some **orthodox** writers claim—agreed with the **Manichaeans** in their ideas.

According to **Beausobre**, **Mani's** conception was entirely Platonic. The entire intellectual world consisted of emanations from the highest Godhead or the Father, and among these emanations, the **Son** and the **Holy Spirit** were the most eminent. This is certainly a conception held by many great and **orthodox** teachers of the Church. However, there is no reason why one should explain **Mani's** concepts through **Plato** rather than from the philo-

According to this system, which did not accept the production of things through emanation, and even described **Ormuzd** <note>The Zoroastrian god of light.</note> as arising from a mixture of water and fire (93), the **Son** was always a creature of the **Father**. But because the Father had formed him from the purest light—just as he himself was an essence of fire and light—the Son was a consubstantial part of the Father. Here, then, **Mani’s** system stood to some extent in the middle between the systems of the **Orthodox** and the **Arians**.

From this, one can explain much of what has been said by the **Orthodox** and the **Arians** regarding **Manichaeism**. The **Son** was always "Light from Light," the archetype of the Infinite, the epitome of all perfections, and <term>homoousios</term> <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος," a Greek term meaning "of the same substance," central to the Nicene Creed.</note> with the Father, as **Photius** <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and influential scholar.</note> asserts of the **Manichaeans** (94). It also confirms the **Persian** teaching itself to a certain degree, according to which the Father is Primal Light and Primal Fire, and from a mixture of this with the Primal Water, **Ormuzd** was created; yet he remained a creature of the Father, whose perfections were far more exalted than those of the **Son**.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Mani’s theology, specifically examining how his views on the Son and the Holy Spirit diverged from Christian orthodoxy by incorporating elements of Persian and Chaldean philosophy. It addresses the controversial claim that Mani viewed the Son and Satan as parallel emanations from the Father.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

that they held him to be a creature of the Father, differing in very important points from the ideas of the **Orthodox**. For while the latter asserted that the divine essence was still constantly communicated to the Son, **Mani**, on the other hand, assumed that the Son and the Holy Spirit were entirely separate from the Father, and also distinct in terms of location, since the Son has his dwelling in the **Sun**, but the Holy Spirit in the **Air**. These are ideas which, as one clearly sees, were borrowed from **Persian** and **Chaldean** <note>Referring to the ancient astrological and philosophical traditions of Mesopotamia.</note> philosophy.

Regarding the eternity of the Son, **Mani’s** system is subject to many difficulties. According to the almost generally accepted opinion, **Mani** held the Son and the Holy Spirit to be beings who were created by the Father only after the production of this world; or at least that if the coarser visible world—as a more recent scholar says—had not arisen, there would have been neither Son nor Holy Spirit in the system of the **Manichaeans** (95). This might not be very far removed from the system of some **orthodox** teachers of the church, by whom the production of the personality of the Son was also placed in connection with the existence of the visible world.

What **Fortunatus** <note>A Manichaean priest who famously debated Saint Augustine in 392 AD.</note> says in **Augustine**, that the Word was born only since the creation of the world, seems to confirm this opinion. But
<meta>catchword: it</meta>

---

(95) **Semler’s** *Historical Introduction to Baumgarten’s Polemic*. I. p. 284. <note>Johann Salomo Semler (1725–1791) was a German biblical critic; Siegmund Jakob Baumgarten (1706–1757) was a prominent theologian.</note>

<column-break/>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

it is not only inconceivable how the **Manichaeans**, holding this view, could call the Son the **Firstborn**, as is done by **Secundinus**, who attributed to him the right of primogeniture <note>The right of the firstborn to inherit the primary rank.</note> even before the intelligences of the invisible world (96); but also how, with this same view, one could accuse the **Manichaeans** of asserting that the Father's first two productions were the **Son** and **Satan**. **Beausobre** <note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a scholar who argued that Manichaeism was more rational than its critics claimed.</note> wanted to regard this as a mere slander, and the conclusion—that the **Manichaeans** and their descendants, the **Bogomils** <note>A medieval dualist sect in the Balkans, often linked by later writers to Manichaeism.</note>, held **Satan** to be older than the Son and to be greater and more excellent—is undoubtedly an invention of the monk **Euthymius**. Yet, not everything here is mere slander. According to **Persian** doctrine <note>The Zoroastrian tradition.</note>, **Ahriman** <note>The principle of evil and darkness.</note> was indeed, like **Ormuzd** <note>Ahura Mazda, the principle of light and goodness.</note>, produced by the Primal Being, and they stood in parallel with one another (97).

That this concept was also not entirely unknown among the other Christians of these times, and was not merely an invention of **Mani**, is visible from the passage of **Lactantius** <note>An early Christian author (c. 250–325 AD) who served as an advisor to Constantine I.</note> cited above (*).

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Mani’s theology, specifically refuting the idea that he believed the Son was created after the physical world. It draws parallels between Manichaean thought and Persian Zoroastrianism, citing the influence of figures like Beausobre and Anquetil-Duperron on the study of these systems.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

The most important evidence, however, which opposes the claim that **Mani** <note>The 3rd-century Persian prophet and founder of Manichaeism, which combined elements of Christianity, Gnosticism, and Zoroastrianism.</note> held the Son to be a being created only after the production of this world, is that he attributed the production of all good spiritual natures to the Son. This is entirely in the style of the **Persian doctrine**, according to which **Ormuzd** <note>Ahura Mazda, the supreme creator deity in Zoroastrianism representing light and goodness.</note> is likewise the producer of the **Amschaspands** <term>Amesha Spentas: "Immortal Holies," divine sparks or archangel-like beings in Zoroastrianism</term> and **Izeds** <term>Yazatas: Zoroastrian spirits or lesser deities worthy of worship</term>, just as all dark and evil natures and **Dews** <term>Daevas: harmful supernatural beings or demons in Persian mythology</term> were produced by **Ariman** <note>Ahriman or Angra Mainyu, the destructive spirit and adversary of Ormuzd.</note> (98).

Where it therefore appears that **Mani** placed the creation of the Son only after the emergence of this world, such a thing is likely to be understood as the *appearance* of the Son. According to the mythical theology of the **Manichaeans**, he appeared as a power of light in order to snatch the stolen particles of light and the souls away from the power of darkness. **Mani’s** conception here bordered on that of some **Platonizing** Church Fathers, who admittedly did not hold the Son to be eternal, but nonetheless placed his existence long before the emergence of this visible world.

In general, it seems that **Mani** neither thought as orthdoxly as **Beausobre** <note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a French Protestant scholar famous for his extensive history of Manichaeism.</note> believed, nor departed as far from the opinions of many church teachers as others believe. Perhaps 
<meta>catchword: there would have been</meta>

among them, as **Epiphanius** <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403), a staunch defender of orthodoxy known for his catalog of heresies.</note> says, there were parties—likely those who sought to unite their religious principles more with Eastern philosophy 
<column-break/>
than others—for which this opinion serves as proof.

(98) *Zend-Avesta*. II. P. 345. 346.

---

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

we would be more able to properly survey his true system if we could compare his writings with the reports of the Persian religion and philosophy now given to us by **Anquetil** <note>Abraham-Hyacinthe Anquetil-Duperron (1731–1805), the French orientalist who brought the first Zend-Avesta manuscripts to Europe.</note>. The entire system of this man undoubtedly had the intention of gaining a following among both **Christians** and **Magi** <note>Persian priests or wise men.</note>, just as Greek church teachers from the **Alexandrian school** sought to achieve the same regarding the Pagans.

### X.
## On particular ideas in several forged works, and Conclusion.

From what I have said so far regarding the various ideas that existed among both the so-called **Orthodox** and specific parties concerning the **Person of Christ**, one can already form a fairly accurate picture of the entire state of Christian doctrine on this point in the first three centuries. Before I proceed from here to the actual history of the **Arian controversies**, I must still mention the **spurious writings** that were circulated among Christians during these times, and in which some remarkable features relevant to this topic occur. These include **apocryphal Gospels** and other writings attributed to the Apostles, the **Testament of the Patriarchs**, the **Clementine works**, the **Canons of the Apostles**, and others of a similar nature.

<summary>This page explores the parallels between Mani's theology and Zoroastrianism, suggesting Mani intended to bridge the gap between Christian and Persian beliefs. It also introduces the study of early Christian apocrypha as a precursor to the Arian controversy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether early apocryphal texts supported the Arian or orthodox views of Christ’s nature. The previous page discussed the Manichaean view of the Son as a spiritual creator figure.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>
# Attempt at a History

Of some of the alleged Gospels, it is only to be expected that the doctrine regarding the person of the Lord presented in them would perfectly agree with the concepts of the party by which they were accepted. This applies to the Gospels of the **Nazarenes** <note>Early Jewish Christians who maintained Jewish law alongside belief in Christ's divinity.</note>, the **Ebionites** <note>A Jewish Christian sect that generally viewed Jesus as a human prophet rather than divine.</note>, those of the various **Gnostic** parties, and that of the **Manichaeans**.

**Epiphanius** <note>A 4th-century bishop and counter-heresy writer.</note> indeed accuses the *Gospel of the Egyptians* of asserting the **Sabellian** doctrine—the idea that the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit were one and the same (99); but this does not agree at all with the Gnostic origin of this Gospel indicated by **Clement of Alexandria** and **Jerome** (100).

**Photius** <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and scholar.</note> says something similar regarding the *Travels of the Apostles* (attributed to the Manichaean **Leucius Charinus**), claiming that the Son was considered one with the Father in that work (1); but if his other reports are no more reliable than this one, there is little to be built upon them.

In other such alleged Gospels, such as the so-called *Protevangelium of James*, the *Gospel of the Infancy of Christ*, and the so-called *Gospel of Nicodemus*, nothing is found

that would be contrary to the orthodox system of doctrine. Even the miracles attributed to Christ in his childhood, which are admittedly quite absurd, testify to the fact that the authors of these accounts did not think of him as a mere prophet, but as a being of a higher kind.

In the *Protevangelium*, Mary asks the angel who announces the conception of Christ: "Shall I conceive by the living God, and give birth as all women give birth?" Admittedly a strange question, but one which, like the angel's reply modeled after Luke 1, always indicates the orthodox concept of the author. This also includes what is told in this same Gospel regarding the astonishment of all nature at the birth of the Lord (2).

The same applies to the *Acts of Pilate*, or the alleged *Gospel of Nicodemus*, where various passages occur in support of the divinity of Christ. But even if older accounts may have served as the foundation, the writing itself is a more recent fiction, which cannot be considered here.

# Attempt at a History

...the coming of Jesus, as it happened; of his mission, and why he was sent by the Father; of the power of his deeds; of the mysteries that he made known during his stay in the world; by what power he performed his miracles; and of the new doctrine that he preached; of the insignificance, poverty, and lowliness of his external humanity: how he humbled himself, died, and diminished his divinity; what he suffered from the Jews, how he was crucified, and so forth. To be sure, these are all rather general determinations; but from them one sees at least this much: that the author did not consider Christ a mere human, but attributed a divine nature to him, and yet did not consider him one and the same with the Father. Similarly, in the passage shortly preceding, Christ is called our Lord and God, and Son of God, and it is said of him that he accomplished that which was assigned to him by his Father (3).

Still more striking are the passages that appear in the so-called *Testament of the Twelve Patriarchs* <note>A series of twelve books staged as the deathbed speeches of the twelve sons of Jacob; though based on Jewish traditions, it contains many early Christian additions.</note>. For instance, it says in the *Testament of Simeon*: then Shem shall be glorified, when the Lord, the great God of Israel, shall appear on earth as a man. — Then I shall rise with joy and praise the Most High for his wonders, for God, having taken on a body and eaten with men, has saved them (4).

# of Arianism.

It is visible that Christ is the subject here, who is also soon after called King, God, and Man. Other passages also agree with this, such as where it is said that the Lord himself, the light of righteousness, shall appear, and men will see God in human form; that God will appear and dwell among men on earth; that God, hidden in the man, will come like a human and eat and drink with men; and it is said of the Jews that they did not believe in God, who appeared as the Redeemer in the flesh (5). These are all expressions that leave not the slightest doubt that the author of this work acknowledged the divinity of Christ. Just as little do the expressions where he is called not only the Son of God and the Most High, but also the "growth" or the "offspring" <note>The German "Gewächs" and "Sprößling" here suggest a being that has sprouted or grown out of the Father's essence.</note> of the highest God, leave any doubt that he derived the existence of the Son from the essence of the Father. But it remains highly probable that the Jewish and Platonic doctrine of **emanations** <term>Emanation: The idea that various levels of reality or beings "flow out" from a single, supreme source, becoming less divine as they move further away from the origin.</term> played a role here; for the Son, although he is called the King of Heaven who appeared in human form, is presented only as an exalted angel who represents humanity before God and is the mediator between God and man (6). He is always a being who is infinitely greater than the angels, but yet also always lesser than the Father.

<meta>This page continues the examination of early Christian literature to trace the roots of Arian thought. <page-num>160, 161</page-num>
# Attempt at a History of Arianism.

In the so-called *Clementine Recognitions* <note>An early Christian theological romance attributed to Clement of Rome, though likely written by an anonymous author; it survives mainly in a Latin translation by Rufinus.</note>, which were probably written at the end of the second or the beginning of the third century, one finds an even more significant contribution. They seem more likely to be the work of an Alexandrian Jewish-Christian than to have been corrupted by an *Ebionite* <note>Members of an early Jewish Christian sect who maintained Jewish law and generally viewed Jesus as a human prophet rather than divine.</note>, or by followers of *Ebion*, as some have believed. The author's conceptions of Christ diverge widely from the notion of the *Ebionites*, whatever that may have been. *Photius* <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and scholar whose 'Bibliotheca' provides summaries of many ancient works now lost to us.</note> claimed to have found in them many absurdities and blasphemies against the Son of God, in the manner of *Arius* (7). What gave occasion for this accusation will soon be shown; perhaps it was only a single expression, though many other passages outweigh it.

The first thing that is striking concerns the traces of the doctrine of the Trinity that appear in this writer. Finally, it says,
> I exhorted them that they themselves, before they went to the Gentiles to preach to them the knowledge of God the Father, might first be reconciled with God through the acceptance of His Son: for otherwise they could not be saved, if they did not hasten through the grace of the Holy Spirit to be purified through the baptism of the triple invocation (8).

And shortly thereafter:
> if someone is not baptized with water, under the invocation of the triple blessedness <note>original: "dreifache Seeligkeit". This refers to the baptismal formula—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—but the author notes the unusual phrasing.</note>, he cannot enter the kingdom of heaven.

— This is

(7) *Photius, Library [Bibliotheca], Codex 112, 113, p. 289.*

the decree of the unbegotten God. —
> But do not think that we speak of two unbegotten gods, or that the one is divided into two: rather, we call the Son of God the Only-begotten, who was begotten not from another source, but from him himself (the Father) in an ineffable way, and we say the same also of the Holy Spirit (9).

In both passages, the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are mentioned, and in the latter, incorrect ideas are even refuted—perhaps Gnostic ones, as *Cotelier* <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier (1626–1686), a French scholar who edited the works of the Apostolic Fathers.</note> believes based on *Gregory of Nazianzus*; however, it is still very doubtful whether the author's concepts of the Trinity are indeed the same as those later regarded as the orthodox doctrine. At the very least, it is strange that the author speaks so little here, as in other passages, of the invocation of the three divine persons, but only of the invocation of the triple blessedness, with which a clear concept can hardly be associated (10).

The representation of Christ as Prophet, Teacher, and Lawgiver is the most prominent feature in these writings, and Christ is almost always called the true Prophet, and for this purpose is also compared with *Moses*. The purpose of his mission, according to the author, is actually this: that through him the sacrificial service might be ended, and through baptism

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the 'Clementine Recognitions' regarding the nature of Christ, specifically how the author distinguishes Christ from created beings like angels while attempting to avoid the physical metaphors used by other early church writers.</meta>

baptism the grace of God is communicated to man (11). But regardless of this, Christ, according to his <note>Referring to the author of the *Recognitions*.</note> concepts, is not merely a messenger, or an angel who carries out the task assigned to him by God. He is not called God in the same sense as the angels, who, as the author says, are called gods out of reverence for the one who sent them (12); rather, he is truly God because he is the Son of God. Our Master, it says, who is the Son of God and of man, is evidently good (13). However, he is not only the Son of God—a title that is also given to lesser natures—but he is the **Only-begotten Son**. As to why both titles are attributed to him, the author expresses himself in the following manner: God, who never began to be (the Father), generated the Firstborn of all creation, as was fitting for God: not in such a way that He changed or transformed Himself, nor through division, outflowing, or extension: for these are passions <note>In this context, 'passions' refers to physical changes or sufferings that affect material bodies.</note> that belong to physical things. And shortly thereafter he adds: He (the Son) is called the Only-begotten because he has everything he is from the Ungenerated: he is called the Son because he is born of the Unbegotten (14).

It is visible that the author derived the existence and essence of the Son from the Father, and

his conceptions of this diverge from those that were accepted among many orthodox teachers of the church, as well as among various Catholics. He accepts neither the <term>emanation system</term> <note>The idea that the Son 'flowed out' from the Father like water from a spring or light from the sun.</note>, nor extension, nor a division in the divine nature; and yet, the Son has his essence from the essence of the Father in such a way that he is thereby distinct from the angels and spirits, and is, in a special sense, the only-begotten Son of God.

To this, it seems contradictory when he calls the Son the "first growth" of the Father: an expression actually used by those Church Fathers who considered the Son to be an outflow of the divine essence, as was done by Athenagoras, Justin, Methodius, and others (15); but the author accepts neither the <term>emanation system</term> nor the doctrine of an <term>internal word</term> <note>original: "λόγος ἐνδιάθετος" (logos endiathetos). This was a philosophical concept used by some early Christians to describe Christ as God's internal thought before he was 'spoken' into existence at creation.</note>. All of these are, to him, conceptions that are too physical. God, he says, begat the Son and remained as He was, without suffering any division. — As He willed, His power was not too sluggish to do that which He willed, and did not go further than His will: but according to the measure by which He willed, He begat the Son. However, he explains the manner and way of this begetting through this comparison: if coarser bodies, without suffering any changes, produce shadows, how much more can we assert that the Only-begotten of the

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the 'Clementine Recognitions,' focusing on the author's philosophical arguments regarding the relationship between the Father and the Son, specifically the concept of an "ungenerated power" and the eternal nature of Christ.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

ungenerated power follows when the will of that power goes before it. But just as bodies must be conceived of before the shadow they produce, so much more must the ungenerated power be conceived of before the generated power, etc. (16). A very strange comparison.

One might think that the author, in this instance, simultaneously considered the necessary eternal existence of the **Son**, in such a way that just as no shadow can be conceived without a body, and conversely no body without a shadow, so too the **Son** could never be conceived without the Father, and the Father never without the **Son**. And indeed, the author asserts the eternity of the **Son of God**, whereas other—even orthodox—teachers only asserted the eternity of the internal Word, placing his production as the Son only shortly before the creation of things. The Son, he says, has been with the Father from the beginning and always: **Christ** has been from the beginning and always. He therefore also calls him the eternal **Christ**, who is the **Son of God** and the beginning of all things (17). But despite these assertions, he allows the production of the **Son** to depend solely on the will of the Father, and calls him the "will out of the first will," the "thing made," and the "creature of the Father" (18).

(18) *Book I. num. XXIV. Book III. num. VIII.*

<header>of Arianism.</header>

These are, in all probability, the expressions that <term>Photius</term><note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and a scholar who reviewed many ancient texts that are now lost.</note> attributes to the author as blasphemies in the manner of <term>Arius</term><note>Arius was a priest whose teachings led to the Arian controversy; he argued that Jesus was a created being and not co-eternal with God the Father.</note>, although these expressions had already been used regarding **Christ** by other Church Fathers before the Arian era.

One thing I cannot pass over on this occasion, where the eternity of the **Son** is being discussed, is this: that the author says of **Christ** that he is "Standing eternally" <note>original: "Stans in aeternum"</note>, the **Son of God** who remains forever, just as <term>Simon</term><note>Simon Magus, a figure from the New Testament often depicted in early Christian literature as a heretical rival to the apostles.</note> had claimed of himself. This is explained by the fact that **Simon** claimed that even his body, because of the divine power connected to it, was not subject to decay. "The Remaining One" ("Standing eternally") and **Christ** are, for the author, completely synonymous (19). The author of the *Recognitions* places the reason for the name **Christ** in the fact that the Father anointed him with the oil from the Tree of Life (20). Whether this refers to the human or divine nature of the **Son** is very uncertain. At the very least, according to the concepts the author forms of the person of **Christ**, the latter could also be the case.

However much the author otherwise asserts the eternity of the **Son** and his existence from the Father, he nevertheless does not consider him to be God in the same sense as the Father. It is, as it were, only a "secondary divinity" <note>original: "Deitas secundaria"</note> that he attributes to him, which is derived from the fact that he calls him the "creature" <note>original: "Factura"—literally something made or manufactured.</note>

regards as the highest God. He says that the service of the only God, who is uncreated, is excepted; but all else is His creation. Just as it is characteristic of Him to be God, who alone is not made; so too all that is made is not truly God (21). That the Son is counted among these <term>created things</term> <note>original Latin: *facturis*</note> by the author is further evident from his statement that the Holy Spirit can be called neither Son nor Firstborn, because he was made by one who was himself made, and is subordinate to the Father and counted with the Son, because he is the first of the second and the perfect seal of divine power (22).

These are ideas that differ in many respects from all those previously cited, yet one can clearly see that their author wanted to find a certain middle path between the opinions prevailing in his time—though this path was certainly no better.

Furthermore, various traces of the subordination <note>In early Christian theology, 'subordinationism' is the belief that the Son and the Holy Spirit are subordinate to God the Father in nature or being.</note> of the Son still occur: and of the Father it is said that He is the head of the Only-Begotten, who stands as it were in the middle between the Father and the creatures, and through whom one comes to the Father. It is also said that God made the world through Christ, and that the Holy Spirit works at God's command, and as it were

The so-called Clementine Homilies stand reasonably in parallel with the Recognitions; one seems to have been the basis for the other, and they deserve special attention regarding their conceptions of Christ. Such traces of the Trinity do not occur here as in the Recognitions, and the author, when speaking of baptism, avoids the expression used there regarding the invocation of the triple blessedness <note>A reference to the Trinitarian formula: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</note>. However, the doxology <note>A short hymn of praises to God, often added to the end of canticles, psalms, or hymns.</note> still appears, with which the author has Peter conclude his speech, and indeed in the way it has always been customary in the orthodox church (24). <meta>catchword: God</meta>

departs from the orthodox church, but he also differs markedly from the author of the *Recognitions*. He attributes divinity to the Father in the truest and highest sense because He is the **Uncreated**. To Him alone, he says, belongs the most excellent honor, because He alone is the **Unbegotten**. Everything else is created. It is therefore characteristic of the Uncreated to be God, and therefore anything that has been created is not God.

That only the Father is being spoken of here, and not the Son as a person of the divine essence—and that the actual Creator of things is not included—emerges even more clearly from the following passage, where it says: "We have only one God, only one, who created and adorned everything, and whose son is Christ." — Our Lord has therefore also declared that besides this one there are no gods; he did not present himself as God, but rather blessed the one who called him the Son of God, by whom everything was created (26).

The author then has Simon <note>Refers to Simon Magus, often depicted in early Christian literature as the theological rival of the Apostle Peter.</note> pose the remarkable question: whether the one who is from God must not also be God? With this, he seeks to dispute the doctrine of the <term>Monarchy</term> <note>The "Monarchy of the Father" is a theological concept emphasizing that God the Father is the sole source and primary principle of the Trinity, a key point of debate in early Arian and anti-Arian controversies.</note>, since from Peter's own assertion that Christ is God's Son, it must follow that the Son...

Against this, he has Peter say not only that Simon should show how that is possible, since Christ did not tell this to his disciples; but he also adds: it belongs to the Father not to be born, but to the Son that he is born. However, the begotten cannot be compared with the unbegotten at all. — He who is identical to no one in all respects cannot (as Simon had just objected) share the same names with someone. — The former (the Father) is begotten of himself or unbegotten; the begotten (the Son) cannot be named as the former is, even if the begotten were of one and the same essence with the unbegotten.

He says further that human souls also originate from God and were of the same essence, but were nonetheless not called gods for that reason. But even if one wanted to call these gods, what great thing would it then be to also call Christ God, who had what everyone else had? We call him God, he says, to whom belongs that which cannot belong to others. Thus, God is immeasurable because He is limitless, and no other can be immeasurable to that degree (27).

The author, as one can see, is devoted to the <term>Emanation system</term> <note>A philosophical theory where all things flow out or "emanate" from a primary divine source. *Cotelerius* <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier (1629–1686), the French scholar previously mentioned.</note> finds in this passage traces of the opinions of the *Ebionites* <note>A Jewish-Christian movement that emphasized the humanity of Jesus and the necessity of following Jewish law.</note> and appeals to the testimony of *Epiphanius* <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403), a bishop known for his "Panarion," a work refuting various heresies.</note>, where indeed several things agreeing with this occur (31): but it is certain that these are not so much the ideas of the *Ebionites*, but rather of those Jewish-Christians who combined Eastern philosophy with the teachings of Christianity; for it is clear from *Epiphanius* himself that he confuses the doctrines of the *Sampsaeans* <note>A group associated with the Elcesaites who practiced ritual washings.</note> and *Elcesaites* <note>A Jewish-Christian sect that followed the "Book of Elchasai."</note> with those of the *Ebionites*. I have already shown previously that *Methodius* <note>Methodius of Olympus (died c. 311 AD), an early Christian author who often argued against Origen.</note> held the same thought, and that it—like the opinion of *Apollinaris* <note>Apollinaris of Laodicea (c. 310–390 AD), who taught that Christ had a human body but a divine mind.</note> that Christ was already clothed in a human, though heavenly, body before his birth from Mary—takes its origin from the doctrine of the Kabbalists regarding *Adam Kadmon* <note>In Jewish mysticism, the "Primordial Man," the first spiritual reflection of the Divine.</note>. *Methodius* goes so far as to say that Adam was not only the type and the image of Christ, but was Christ himself.

Another remarkable concept, and one which, as it appears, does not fit with the former at all, is that the author speaks of two great leaders, to one of whom this world fell and to the other that world, the former being called the left (*) and the latter the right. That he is speaking of Christ and the Devil is evident from the context of these passages (37).

The exact opposite of the *Recognitions* and *Homilies* is the *Epitome* attached to them, which not only contains nothing of all these strange ideas about the person of Christ, but also presents the orthodox doctrine of the Church throughout (39). To these writings may also rightly be added the so-called Apostolic Constitutions and Canons. Very frequently in the Constitutions, the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit are mentioned together, not only where baptism is the subject, but also in the prescribed prayers (40). But despite this, the discussion here is about anything but the kind of Trinity that was later viewed as the universal and orthodox doctrine of the Church. The author contradicts both those who held Christ to be a mere human, as well as those who denied his human nature and viewed him as one and the same as the Father and the Holy Spirit (41) <note>This refers to the heresy of Modalism or Sabellianism, which taught that Father, Son, and Spirit were just different "modes" or masks of a single person.</note>. Yet his ideas diverge widely from the later Nicene views <note>The Council of Nicaea in 325 AD established that the Son is "of the same substance" as the Father, which the author of this text seems to avoid.</note>.

The doctrine of the sole and highest **Monarchy** <term>Monarchy: in early theology, this refers to the "Monarchia," the principle that there is only one ultimate source or "sole rule" in the Godhead, usually identified with the Father</term> of the Father remains always the main principle in the author's system. You have, he says, left the **foolishness of polytheism**, and have turned through Jesus Christ to the **true Monarchy**, to the almighty God. Furthermore: we make it known to you that the almighty God is only one, outside of whom there is no other, and that He alone must be revered and worshipped through Jesus Christ our Lord in the holy

<meta>This page continues the examination of the "Apostolic Constitutions," specifically analyzing the divine titles attributed to the Son and how the author's Christology compares to later Athanasian (Nicene) orthodoxy versus Arianism.</meta>

# Attempt at a History
### of Arianism.

the judge, the lawgiver, the angel of the Father, the only-begotten God, God the Word. Indeed, the author says the same of him as he otherwise says of the Father: that he is the King, Lord, and God of all nature, whether it be intellectual <note>In this context, "intellectual" refers to the spiritual or invisible realm of existence.</note> or sensual <note>Meaning "sensible," or the physical world perceptible to the senses.</note>, who is before all things, and through whom all things exist (44).

Nowhere does the author say of Christ that he was created or brought forth out of nothing; nor does he have him come into existence only at the moment of the creation of things. Rather, he says that he is the Christ of God, who was begotten of the Father before all eternities, the only-begotten Son, the Word-God (45). In the latter of the two passages cited below, the word was even *created* <note>original: "ἐκτίσθη" (ektisthē)</note>, not "made," which would directly contradict the Arian concept <note>Arians typically argued that the Son was a "creature" (ktisma) made from nothing, whereas this text suggests a more complex origin.</note>. However, Cotelerius <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier (1629–1686), a French scholar who edited the works of the Apostolic Fathers.</note>, who did not find this expression in other manuscripts, has already declared it to be an interpolation.

But despite all these grand expressions and frequent affirmations of the divinity of the Son, the author's ideas on this matter still diverge widely from the later Athanasian views <note>Referring to the followers of Athanasius, who championed the "consubstantial" (same substance) view of the Trinity at the Council of Nicaea.</note>. Regarding the origin of the intellectual world, he certainly does not express himself clearly; it is, however, probable that he adhered to the system of emanation <note>A philosophical view where all things flow out from a central divine source.</note>, and that the existence

to be <note>continuing from "the existence" on the previous page</note> of the intelligences <note>Spiritual beings or minds.</note> was derived from the essence of the Father no less than the existence of the Son. Consequently, he gave Christ only the essential advantage over the angels and spirits that his existence is immediate, while theirs is mediate—namely, through the Son. You, it says, are by nature the first, the law of the existence of all things, and you surpass all. You have brought all things from non-being into existence through your only-begotten Son. But him you begot in an immediate manner according to your will, power, and goodness, before all eternities, as the only-begotten Son, God the Word, the living Wisdom, the firstborn of all creation (46). The advantage that the Son has over the other Aeons <term>Aeons: in early Christian theology, these are divine beings or ages that emanate from God.</term> according to this view rests not in a difference of essence, but partly in his primogeniture, and partly in that he has his existence from the Father in an immediate way, whereas all others were first brought forth through him.

One may think of this idea as one wishes; yet it is certain that it had already been common among many Christian teachers for a long time. For Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), a historian and bishop who held "Subordinationist" views, placing the Son below the Father.</note> says according to this same view: the Holy Spirit, the Comforter, is neither God nor Son: for he does not have his existence from the Father in the same way as the Son, but belongs among the things that were made through the Son. the mysteries handed down by the divine voice (47).

Furthermore, the existence of the Son is not necessary, such that the Father could never be conceived of without the Son; rather, although he was brought forth before all eternity, this bringing forth nevertheless rested upon the free will of the Father and was a work of his will, his power, and his goodness. Thus, just as the Son owes his existence to the Father, the latter is also the Son's head and God. The "head of Christ," says the author, is his God and Father. And in the prayer for the Catechumens <note>Individuals receiving instruction in the Christian faith before baptism.</note> it says: "Almighty, unbegotten, and inaccessible God, you who alone are the true God, the God and Father of Christ your only-begotten Son, the God of the Paraclete <note>original: "Parakletus"; referring to the Holy Spirit as the Helper or Comforter.</note> and Lord of all things" (48). In this regard, it is also indisputable that the Son is, in turn, called the God and Lord of all visible and invisible things—and especially in the *Constitutions* he is called "our God"—because all things were called into existence through him, just as he in turn owes his existence to the Father, who is his God.

This last comparison of the Holy Spirit with the deaconess likely has its basis in the notion, which already existed among the Nazarenes <note>An early group of Jewish Christians who maintained Jewish law; in Hebrew, the word for Spirit, "Ruach," is feminine.</note>, that the Holy Spirit is of a feminine nature. With this author, it appears that he was also devoted to the same opinion regarding two masters—to one of whom the present world was entrusted, and to the other the future world—which I have already cited previously from Lactantius <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author and advisor to Constantine I.</note> and the author of the *Homilies* <note>Referring to the pseudo-Clementine *Homilies*, an early Christian work.</note>.

### Conclusion.

These are the various conceptions that prevailed among Christian teachers across their different factions concerning the person of Christ, from the beginning of Christianity until the fourth century. They had their basis partly in the former religious concepts of the new Christians; partly in the various philosophies to which the Christian teachers were devoted and which they connected with Christianity; and they owe their existence partly to the zeal for the doctrine of the Monarchy <term>Monarchy: In early theology, "Monarchianism" was the emphasis on the "sole rule" or unity of God, often leading to debates about how the Son and Spirit fit into that unity without creating "three gods."</term>, and partly to the efforts to oppose those in error with emphasis. However, the lack of precision in theological expression—which only attained greater accuracy over time—played no less a part; many expressions used by "heretics" <note>original: "Irrlehrer"; literally "teachers of error."</note> are found among the "orthodox" <note>original: "rechtgläubigen"; literally "right-believing."</note> teachers of the church, without one being able to claim with certainty that the latter associated the same concepts with them as the former.

But the more the spirit of speculation <note>In this context, "speculation" refers to the application of philosophical reasoning to divine mysteries.</note> moved from the philosophical schools into Christian society, and the more it was counted among the essential qualities of a perfect Christian, and especially—

---

<page-num>185</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
<sig>M 5</sig>
<meta>Catchword: merely</meta>

—namely of a teacher of the church, to penetrate deeply into these mysteries and to know and say more and with more precision about the Godhead than was revealed in the Holy Books, the more these various opinions increased.

One would be very much mistaken to claim that people remained completely indifferent regarding these various conceptions. Two among these different opinions seem to have been held as equally erroneous and equally hated from the very beginning: first, the opinion of those who held Christ to be a mere human being; and next, the Gnostic conceptions, according to which the true humanity of Christ was denied. Both were already contested in the earliest writings, although the external situation of the Christians in the first ages prevented them from proceeding against those who thought differently in the same manner as they did later.

However, as long as there was agreement regarding this truth—that "Christ is God and true man"—people seem to have been fairly indifferent regarding specific conceptions of the Lord's divine nature: its origin, rank, dignity, power, and relationship to the Father and the Holy Spirit. This is shown by the diverse and singular opinions encountered on this subject among the Church Fathers.

Over time, this indulgence also became more restricted, as Sabellius <note>A 3rd-century priest who taught that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were merely different "modes" or faces of one God, rather than distinct persons.</note> and others called the distinct personality of the Son into doubt. Whether they were...

### I.

## Concerning Arius and Alexander, Bishop of Alexandria, and the Beginning of Their Disputes.

When one examines the various opinions that have prevailed from the beginning among Christians regarding the person of Christ <note>The "person of Christ" refers to the theological study of Jesus's nature—specifically how his divinity relates to his humanity and to God the Father.</note>, and contrasts this with the behavior of Christians in regard to those same opinions; it is indeed very strange that a dispute of the kind that arose here between Arius, a presbyter <term>presbyter: a senior member or priest of the local church who served under the authority of a bishop</term> at Alexandria, and his bishop Alexander, could become so important, have such harmful consequences for both the Church and the State, and be carried on through so many centuries, as actually occurred.

Much of the cause for this lay in the external situation of the Christian society itself at that time. For some time, people had already begun to be not so forbear- <note>The German word "nachsichtig" (lenient or forbearing) is split here; "nach-" appears at the end of this page, while the catchword "sichtig" indicates the start of the next.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the beginning of the Arian controversy in Alexandria, questioning how a local dispute between a priest and his bishop could escalate into a centuries-long conflict affecting both church and empire.</summary>

<meta>catchword: it [es],</meta>

[forbear-]ing <note>The word "nachsichtig" (lenient/forbearing) began on the previous page.</note> in regard to the specific and more detailed conceptions of the person of Christ than in earlier times. The Christian religion had also recently become the dominant religion in the Roman Empire—or at least the one in whose favor the sovereign <note>Referring to Emperor Constantine the Great.</note> had declared himself—and the prestige of the bishops had increased immensely, their revenues were augmented, and their power within the Church had grown significantly. All of this contributed not a little toward making this dispute (which at another time and under different circumstances would have been considered quite trivial) into exactly what it eventually became.

In the course of time, even the Arian disputes could be viewed as political affairs. Meanwhile, it cannot be denied that the character and personal conduct of these two men <note>Arius and Bishop Alexander.</note> played a significant role in the vehemence and obstinacy of this conflict.

One discovers a man who is stubbornly preoccupied with his own opinion, who has enough pride to demand of others that they think as he does, and who, if this does not happen, becomes angry—a man whose wrath knows no bounds, and in general a man full of passions. <term>Socrates</term> <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380–439 AD), a church historian who aimed for objectivity.</note> himself says that <term>Alexander</term>, when he saw and heard that <term>Arius’s</term> opinion was receiving approval from others, became very angry. Even <term>Sozomen</term>, who seeks to defend <term>Alexander</term>, lets it slip that he ordered <term>Arius</term> to abandon his opinion and to think as he did. The clearest proof of his vehement temperament, however, is provided by the letters themselves that were written by him on this occasion. This vehemence has undoubtedly earned him the name of a champion of evangelical <term>dogmas</term> <note>original: "Lehrsätze"</note> and a protector of apostolic doctrine, which <term>Theodoret</term> <note>Theodoret of Cyrrhus, a theologian and historian of the 5th century.</note> gives him (7).

From the way he defends his opinion, one does not form the best ideas about his scholarship. But <term>Arius’s</term> <term>exegesis</term> <note>The critical interpretation of religious texts, specifically the Bible.</note> would likely not have been much better than that of his bishop. The mildest judgment one can make remains this: that both were fairly similar to each other, and that these unfortunate quarrels would never have disrupted the church if the dispute had been conducted by men with fewer passions and more moderate principles. Such were the natures of the two men between whom these disputes began.

Before these disputes broke out, Arius was already involved in other affairs and had been excluded from church communion for this reason. These were the disturbances that arose under Bishop <term>Peter of Alexandria</term> <note>Bishop of Alexandria from 300–311 AD, martyred during the Diocletianic Persecution.</note> with <term>Meletius</term> <note>Meletius of Lycopolis, the initiator of the Meletian schism.</note>, Bishop of <term>Lycopolis</term> in the <term>Thebaid</term> <note>A region of ancient Egypt, named after its capital, Thebes.</note>.

The occasion for this was the persecution under the Emperor <term>Diocletian</term>, and the entire dispute concerned nothing regarding faith, but rather church discipline and laws. However, it is uncommonly difficult to determine with certainty what gave rise to this separation. For <term>Athanasius</term> blames Meletius, claiming that during the persecution he denied Christ and sacrificed to idols. <term>Socrates</term> <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian.</note> says the same. <term>Sozomen</term> and <term>Theodoret</term> say nothing of this, but only report that he ordained without authority. This is also stated in the letter of the four Egyptian bishops published by <term>Maffei</term>. <term>Epiphanius</term>, on the other hand, even makes Meletius out to be a <term>confessor</term> <note>A title for someone who suffered for the Christian faith but was not martyred.</note>, and places the cause of the separation between him and Peter in the fact that the latter showed himself more lenient toward the <term>lapsed</term> <note>Christians who had renounced their faith or performed pagan sacrifices under pressure during persecutions.</note> than Meletius; but he also reports at the same time what Sozomen, Theodoret, and the four bishops say in their letter: that Meletius ordained bishops, elders, and deacons in various places.

<margin>(8) Athanasius, *Apology against the Arians*, number 59. Socrates, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 6. Sozomen, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book...</margin>
If one weighs these reports against each other, one cannot help but prefer the accounts of Epiphanius, as some recent scholars have done (8). In short, Mele-

<margin>Book I, chapter 24. Theodoret, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 9. Epiphanius, *Against Heresies*, LXVIII, number I. Maffei, *Literary Observations*, volume G, page 28. Dr. Walch’s *History of Heresies*, Part 4, pages 370-379.</margin>
Meletius was deposed from his office and placed under the <term>ban</term> <note>Excommunication or formal exclusion from the church.</note> by the Bishop of Alexandria, Peter. Meletius, however, did not heed this, and since he had possessed an uncommonly large following of bishops, elders, monks, and people of all ranks from the beginning of his disputes with the Bishop of Alexandria, a formal schism now arose in the Egyptian church. Athanasius and Socrates say that Meletius and his followers slandered Bishop Peter and his successors. This is quite possible. But it is also certain that Peter’s vehemence contributed not a little to this. For Peter went so far in his zeal that he even declared the baptism of the Meletians to be invalid (9). And Athanasius, who otherwise only calls them <term>schismatics</term>, insists that they should not be called Christians, but Meletians.

<margin>(9) Sozomen, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 15.</margin>
This separation arose in the first six years of this century, most likely in the year 306, and Arius was also among those who sided with the party of Meletius.

Whether Arius joined this party to make his fortune in it, as he has been accused of doing, is very uncertain. Very many upright men were devoted to it, and Epiphanius even says that

He did not remain on the side of the Meletians <note>Followers of Meletius of Lycopolis, who favored strict discipline against Christians who had compromised during Roman persecutions.</note> for long, however, but submitted to Bishop Peter, who also ordained him as a deacon. But this also did not last long. Arius fell out with his bishop and was excluded from the communion of the church by him. <term>Maimbourg</term> <note>Louis Maimbourg (1610–1686), a Jesuit historian known for his histories of Arianism and the Crusades.</note> says that Arius left the Meletians because he did not find his account satisfied by them, and returned to them again when he saw that their party was increasing. But this is without foundation. <term>Sozomen</term> <note>A 5th-century historian of the early Christian Church.</note> says nothing of what moved Arius to withdraw from the Meletians; he only says that as a restless man, he criticized the behavior of his bishop for excommunicating the followers of Meletius and declaring their baptism invalid (10). and even here, the violence of Peter might be more to blame than the behavior of Arius.

If the report found in the *Acts of Saint Peter* is well-founded—that <term>Achillas</term> and <term>Alexander</term> <note>Successive Bishops of Alexandria after Peter.</note> pleaded for Arius, but Peter declared in response that he could not readmit the traitor, for Christ had appeared to him and shown him

One can easily expect that the accounts of the beginning and the cause of these disturbances must turn out very differently. But that does not prevent one from recognizing the truth, and in each of these various accounts lies at least a ray of truth that can be united with the others.

In all these accounts, there is nothing that is not very probable. Since Arius served as a <term>presbyter</term> <note>From the Greek "presbyteros," meaning elder; a rank of ministry below the bishop.</note> at a church and had the task of interpreting the Holy Scriptures, it was not surprising that he presented his opinions on the person of the Lord. The same ideas had also been held and presented by others. Furthermore, it is not surprising that this man found approval and followers for his opinions. This matter had not yet been brought into a clear light. Even Alexander, if one wishes to believe Sozomen, initially considered this doctrine a doubtful and disputed matter about which one could think one way or another; he wavered in the beginning and gave approval now to this side, now to that, until he finally declared himself for the opinion of those who held the Son to be <term>consubstantial</term> <note>From the Greek "homoousios," meaning of the same substance or essence as the Father.</note> (16). # Attempt at a History
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: be.</meta>

it is unbelievable that an elder from the Church of Baucalis <note>The Baucalis church was one of the oldest and most influential parishes in Alexandria, where Arius served as a priest.</note> took part in these disputes and provoked Alexander to anger, or that Meletius sought to defile a man who had left his party together with his bishop.

All this may remain as it is, and yet the account given by <term>Socrates</term> <note>Socrates Scholastikos (c. 380–439 AD), a historian who wrote a continuation of Eusebius's church history.</note> regarding the origin of the Arian disputes—which has received the most approval even in modern times—can still be true. This account is as follows: Alexander, he says, was teaching one day in the presence of the elders and other members of the clergy in a philosophical manner, with an ambitious spirit, concerning the Holy Trinity, and asserted that there was a Unity in the Trinity. Arius, one of the elders under his authority—a man who was not unpracticed in <term>dialectic</term> <note>The art of logical argument and debate.</note> and who believed his bishop intended to introduce the doctrine of <term>Sabellius</term> <note>Sabellianism taught that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were not distinct persons, but merely different "modes" or masks of a single divine person.</note>—stepped forward in the heat of the dispute to the opposite opinion. He vehemently opposed what the bishop had said, concluding thus: "If the Father begat a Son, then the one who was begotten had a beginning to his existence; from this it is evident that there was a time when the Son was not. It follows necessarily from this that the Son derives his existence from nothingness (17)."

If one wished to assemble a complete picture from these various presentations, it would be this:

---

(17) <note>original: "Socrat. Hist. eccles. Lib. I. c. 5. p. 9."</note> Socrates, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 5, page 9.

<column-break/>

<page-num>201</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
<sig>N 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: After</meta>

be. Arius held the conception of the Son that there was a time when he did not yet exist, and that he was called into being out of nothingness. This is a conception I shall speak of shortly. Arius presented this opinion. This was not yet a crime, nor was it an innovation. It had likewise been presented by other teachers of the church, and <term>Tertullian's</term> <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220 AD) from Carthage.</note> expression—that there was a time when the Father was not yet Father—is not much better than that of Arius, who said there was a time when the Son was not. In Alexandria, where such matters had long been debated using philosophy, this could not have been particularly striking. Many held this opinion, and now that Arius presented it, it received even greater approval in Alexandria. Alexander himself initially viewed this whole question as one about which one might think one way or another. But there were others who did not think so. Did Meletius, or a presbyter of the same church where Arius served, first provide the occasion for the conflict? Regardless, there was no lack of people who philosophized differently about the person of the Son and who criticized the leniency of Alexander, who himself could not yet decide which side he should join.

The reason for these differing views lay undoubtedly in the different philosophical principles of both parties. The opponents of Arius undoubtedly favored the doctrine of the <term>Platonists</term> <note>Followers of Plato whose philosophy influenced early Christian thought on the nature of the soul and the divine.</note> and Eastern philosophers, which had many followers in Egypt, concerning divine <term>emanations</term> <note>The idea that the Son and Spirit "flowed out" from the essence of God the Father, like light from a candle.</note>; they believed, therefore, that the Son and the Holy Spirit had their origin in the essence of the Father.

<page-num>202</page-num>
# Attempt at a History
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: One</meta>

According to these principles, there was never a time when the Son did not exist, whether he was considered the internal Word or the Word that had proceeded forth <note>Theologians often distinguished between the "Logos endiathetos" (the word within God’s mind) and the "Logos prophorikos" (the word spoken aloud or brought forth for creation).</note>; instead, he was begotten from the very essence of the Father. While all other intelligences of the spirit-world shared a similar origin—being Words, <term>Sephiroth</term> <note>In Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah), these are the ten attributes or emanations through which the Infinite reveals itself; the author is drawing a parallel between these and early Christian concepts of divine emanations.</note>, numbers, and emanations of the Godhead—they were so only in an indirect way through the Son, and possessed far less perfection. Arius, by contrast, believed in the production of all things out of nothing. His expression, that the Son has his existence <term>from things that are not</term> <note>original: "ἐξ οὐκ ὄντων" (ex ouk ontōn). This is the Greek technical term for "out of nothing," the core of Arius's argument that the Son was a created being.</note>, did not, of course, encompass everything that was attributed to it. Tertullian says: that if God brought forth the world from a pre-existing matter, one could still say it was made "out of nothing," because things were not yet what they later became. Methodius and Origen judge in the same way (18). Nevertheless, his system was directly opposed to the emanation system, and there had always been a time when the Son had not yet existed.

Perhaps Arius’s desire to oppose erroneous opinions played no small part in his conception. At least Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380–439 AD), an influential church historian.</note> says that Arius viewed the opposing opinion as <term>Sabellian</term> <note>Followers of Sabellius taught that God was a single person who simply wore different "masks" or modes as Father, Son, and Spirit, rather than being three distinct persons.</note> (19).

---

(18) Tertullian, *Against Marcion*, Book II, ch. 5. Methodius in Photius’s *Library*, codex 236, p. 499. Origen in the *Philocalia*, ch. 24.

(19) Socrates, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, ch. 5.

<column-break/>

<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
<meta>catchword: One</meta>

One can also, with good reason, include the <term>Manichaeans</term> <note>A major religion founded by the prophet Mani, teaching a dualistic struggle between a good world of light and an evil world of darkness.</note> and <term>Valentinians</term> <note>Followers of Valentinus, a Gnostic teacher who proposed a complex system of divine emanations called "Aeons."</note> here, the latter of whom had a large following in Egypt. Although the orthodox, in adopting the emanation system, certainly departed far from the Gnostic parties—as Hilary says (20)—the Arians always believed they found a correspondence between their opponents' ideas and the principles of these heretics. Therefore, they declared that they did not, like Valentinus, consider the Son a <term>probole</term> <note>A "projection" or "emission" of the divine substance.</note> of the Father, nor did they, like Mani, consider him a <term>consubstantial</term> <note>Of the same substance or essence; "homoousios."</note> part of the Father.

As indulgent or indifferent as Alexander had been at first, he showed himself just as zealous afterward, once he had taken a side. In a meeting of the Alexandrian clergy, he presented his opinion on the origin of the Son in a proud manner and philosophized about the unity of essence within the Trinity. Arius contradicted this opinion as one that led to the error of Sabellius, and in response, he presented his own in an equally philosophical manner. Alexander now used his authority as Bishop and commanded the <term>presbyter</term> <note>A priest or elder; in this case, Arius himself.</note> to renounce his opinion and instead adopt his own. This led to heated disputes; and since he could not persuade Arius—and many bishops and others among the clergy held that Arius's opinion was correct—Alexander excluded him and his followers from the communion of the church (21).

# Attempt at a History
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: he</meta>

<margin>X</margin>
Arius has been accused of inciting this schism out of envy over Alexander's elevation to the episcopal dignity, a position for which he had reportedly held hope (22). This is an accusation that has been made against several heretics. Philostorgius <note>An Arian church historian whose work survives mainly in summaries by others; he naturally presents Arius in a more favorable light.</note>, on the contrary, asserts that Arius had the majority of votes for himself during the episcopal election, but out of humility, he directed matters so that the choice fell upon Alexander (23). Little trust is to be placed in the reports of either side. What can be presumed with good reason is this: that Arius, too proud of his philosophical insights, stubbornly maintained his opinion—likely also out of regard for the following he already had in Alexandria—and that Alexander, displeased by this very following of a presbyter <note>A "presbyter" is a leader or elder in the early church, roughly equivalent to a priest today. Alexander was the Bishop, making him Arius’s superior.</note> and with too much preoccupation for his episcopal authority, ordered him to renounce his opinion. When this did not happen, Alexander excluded him and his friends from the community.

At this stage, the dispute did not yet involve all the matters that later became contentious between the Arians and the Orthodox, but only the single question: whether the Son is as eternal as the Father or not, and whether he was brought forth from the essence of the Father or out of nothing? For in other respects, Alexander did not deviate far at all from the conceptions of the later Semi-Arians <note>A middle-ground group that tried to find a compromise between the "same substance" (homoousios) of the Orthodox and the "different substance" of the Arians.</note>. The Son, he himself says, is lesser than the Father in that

he is not unbegotten, as the Lord himself taught when he says: *the Father is greater than I.* — There is a great difference between the unbegotten Father and the things created by him out of nothing. Between the former and the latter, however, stands the only-begotten nature in the middle, through which the Father of the divine Word made all things out of nothing, but which itself was begotten by the Father.

<margin>Year of Christ 319. 320.</margin>
That was the occasion for the unfortunate schism, which arose approximately in the years 319 or 320, and which brought about the consequences of which I shall soon speak.

## II.

### History of Arius and his followers until his expulsion from Alexandria.

Alexander and Arius, in that conversation I last mentioned, had diverged too far in their ideas for any agreement to be considered. Furthermore, their respective temperaments led them even further apart than might otherwise have happened with somewhat gentler dispositions. Each had followers of his opinions, and each considered it necessary to increase their number.

<meta>Continuity: The previous page described how Bishop Alexander of Alexandria excluded Arius and his followers from the church community after Arius refused to renounce his views on the nature of Christ’s divinity. This page explores how both sides began to seek support from the wider Christian world.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>
# Attempt at a History
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

From a fragment of a speech by *Alexander* to the clergy of *Alexandria* and *Mareotis*, one sees that *Alexander* and his followers wrote to *Arius* and his party, exhorting them to abandon their errors and return to the faith of the Catholic <note>In this historical context, "Catholic" refers to the "universal" or "orthodox" church, rather than the specific Roman Catholic denomination of today.</note> Church (24). Since this letter is no longer extant, its arguments cannot be judged. Suffice it to say, it was fruitless. *Arius* remained firm in his opinion and sought to gain more and more followers for it—an effort that *Alexander*, for his part, did not neglect. *Sozomen* <note>A 5th-century Christian historian whose *Ecclesiastical History* is a major source for this period.</note> says that among the presbyters of the Alexandrian church, *Aethales*, *Achillas*, *Karponas*, *Sarmatas*, and another *Arius* stood with *Arius*. Among the deacons were *Euzojus*, *Macarius*, *Julius*, *Menas*, and *Helladius*. Likewise, several bishops and other liturgical personnel, as well as not a few of the common people, supported him (25).

Both parties soon realized that these disputes would not long remain within the borders of *Alexandria*, and they therefore sought to win over foreign bishops as well. *Socrates* <note>Socrates Scholasticus, another 5th-century historian who documented the Arian controversy.</note> says that *Arius* found many friends for his opinions in *Egypt*, *Libya*, and the *Thebaid*, and that *Eusebius* of *Nicomedia* <note>One of the most influential bishops of the era and a key supporter of Arius.</note> especially declared himself for them. *Sozomen* says that *Arius* and his followers sought to earn the favor of other bishops, sent delegates to them, and provided them with a confession of faith, requesting that the bishops, if—

---

(24) <note>original: *Cotelerii Patres Apost. Tom. I. p. 410. not. 92.*</note> *Cotelerius, Apostolic Fathers, Vol. I, p. 410, note 92.*
(25) <note>original: *Sozomen. Hist. eccles. Lib. I. cap. 15.*</note> *Sozomen, Church History, Book I, chapter 15.*

<meta>catchword: if</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>207</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

if they approved of their views, they might write to Bishop *Alexander* and persuade him not to be angry with them any longer; but if they thought otherwise, they should instruct them on how they ought to think. *Sozomen* says that this drew very many over to the party of *Arius* (26). Similar efforts were also applied by *Alexander*, who wrote not only to various bishops of *Egypt*, *Libya*, and the *Thebaid*, but also to *Syria*, *Lycia*, *Pamphylia*, *Asia*, *Cappadocia*, and other regions, recommending his cause; he was fortunate enough that several declared for him (27).

Matters did not stop at these attempts to disparage one another through letters to outsiders. From a letter by *Alexander* to Bishop *Alexander* of *Constantinople*, one sees that the friends of *Arius* held special gatherings; for he says that they no longer wish to submit to the Church, but have come together in "dens of thieves" <note>original: "Räuberhölen." This is a derogatory biblical reference Alexander used to describe the unauthorized worship spaces of the Arians.</note> which they established for themselves. Although *Alexander* counts this among the reasons that moved him to exclude them from the Church, it seems that this only occurred later. Beyond this, however, he complains that *Arius* and his followers have stirred up riots and persecutions against him, and have drawn women of a disorderly lifestyle <note>A common rhetorical tactic in early church polemics was to accuse the opposing side of being influenced by "disorderly" or "silly" women.</note> into their interest, who have run about everywhere, and—

# Attempt at a History
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

<margin>X</margin>
...slandered Alexander and those who stood with him. Alexander does not say how he and his own followers conducted themselves. From the vehemence prevailing in his letter, it can be concluded with much probability that they, too, did not behave as lambs. Suffice it to say, both parties sought to cause each other as much disadvantage in Alexandria as was possible, and to strengthen their factions while also obtaining friends and adherents among foreign bishops and congregations.

<margin>X</margin>
When Alexander saw that his vehemence was answered with equal vehemence from the opposing party, and that the number of Arius’s followers not only increased daily in Alexandria but also gained friends and protectors among foreign bishops—who were men of learning and piety—he became extremely incensed (28). Among these, *Eusebius*, Bishop of *Nicomedia* <note>An influential bishop who was a distant relative of the Imperial family and a key supporter of Arius.</note> (where the Emperor was staying at the time), was one of the most prominent; he was a learned man who stood in high regard at the imperial court. Alexander took the rapid step of organizing a meeting of nearly one hundred bishops from *Egypt* and *Libya* <margin>☉ Year of Christ 321.</margin> in the year 321 in *Alexandria*, so that this matter might be judged. The specific circumstances and proceedings of this council are not known. However, it appears neither that Alexander appeared there as a plaintiff, nor that the bishops were regarded as judges over him and Arius,

(28) <note>original: "Socratis Hist. ecclef. Lib. I. cap. 6. Sozomenus Hist. ecclef. Lib. I. cap. 15." — References to the *Ecclesiastical Histories* of Socrates Scholasticus (Book 1, Chapter 6) and Sozomen (Book 1, Chapter 15).</note>
