<page-num>209</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
**Second Part. D**

nor that all the bishops present at the meeting were of Alexander's opinion. From his letter to *Alexander of Constantinople*, the opposite is rather evident (29).

The outcome of this assembly was that the opinion of *Arius* and his followers was condemned as heretical, they were excluded from the communion of the church, and *Arius* was removed from his dignity within the church (30).

This harsh procedure was not, in itself, capable of quieting this unfortunate dispute, which had already progressed so far that—as *Socrates* says—people sought to make Christianity ridiculous on the public stages <note>The author refers to the fact that the theological disputes became so widespread that they were even mocked in secular theaters.</note>. In this, the loss was undoubtedly greater than the gain would have been, even if the disputed question could have been determined with the greatest certainty. But through this very thoughtless and violent step, the evil was undoubtedly bound to spread even further, since many bishops of prestige, learning, and piety had joined the opinion of *Arius*, and were now struck by the sentence of condemnation along with him.

However, what had not yet been completed by this side was finished by the harsh letters which *Alexander* sent immediately afterward to all foreign bishops, in which he communicated the conclusions of this council...

made [the conflict] known. Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380 – after 439), a Greek church historian whose "Ecclesiastical History" is a primary source for the Arian controversy.</note>, who is not suspected of any partiality toward the Arian faction, says himself that Alexander made the matter worse through his letters and further ignited the strife. By doing so, the bishops of other regions were forced to take sides, whereby everything was filled with riot and confusion (31).

The first of these letters is a circular addressed to all bishops of the Catholic <note>In this context, "Catholic" refers to the "universal" or "orthodox" church before the great schisms.</note> Church. The introduction makes a general point about the unity of the church, which requires that all churches take part in the fortunate and adverse fates of others. From there, he immediately comes to the misfortune that has struck the Alexandrian church through godless people, who are enemies of Christ and forerunners of the Antichrist. He then declares that he would have shrouded the entire matter in the deepest silence; however, Eusebius of Nicomedia had taken up the cause of the apostates and recommended them to others. On this occasion, Eusebius receives his share [of criticism], because he exchanged his diocese of Berytus <note>Modern-day Beirut.</note> for that of Nicomedia, and is simultaneously depicted as one who seeks nothing less than to arrange the affairs of the church according to his own whim and will, and to ensnare ignorant people in heresy. He also speaks of former ill-will on the part of Eusebius, from which one can surmise that there had already been disagreements between these two bishops in the past. He then lists the names of the apostates, namely: Arius, Achillas, Aithales, Karponas, another Arius,

Then he presents the doctrine of Arius and his followers. They teach that God was not always Father, but that there was a time when He was not. The Word of God <note>The "Logos" or Jesus Christ.</note> had not always existed; rather, God, who has always been, made him out of that which is not; he is therefore the creature and work of the Father. He is also not equal to the Father in essence, nor is he by nature the Word of the Father or His true wisdom. He is one of the created things and is called "the Word" and "Wisdom" improperly, since he himself was made by the Word and the Wisdom existing in God, in which God made all things. He is subject to change, something foreign, and distant from the essence of God. The Father is invisible and incomprehensible to the Son, who does not even know his own essence: he was brought forth for our sake; God used him as an instrument to create us, and he would never have been created if God had not wanted to create us, and he could also be changed like the Devil, as a creature of a changeable nature.

Afterward, he says that he held a council with the bishops of Egypt and Libya and excluded Arius and his followers, whom Eusebius, however, had received.

# Attempt at a History

...there was a time when the Son was not yet: for John says: in the beginning was the Word. One could not count the Son among the created beings: for the Scripture calls him the only-begotten Son, and says that all things were created through him. He could not have been brought forth out of nothing: for the Father says: (Psalm 45:1) *my heart has brought forth a good word*, and *from the womb before the morning star have I begotten thee* <note>These Old Testament verses were historically interpreted by the Church Fathers as proof of the eternal generation of Jesus Christ (the Word) from God the Father.</note>. He could not be unlike the Father according to his essence: for he is the image and the radiance of the Father: *he who sees me, sees the Father*. He had always been the wisdom and the understanding of the Father: for otherwise one would have to say that God was previously without wisdom and without understanding. He is also not subject to change: for he says of himself, *I and the Father are one: I am in the Father and the Father is in me. — See me, I am he and am not changed.* — He says hereafter that the preceding heresies could still be justified in comparison with this one, and since Arius and his followers were those of whom Paul prophesied, that in the latter times such people would come who would turn others away from the faith and lead them into the teachings of the devil; they were thus rightly placed under an <term>anathema</term> <note>original: "Anathema"; a formal curse or excommunication by a religious authority, casting one out of the community of believers.</note>. One should therefore not give credence to Eusebius, or any other, nor receive these people, nor even greet them (32).

I do not wish to anticipate the reader’s judgment of this letter. One can entirely disapprove of Arius's notions, and yet one will have to admit, if one is only somewhat familiar with the way of thinking of the most learned Church Fathers of the preceding centuries, that these very opinions attributed here to Arius were not first invented by him.

One can be ever so zealously devoted to the orthodox opinion, and yet one will not be able to deny that Alexander was a poor defender of a good cause. Many consequences that Alexander drew from Arius's teaching were certainly denied by Arius himself. Arius certainly could not accept the consequence that God had been without understanding and wisdom if the Son had not always been the understanding and wisdom of the Father: for as Alexander himself had cited shortly before, Arius maintained that the Son was made through this very Word and Wisdom of God, through which He brought forth all things. The violent attacks against Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Nicomedia, a powerful bishop and supporter of Arius.</note>, however, which occur in this letter, could hardly do otherwise than to spread the fire ever further, which one should have endeavored to extinguish with all one’s might. # Attempt at a History of Arianism

<header>Attempt at a History of Arianism.</header>

<margin>(33) Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century historian of the early Christian church.</note> in the place cited <note>original: "a. a. O." (am angeführten Ort)</note> p. 13.</margin>
among whom was primarily Eusebius of Nicomedia, who felt particularly offended by this letter, and whose example many bishops followed because he held high standing at the imperial court (33).

<margin>(34) Cotelerius’ <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier, a 17th-century French scholar of early Christian texts.</note> note 92 on the *Apostolic Constitutions* <note>A collection of early Christian instructions on doctrine and liturgy.</note> Book VIII, chapter 28.</margin>
It does not seem that Alexander sent this letter out immediately from the Council which he had assembled: for the signatures of the same do not contain the names of the bishops, but rather of the clergy of Alexandria and Mareotis <note>A region in Egypt near Alexandria.</note>. The reasons for this are unknown, just as this part of Arian history is generally very obscure. Suffice it to say, after Arius and his followers were condemned at the Council, Alexander gathered the clergy of Alexandria and Mareotis who were under his authority, made known to them the verdict of the bishops of the Council, and requested from them the signature of this letter, which he then received. From the fragment that exists of this presbyterial synod <note>A meeting of elders or priests, rather than a full gathering of bishops.</note>, one sees that he had them come together so that they would know what had been written and might approve it, and likewise give their consent to the deposition of the elders and deacons who had been removed at the Council because they were from their own ranks (34). Both were achieved, and it is therefore stated in the signature of that letter that they were satisfied with what had been written, and with the deposition of Arius and those who accepted the godless doctrine.

->So<-

<column-break/>

As zealous and effective as Alexander proved himself to be in suppressing his opponents, he nevertheless achieved very little, and the force he applied rather drove them to resist him with even greater strength. They remained in Alexandria, holding their own separate worship services, and thus a formal separation into two parties was the result brought about by the Council's sentence of condemnation. While Alexander and his friends sent delegates and letters to all places and received favorable replies from some bishops; Arius and his followers did the same and also received favorable answers from bishops. However, what made the Arian party particularly formidable to the Bishop of Alexandria was the joining of the so-called Meletians <note>Followers of Meletius of Lycopolis, a group already in schism with the Bishop of Alexandria over the treatment of Christians who had lapsed during Roman persecutions.</note> to their side.

It has not, indeed, been held against the Meletians that they thought the same as Arius regarding doctrine; and perhaps they did not consider the whole matter as important as both parties made it out to be, but it was enough for both the Arians and the Meletians that the Bishop of Alexandria was their opponent. Both therefore joined together to better resist him with united forces.

It is unknown whether Alexander held another council after this first one. But both the approval that the Arians found in Egypt and the neighboring lands, as well as the letters they received from even more distant bishops (which they did not fail to display), and which [increased] their following

<summary>This page describes how Bishop Alexander's attempts to suppress Arianism through local synods and depositions failed to end the conflict. Instead, it led to a formal schism in Alexandria and a strategic alliance between the Arians and the Meletians against Alexander's authority.</summary>

# Attempt at a History

<header>Attempt at a History</header>

...had to increase were things that Alexander could by no means view with indifference.

To this was added the fact that Eusebius of Nicomedia <note>A powerful bishop and a key supporter of Arius; he later baptized Emperor Constantine.</note> actually wrote to Alexander, advising him to suppress the matter and to readmit Arius into the church community. When Alexander likely refused to agree to this, Eusebius also wrote to other bishops, seeking to persuade them not to believe Alexander (35).

The perceptions of Alexander's doctrine that were presented to other bishops—both from this side and from Arius himself—seem to have actually found some acceptance with several. Consequently, Alexander drafted the letter which Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus, a 5th-century theologian and church historian.</note> has preserved for us. According to some editions, it is addressed to Alexander, Bishop of Constantinople (36), but it is more likely a circular letter like the previous one, in which he takes special care to place his opinion in a clearer light.

In this letter, Alexander first portrays himself as a sufferer who is being persecuted by ambitious and greedy people. He warns the other bishops against these men so that they do not sneak into their dioceses in a deceitful manner. These men were Arius and Achillas <note>An Alexandrian deacon and early supporter of Arius.</note>, who had instigated a conspiracy and would no longer submit to the judgment of the church.

<insert>2</insert>

...They held meetings in robber dens <note>original: "Räuberhölen"; a reference to the Biblical "den of thieves."</note> and slandered both Christ and Alexander day and night. They criticized apostolic doctrine, denied the divinity of Christ, and held him to be equal to other men, while also rejecting the words by which His eternal divinity would be proven.

He then complains about the behavior of his opponents: they mocked him, daily incited riot and persecution against him, and had drawn disreputable women who roamed the streets into their interests. Consequently, he had cast them out of the church. However, they continued their actions, running to other bishops who thought as he did under the pretext of establishing peace and harmony, in order to lead them into error. They also demanded letters from these bishops, which they then read to their own followers to strengthen them in their godlessness. They hid what they taught and why they had been expelled behind obscure words, while seeking to disparage Alexander's teaching. In this way, they misled various bishops into receiving them into their community against church laws, thereby strengthening them in their devilish undertakings against Christ.

He then describes the doctrine of the Arians, though in stronger colors than in the previous letter. The Son of God, they claimed, belongs among the rational and irrational things that were brought forth out of nothing. He is also changeable and, by nature, has no advantage over other "sons of God." # Attempt at a History

<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: follow</meta>

...his moral behavior and striving did not change for the worse.

After this, he refutes the dogmas of his opponents piece by piece. That he is the Son of God, and was not brought forth from nothing, he proves from John 1:18 <note>John 1:18: "No one has ever seen God, but the one and only Son, who is himself God and is in closest relationship with the Father, has made him known."</note> and concludes from this that the Father and the Son were two things <note>German: *Sachen*; here referring to distinct persons or realities within the Trinity.</note> that could not be separated.

That the Son does not belong to created things, but is instead their Creator, he proves from John 1:1–2 and also derives from this that the thing made cannot be of one and the same nature as the one who made it. John also indicated the difference by saying of the Son, *he was*, whereas of the creatures, he says that they were made.

Furthermore, *Alexander* counts the essence and the manner of the Son’s existence among those things that surpassed even the understanding of angels, and he considers it impermissible to investigate such things.

That the Son was not brought forth from nothing or in time, he proves by the fact that the phrase "he was not" <note>This refers to the famous Arian slogan: "There was a time when he was not."</note> must have been said in some specific time; but if everything was made before him, then this time would also have been made before him. If the Son had not always existed, the Father would have lacked a part of his perfection, and there would have been a time when he did not have his wisdom. In such a way, one would have to nullify the existence of God himself. If one were to say that the radiance of glory did not exist, then it

---

<header>of Arianism.</header>
<meta>catchword: His</meta>

would follow that the light did not exist, of which the Son is the radiance. From this it also follows that his sonship has nothing in common with that of others; and just as he surpasses all others to whom he has given existence, so also does his sonship—which is according to the nature of the Father’s divinity—surpass the sonship of those who are only adopted as children through him. He is also immutable and perfect, while the latter are changeable and stand in need of him.

He next demonstrates with various passages of Holy Scripture that Christ is an essential Son of God, and is very different from those who are adopted.

Following this, he traces the error of *Arius* back to *Ebion* and *Artemon* <note>Ebion and Artemon were early heretics who generally denied the divinity of Jesus, viewing him as a mere man.</note>, whom *Paul of Samosata* <note>A 3rd-century Bishop of Antioch who taught that Jesus was born a man but became divine.</note> had followed, who in turn had *Lucian* as a successor; he also complains about three Syrian bishops who strengthened *Arius* in his malice through their approval.

He also seeks to prejudice the bishops to whom this letter is addressed against the Arians by describing their vain boastings: that none of them understood the slightest bit, and that they, the Arians, considered themselves alone to be wise.

Finally, he comes to the slanders that were spread by the Arians against him and all those who thought as he did, as if they accepted two unbegotten beings <note>Arian polemics often accused the orthodox party of "Ditheism" (believing in two separate Gods) because they held the Son to be co-eternal and uncreated alongside the Father.</note>.

<summary>Bishop Alexander uses logic and scripture (primarily the Gospel of John) to argue that the Son is the eternal Creator rather than a created being. He links Arianism to earlier heresies like those of Paul of Samosata and criticizes the arrogance and divisiveness of the Arian faction.</summary>

# Attempt at a History

<header>Attempt at a History</header>

His explanation of his position, by contrast, is remarkable. "They do not understand," he says, "what a great difference there is between the unbegotten Father and the things that are brought forth from nothing by Him." — Between these two stands the only-begotten nature in the middle, through which the Father of the Word of God made everything from nothing, but which was begotten from the Father Himself; here he refers to the passage *1 John 5:1*, although it is not cited in the most accurate manner.

Regarding this generation of the Son from the Father, he says that it did not happen in a physical way through division, separation, or outflow <note>original: *Ausfluss*; likely referring to "emanation," a concept in Gnostic philosophy</note>, as *Sabellius* and *Valentinus* taught, but in an entirely inexpressible way.

He also says that through the expressions he uses regarding the Son—*he was always, before all times*—he in no way intended to depict the Son as the Unbegotten: these were only intended to be expressions with which he hoped to make the divinity and, as it were, the age of the Son comprehensible to his listeners. To the Father alone belongs the status of having no author of His existence, but to the Son that He was begotten without beginning by the Father; moreover, *Christ* Himself says of the Father that He is greater than He. Besides the Father and Son, he still confesses the Holy Spirit, who inspired the holy men of the Old and New Testaments.

There follows a kind of <term>Creed</term> <note>original: *Symbolum*; a formal statement of Christian belief.</note>, in which it is remarkable that *Mary* already bears the title of <term>Mother of God</term> <note>original: *Gottesgebärerin*; Greek: *Theotokos*. This term emphasizes that Mary gave birth to God in human form, a central point of later orthodoxy.</note>.

<meta>catchword: This,</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>221</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

This, he says, is his doctrine and that of the apostolic church, and because *Arius* and his followers opposed it, they were cast out of the church.

He begs the bishops not to receive them; they are liars and slanderers whom they should not believe; they disparage Christianity in the public courts and have stirred up all possible persecutions against him. They should believe him, *Alexander*, and, as other bishops have done, sign his <term>Tome</term> <note>original: *Tomus*; an official theological document or letter of decree.</note>. The deacon *Apion* would show them the letters that the bishops from *Egypt, Thebaid, Libya, Pentapolis, Syria, Lycia, Pamphylia, Asia, Cappadocia*, and the neighboring provinces had written to him. This was the only means to bring them to repentance and to the obedience of the church (37).

That was the second letter that *Alexander* sent out in this matter, and which, as anyone can see, was no milder than the first. He calls his opponents furies, madmen, deceivers, and blasphemers who are ruled by a satanic spirit—all of which must have proven enough to anyone with how much passion these matters were being driven.

Whether *Athanasius* drafted these letters, as some
more recent writers wish to claim, is very uncertain. *Theodoret* 
says that *Alexander* also wrote to *Philogonius*,

### III.
### Continuation of these Disputes.

What impact Alexander’s spirited letters had on the bishops to whom they were addressed cannot be determined with certainty. That several of them declared themselves for Alexander is well established, and Epiphanius says that this bishop made a formal collection of the letters from such bishops who had declared themselves in favor of his opinion (38). But it is also certain that many declared themselves to the advantage of Arius, among whom, besides Eusebius of Nicomedia, were also Eusebius of Caesarea, Theodotus of Laodicea, Paulinus of Tyre, Athanasius of Anazarbus, Gregory of Berytus, Aetius of Lydia, Patrophilus of Scythopolis, and many others, who took it upon themselves to restore peace between the contending parties.

This very fact, however—that both sides had their many and great followers—contributed not a little to making them more stubborn and to increasing the evil.

---

(38) Epiphanius, *Against Heresies*, Book 69, number 4.

<column-break/>

<margin>Year of Christ 322.</margin>
In the midst of these unrests, Arius likely left Alexandria in the year 322 and traveled to Palestine. What gave occasion for this is unknown, but it remains proof that both parties proceeded against one another with great vehemence. Athanasius says that Arius fled from Alexandria. Epiphanius, on the other hand, asserts that he was driven out by Alexander, which Arius himself also says in his letter, of which I shall speak shortly (39).

It remains incomprehensible how this was possible according to the political constitution of Alexandria at that time, where the government was still administered by pagans; meanwhile, according to these testimonies, the reality of the story is not to be doubted, whereby—whether Arius fled or was driven out—Alexander and his friends do not appear in a favorable light.

Shortly after this event, Arius wrote a letter to Eusebius of Nicomedia. He says in the same that, as his father Ammonius was traveling to Nicomedia, he had given him this letter to inform him, Eusebius, whose kind sentiments were known, of how violently he was being persecuted by Alexander, who was setting everything in motion against him and had driven him and his followers out of the city as godless <note>original: *Gottlose*; in this context, it refers to heretics or those who do not hold the "correct" view of God's nature</note> because they would not agree with him when he said the Son was...

---

(39) Epiphanius, *Against Heresies*, Book 69, number 3. Theodoret, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 4.

224 Attempt at a History

[The Son] has always existed just as God has, and the Son exists simultaneously with the Father: he was begotten from eternity and is "unbegottenly born" <note>original: *ungeboren geboren*; this paradoxical phrase refers to the doctrine that the Son is eternally generated by the Father without having a beginning in time</note>: God is not ahead of the Son by even a single thought or a single moment: the Son is always God, always the Son, and is from God himself.

He [Arius] reports thereafter that Eusebius of Caesarea, Theodotus, Paulinus, Athanasius, Gregorius, Aetius, and all the Eastern bishops—who asserted that God exists without a beginning before the Son—had been placed under the <term>anathema</term> <note>A formal curse or excommunication by the church</note>, with the exception of Philogonius, Hellanicus, and Macarius. He describes these three as heretical and ignorant people, some of whom considered the Son an "outburst" <note>original: *Ausbruch*</note> of the Father, others a "projection" <note>original: *Hervorstoßung*; likely referring to the Gnostic concept of *probole* or emanation</note> of the same, and others who considered him to be co-unbegotten.

Furthermore, he presents his own opinion: namely, that the Son is neither the Unbegotten, nor a part of the Unbegotten, nor formed from any existing matter. Instead, by the will and counsel of God, he existed before all time as perfect God, the only-begotten, and unchangeable. However, before he was born, or created, or appointed, or established, he did *not* exist: for he is not the Unbegotten. The Son has a beginning, but God has no beginning.

Finally, Arius explains what he means when he says the Son proceeded "from nothing" <note>original Greek: *ἐξ οὐκ ὄντων* (ex ouk onton) — "from things that are not"</note>; namely, that the Son is neither a part of God, nor made from any existing

<meta>catchword: matter</meta>

<column-break/>

225 of Arianism.

<margin>(40) Theodoret, *Church History*, Book I, Chapter 4. Epiphanius, *Heresies*, 69, number 6.</margin>
matter. This, he says, is the reason why they persecuted him (40).

Whether it occurred out of pretense and hypocrisy (which Alexander frequently attributes to his opponent) or for other reasons, one cannot deny that this letter is written with uncommon moderation. No harsh word against his bishop escapes Arius; he merely recounts, without any addition, that the bishop drove him out of the city as one who is godless. He draws no disadvantageous conclusions from Alexander’s teaching and briefly presents his own, like a man who is conscious that there is nothing in his opinion that has not also been accepted by other teachers in the church.

The only thing one might criticize is the attack on the three bishops Philogonius, Hellanicus, and Macarius, and the hint he gives in the letter that all Eastern bishops who asserted that the Father existed without beginning before the Son had been placed under the anathema by Alexander.

It is not surprising, if Arius’s other letters were similar to this one, that various bishops were inclined to favor his side.

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: various</meta>

Whatever one may think of his doctrine and the causes of his behavior, one must confess that he chose a much better path than his opponent, and he achieved his goal. Furthermore, it cannot be denied that many teachers of the Church thought as he did, and finally, the violent proceedings of Alexander could find little approval among people who were not filled with prejudice and were not driven by equally violent passions.

<margin>(41) Sozomen therefore also says that the strict proceedings of Alexander against Arius gained the latter the sympathy and love of many people <note>original: *Sozomeni Hist. eccles. Lib. I. c. 15. p. 32.*</note>.</margin>

Where Arius and his friends went in Palestine is unknown; but Epiphanius <note>A 4th-century bishop known for his massive compendium of heresies</note> says that they went around everywhere and sought to gain the favor and approval of the bishops. That these efforts were not in vain is convincingly proven by the zeal with which the Palestinian bishops took up Arius's cause <note>original: *Epiphanius Haeres. LXIX. num. 4.*</note>.

With the warmest zeal, however, Eusebius of Nicomedia <note>A powerful bishop and a distant relative of the imperial family who became Arius's primary protector</note> championed Arius—whether the cause was that he thought the same as Arius, or the violence with which he had been specifically attacked by Alexander in the first letter; in any case, Eusebius wrote to various

bishops in favor of Arius and also approved of his opinion concerning Christ <note>original: *Socrates Hist. eccles. Lib. I. c. 6. p. 14. Theodoret. Hist. eccles. Lib. I. c. 4. 5.*</note>.

Theodoret <note>A 5th-century historian and theologian</note> has preserved for us a letter from Eusebius to Paulinus, Bishop of Tyre, which was written for this purpose and, in all probability, should be placed in this time.

He says first that the efforts of Eusebius (of Caesarea) <note>The famous church historian and bishop of Caesarea, distinct from Eusebius of Nicomedia</note>, which he had undertaken for the sake of the truth, greatly pleased him; however, he was just as much distressed by the silence of Paulinus. He therefore calls upon him to make similar efforts, as it is wrong to hide one's true opinions.

He then presents his opinion and says that he has never heard that there were two Unbegotten beings, or one who had been divided into two, or who had suffered anything that could only be said of physical things; rather, there was one Unbegotten <note>meaning God the Father, who has no beginning</note> and one who was truly begotten by Him, but not from His essence, and who does not share at all in the unbegotten nature, nor is he from His essence. This one [the Son] is of an entirely different nature, but formed according to the most perfect likeness of his Creator. His beginning can neither be expressed in words nor grasped by the thoughts of men or any beings exalted above men.

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

He then brings forward the reasons for his opinion and seeks to prove from Proverbs of Solomon 8:22 <note>In the 18th century, as in late antiquity, this verse—"The Lord created me at the beginning of his work"—was the primary "proof text" used by Arians to argue that Christ was a created being.</note> that the Son, according to his essence, was created, formed, and begotten, after the likeness of his Creator, and is of an inexpressible and unchangeable nature. However, he argues that the Son is not "from" the Father as a part or an outflowing of Him; otherwise, one could not say that He was made or created, and one would then have to assume that the Son is also unbegotten <note>meaning "without a beginning," a quality Arius believed belonged uniquely to God the Father</note>.

When it is said of the Son, he continues, that he was begotten of the Father, it does not follow from this that he has his existence from the substance of the Father, or is of the same nature. For that the same expression is also used for entirely different things is evident from the passages Isaiah 1:3, Deuteronomy 32:18, and Job 38:28 <note>Arius argues that because God is said to have "begotten" the morning dew or the people of Israel in a metaphorical sense, the "begetting" of the Son should also be understood as an act of creation rather than a biological or substantial division of God.</note>. Nothing is made from the divine essence, but everything is made according to the will of God. Everything, however, was made by God through the Son.

He then asks him to elaborate further on this according to his better insights, and to write to Bishop Alexander, and hopes that through him, Alexander will be led back from his opinions (44).

One sees from this letter that the friends whom Arius had here and there among the bishops not only sought to win over others to his and his followers' advantage—as people who were being unjustly suppressed for a good cause—

(44) <note>original: *Theodoret. Hist. eccles. Lib. I. cap. 5.*</note> Theodoret, *Church History*, Book I, chapter 5.

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
<sig>P 3</sig>

but also continually endeavored to dissuade Alexander from his opinion and to move him toward a reconciliation with his opponent.

Eusebius of Caesarea <note>The famous church historian and bishop who attempted to find a middle ground during the conflict.</note> wrote, as Athanasius says, with this intention to a Bishop Euphration, in which he (as this writer says) plainly declares that Christ is not true God. This is undoubtedly to be understood in the sense that he did not consider Him to be God in the same sense as the Father—which other church teachers, without being whole or half-Arians themselves, have also said.

Athanasius, Bishop of Anazarbus, brought Alexander’s harsh proceedings against Arius to mind and simultaneously defended Arius's opinion. Others sought different means to unite both parties, such as George—who had formerly been a presbyter in Alexandria but was deposed, and later became Bishop of Laodicea—who wrote to both Arius and Alexander and sought to unite them through an ambiguous turn of phrase (45).

How these efforts were received is easy to imagine. But however one may judge the causes of Arius and his followers and protectors, they nevertheless—as much as could be expected from people who were just as convinced of the truth of their opinion as their opponents—happened...

<meta>image quality: clear; catchword: schiede</meta>

<margin>X</margin>
...could be done, nothing was neglected to restore peace. 

Arius gave proof of this during his stay in Nicomedia, where he had gone in the year 323, likely to be closer to his greatest protector, Eusebius. It was here that he wrote his well-known book *Thalia* (<note>original Greek: Θαλεια; meaning "The Banquet" or "The Feast"</note>), which has been the most highly regarded among all his writings.

The intention of this work was undoubtedly to place his actual teachings in a clearer light and to make them acceptable to others. It appears to have been written in a soft, pleasant, and ingratiating language; for this reason, the style used therein has been compared to the effeminate expressions of the Egyptian poet Sotades <note>Sotades was an ancient Greek poet known for a specific "wanton" or "limping" meter; by comparing Arius to him, his opponents like Athanasius were accusing him of being undignified or overly sensual in his religious writing</note> or Sosates. However, that it consisted entirely of poems, as some have believed, is not likely based on the fragments that still remain.

Athanasius has shared the following passages from it:

<margin>(46) Athanasius, *On the Councils of Ariminum and Seleucia*, Works, Volume I, page 883.</margin>

> God, it says in one passage (46), is ineffable to all, and has nothing that is similar to Him or equal to Him in glory. We call Him the Unbegotten to distinguish Him from the one who is begotten by nature; the Unoriginate, in regard to the one who has a beginning; the Eternal, in regard to the one who came into existence in time. For the Unoriginate made His Son the beginning of creatures, fathered him, and adopted him as Son. This Son, according to his existence, has nothing divine! For he is neither equal to God nor <term>consubstantial (of the same substance)</term> with Him. — God is invisible to all things that exist through the Son, and even to the Son himself. However, in so far as God can be seen, and in a certain measure, the Son sees the Father. It is a Trinity <note>Arius uses the word *Dreiheit* (Trinity), but in his view, the three "Persons" are not equal in power or nature</note>, but of unequal glory, and without a mixing of their existences, and each person is infinitely more glorious than the other. The Father is, according to his essence, foreign to the Son, because He is without beginning. The Unity was there; but before the Son was, the Duality did not yet exist, and the Father was God alone. The Son came into existence by the will of the Father; he is the "only-begotten God." He became the wisdom of the wise God according to that wise will. — What the Son is, he is by the will of God. — God is incomprehensible even to the Son. — For that which has begun to be cannot grasp that which has no beginning.

Another passage from this work is cited by Athanasius in his second speech against the Arians, where these sentences occur:

...when God was yet alone, He was not yet Father; He became so only afterwards. The Son has not always existed. Namely, when God wished to create us, He first made one whom He called the Word, Wisdom, and the Son, in order to form us through Him. There are two Wisdoms. One is proper to God and exists with Him; through this one, the Son was made, and since this was communicated to Him, He is called the Wisdom and the Word. So too, there is another Word in God besides the Son, and since He [the Son] became a partaker of it, He is called by grace the Word and the Son. — The Word was said to have been subject to change, and it stood within His own free will to remain good or not. But because God knew in advance that He would remain good, He gave Him all glory beforehand. — The Word is not truly God, but is only called so by a title of grace. — The Word is entirely foreign and unlike God in essence and belongs among created things. — The Father is hidden from the Son and cannot even be precisely comprehended by Him; indeed, He [the Son] does not even know His own essence. divided from one another, unequal to each other, and according to essence and glory, infinitely different from one another (47).

Whether these are all Arius’s own words, or a completely faithful extract from his work without added conclusions, is a testament to the paths of error to which a speculative mind <note>original German: "Grübelgeist"; refers to a spirit of over-thinking or excessive brooding on abstract problems</note> can lead men. I fear, however, that some inaccuracies may be present here: for both passages do not perfectly agree with other statements made by Arius.

Meanwhile, while Arius sought to solidify his opinion through writings during his stay in Nicomedia, he simultaneously neglected nothing to win over Alexander, without, however, yielding anything of his opinion. To that end, he wrote the remarkable letter to Alexander, which Athanasius, Epiphanius, and others have preserved for us, and which, as seen from the signature, was drafted simultaneously in the names of the elders and deacons allied with him.

In this, he first sets forth his and his followers' faith, of which he says that they received it from his ancestors and learned it from Alexander himself.

> We recognize, he says, one God, who alone is unbegotten, eternal, without beginning, true-

<page-num>234</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

> ...truthful, wise, good, powerful, unchangeable, immutable, and so on. This one [the Father] begot an only-begotten Son before eternal times, through whom He made the ages <note>Original: "Aeonen," referring to vast spans of time or spiritual realms in ancient cosmology</note> and all things. He begot Him not in appearance, but in truth, and brought Him into existence by His own will as an unchangeable, immutable, and perfect creature of God. But He is not like any of the other creatures: He is begotten, but not like others who are begotten. He is not an emanation <term>probolē <note>original: προβολή; a term used by Gnostics to describe spiritual beings "projected" from the divine source</note></term> of the Father, as Valentinus <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher</note> taught; nor is He a part of the same substance <term>meros homoousion <note>original: μέρος ὁμοούσιον; meaning a part of the same essence or "consubstantial"</note></term> as the Father, as Manichaeus <note>The founder of Manichaeism who viewed the world as a struggle between light and dark</note> taught; nor is He so in the sense of Sabellius <note>A theologian who taught that the Father, Son, and Spirit were merely different "roles" or "masks" of a single person</note>, who divides the unity and calls Him a "Son-Father"; nor as Hierax <note>An Egyptian scholar who used the analogy of one light lighting another</note> would have it, like a light from a light, or like a torch divided into two; nor is it that He who already existed was later begotten and made into a Son—all of which views you have frequently rejected in the assembly of the Church. Rather, as we say, He was created by the will of God before time and before the ages, and received life and existence from the Father, who also shared His glory with Him. But though the Father gave Him dominion over all things, the Father did not deprive Himself of what He possessed without being begotten; for He is the source of all. There are, therefore, three persons: namely God, who is the cause of all things and who

<meta>catchword: the (der)</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

> is the only one without beginning; the Son, who was begotten by the Father apart from time, and was created and established before the ages, and thus did not exist before He was begotten. However, He was begotten apart from time before all things, and has His existence from the Father alone. He is not eternal, or co-eternal with the Father, nor is He equally unbegotten with Him; He does not have His existence simultaneously with the Father, as some say who introduce two unbegotten ultimate beings. Instead, just as God is the unity and the beginning of all, so God is before all things, and is therefore prior to His Son, as we ourselves have learned from you through your teachings in the Church. Thus, just as the Son has His existence, honor, and life from God, so He has received everything from God, who is His beginning; for God rules over Him as His God, and as the one who existed before Him. If one were to understand the passages "from Him," "from the womb," or "I came forth from the Father" <note>Biblical phrases from the Psalms and the Gospel of John used to discuss the origin of Christ</note> in the way some do—as if the Son were a part of the Father’s substance or an emanation of Him—then the Father would be composite, divisible, changeable, and even physical, and subject to bodily changes, even though God is incorporeal <note>meaning without a physical body</note> (48).

<note>(48) Athanasius, On the Councils of Ariminum and Seleucia, Works Vol. I, p. 885 ff.; Epiphanius, Against Heresies, No. 69, sections 7 and 8.</note>

<summary>This page details a letter from Arius to Bishop Alexander, where Arius argues that the Son is a "perfect creature" created before time but not co-eternal with the Father. He explicitly rejects several competing theological views (such as those of Sabellius and Valentinus) to defend his position that God the Father must be the unique, unbegotten source of all existence.</summary>

<page-num>236</page-num>
# Attempt at a History

In *Epiphanius* <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403) was a church father known for his "Panarion," a catalog of heresies</note>, under this letter, besides the name of *Arius*, there also appear the names of the elders *Aethales*, *Achillas*, *Carponas*, *Sarmatas*, and *Arius*, and the deacons *Euzoius*, *Lucius*, *Julius*, *Menas*, *Helladius*, and *Gaius*, as well as the two bishops *Secundus* of *Pentapolis* and *Theonas* of *Libya*, and likewise *Pistus*, regarding whom *Epiphanius* notes that the *Arians* in *Alexandria* had installed him as bishop.

The intention of *Arius* with this letter seems primarily to have been either to bring *Alexander* <note>Bishop Alexander of Alexandria, Arius's primary opponent</note> back from his opinion, or to present the opposing view to him in such a mild light that he might relax some of the severity he had observed until then.

Whether *Alexander* answered this letter is unknown; but this much is certain: that he was moved to milder sentiments by it just as little as by the letters from the bishops who begged him to receive *Arius* back into the communion of the church. *Sozomen* <note>Salminius Hermias Sozomenus, a 5th-century historian of the Christian Church</note> says that *Eusebius* <note>Eusebius of Nicomedia, a powerful bishop and supporter of Arius</note> and his followers repeatedly requested *Alexander* to readmit *Arius* to church communion, but could not move him to do so; they viewed this as an insult to themselves and were thus moved to do everything possible to push through the opinion of *Arius* (49).

---

(49) *Sozomen's Ecclesiastical History, Book I, chapter 15.* <note>Original Latin: "Sozomeni Hist. eccles. Lib. I. cap. 15."</note>

<meta>catchword: When</meta>

<page-num>237</page-num>
# of Arianism.

<margin>Year of Christ 323.</margin>
When, in such a manner, all the efforts of *Arius’s* friends had been unable to shake the steadfastness of the Bishop of *Alexandria*, they held a *synod* <note>A regional meeting of church leaders</note> in *Bithynia* <note>A Roman province in modern-day Turkey</note> around the year 323, likely in *Nicomedia* itself, in order to force *Alexander* by means of an opposing authority to do what they had not been able to achieve by other means. It is said that 250 bishops were present at this *council*.

Nothing is known of its proceedings. It seems, however, that they disapproved of the behavior and opinion of *Alexander*; for *Sozomen* says that at this assembly, a letter was issued to all bishops everywhere, requesting them to maintain church communion with the friends of *Arius* as being orthodox, and also to persuade *Alexander* to do the same (50).

However advantageous this step seemed to give to the cause of the *Arians*—since, if *Alexander* had excluded *Arius* and his followers as *heretics* <note>Original German: "Kezzer," an archaic term for heretics derived from the word 'Cathar'</note> at a *synod*, another...

# Attempt at a History

at least as numerous and respectable a synod <note>A synod is a formal meeting of church leaders to decide on matters of doctrine or administration.</note> took up his cause, recognized him as orthodox, and wished to see communion with him maintained; yet, so little was actually accomplished. Rather, this very situation gave occasion for an even larger and more stubborn schism, and each party sought with all its might to undermine and disparage the other.

**Arius** meanwhile—presumably because he recognized the futility of all previous efforts to be universally accepted and recognized as orthodox—sent deputies to **Eusebius**, Bishop of **Caesarea**, **Paulinus**, Bishop of **Tyre**, and **Patrophilus**, Bishop of **Scythopolis**, and asked them that he and the elders of his party might be permitted to hold liturgical assemblies, since it was customary in **Alexandria** for the elders to hold their own special meetings under the general supervision of the bishop.

The basis of this request seems to lie in the resolutions made at the **Council in Bithynia**. **Sozomen** <note>Salminius Hermias Sozomenus, a 5th-century Christian historian</note> says that a synod was subsequently held in **Palestine**, and that the bishops assembled there granted **Arius** and the elders allied with him their request to assemble the people as they had otherwise been accustomed; however, they were to remain subject to **Alexander** and not cease to plead with him to be reconciled with them and to take them back into his commu-

<meta>catchword: nion</meta>

<page-num>239</page-num>

## of Arianism.

nion (51). For as much as the Arian-minded bishops took up **Arius's** cause, it seems they nonetheless sought to avoid a public break and therefore still maintained a certain regard for **Alexander**, in the hope that they might finally move him to a different behavior and a different frame of mind.

It may be suspected, given the church constitution of that time, that several synods were held in various regions because of this dispute. **Sozomen** says that many councils were held primarily in **Egypt**, without, however, stating anything about their resolutions, nor whether they were held by **Arians** or the **orthodox** (52).

Given the bitterness that had been stirred up in minds everywhere by the letters of both parties, the consequences of these assemblies were nothing other than that the evil became ever greater and more widespread, and the hatred between the parties became ever more intense. **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, often called the "Father of Church History"</note> says that great disruptions arose in other countries and provinces because of this, as not only the bishops fought among themselves, but also the common man took part in it, taking one side or the other; indeed, it became so

Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian</note> says the same thing, and Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus, another influential early church historian</note> states that there was almost no city or village where there was not a dispute over this matter; the common people were not merely spectators of these quarrels but also acted as judges, and even friends and relatives fell into strife and bitterness over it (54).

These, then, were the immediate consequences of these disputes. Whether the victory of a single proposition in speculative theology <note>Speculative theology refers to theoretical or philosophical debates about the nature of God that cannot be proven by direct experience.</note> can be compared in the slightest with such a confusion of minds requires no extensive investigation.

### IV.
### Constantine's Efforts to Settle the Disputes.

Emperor Constantine the Great had until now acted only as a spectator in these disputes, presumably because he did not fear such consequences as might be detrimental to the peace of the Empire, or because he still flattered himself with the hope that the teachers would once again unite. The state in which the Empire had previously found itself also did not allow him to take part in these quarrels. This happened <margin>323 A.D.</margin> however now, since in the year 323 he had defeated Licinius at Chrysopolis and had now taken up his residence in Nicomedia (55).

That Constantine's inclination to involve himself in the affairs of the church played a very large part in this cannot be denied. The changes and regulations made by him within the church are well known. The pagan emperors were *Pontifices Maximi* <term>High Priests; the supreme religious authority in ancient Rome</term>, and the power they held in religious matters according to this dignity was what Constantine the Great sought—so far as it was ever possible with the Christian constitution—
<meta>catchword: best</meta>

(55) Tillemont, *History of the Emperors*, Vol. IV, p. 306 ff.
->Second Part. Q<-

<summary>This page describes the social chaos caused by the Arian controversy, including public ridicule by pagans, and details Emperor Constantine the Great's decision to intervene after securing political control of the Empire in 323 A.D.</summary>
<keywords>Constantine the Great, social unrest, speculative theology, Licinius, Chrysopolis, Pontifices Maximi, religious authority, Arianism history</keywords>

could exist, also to exercise [his authority] in respect to the church (56).

Certain political considerations likewise cannot be doubted here, and given the state of the Empire at that time, nothing could have been more important to the Emperor than ensuring that the peace of the realm was not disturbed by religious quarrels, and that no restless mind <note>restless mind: likely referring to potential political agitators or schismatics</note> could use them to his own advantage. Perhaps **Socrates** <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century historian</note> refers to this when he says that the Emperor viewed these quarrels as a misfortune that affected him personally (57). It may also be, however, that true religious sentiments contributed to this. This is suggested by **Sozomen** <note>Sozomen, another contemporary church historian</note> when he says that **Constantine** was deeply grieved that many were deterred from embracing Christianity by these disputes among the teachers (58).

Furthermore, it is not unlikely that the Arian-leaning bishops, and foremost among them **Eusebius of Nicomedia**, themselves contributed to the Emperor taking an interest in these disputes. Although the older historians do not say a single word about this, this

this suspicion of some modern scholars is not entirely unfounded.

**Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Nicomedia, a powerful bishop who supported Arius</note> already held a position of respect with the Emperor's sister, **Constantia**, who was married to **Licinius** and had great influence over her brother; through her mediation, he achieved similar standing with the Emperor himself. It is therefore not improbable, considering the previous efforts of **Eusebius** and his friends to suppress the disputes, that the Emperor was moved to take up the matter either by Eusebius himself or through the representations of **Constantia**.

**Maimbourg** and other modern scholars have suspected that **Eusebius** had already prejudiced the Emperor against **Alexander** (59) <note>Alexander, the Bishop of Alexandria and primary opponent of Arius</note> to his disadvantage. It seems that **Constantine's** letter provided the primary occasion for this suspicion. But one must soon reject this when investigating **Constantine’s** conduct in this matter. Certainly, his behavior would have been quite different if **Constantine** had been prejudiced against **Alexander**.

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>244</page-num>

If **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Nicomedia, the bishop who supported Arius</note> and his friends truly played the role in the letter and in the Emperor's conduct that is attributed to them, then their behavior deserves praise rather than blame—especially when one considers that **Alexander** <note>Bishop Alexander of Alexandria, Arius's superior and opponent</note> and his friends had shown themselves so harsh and unyielding, despite all efforts from the other side to restore peace.

<margin>Year of Christ 324.</margin>
To settle these disputes, **Constantine** wrote to the two main figures of the conflict, **Alexander** and **Arius**, a remarkable letter which **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the historian, who included the text of the letter in his "Life of Constantine"</note> has preserved for us in its entirety. This letter has been praised as much by the ancients as modern writers have found much to criticize in it (60). It seems that **Arius** had at that time left Palestine again and returned to Alexandria. The reasons for this, and the further circumstances surrounding it, are entirely unknown to us.

->In this letter the Emperor explains<-

first the efforts he had applied to restore peace to the Empire as well as to suppress the unrest stirred up by the **Donatists** <note>A schismatic Christian group in North Africa that disagreed with the mainstream church over the validity of sacraments performed by "lapsed" priests</note> in Africa, and that he had intended to

<page-num>245</page-num>
<sig>Q 3</sig>

send some of the Eastern bishops to Africa to settle the disputes that had arisen there. For the light of truth had risen in the East and had spread its rays from there over the earth. Therefore, the bishops of these regions should be better able than others to judge the matter and bring it to light. But against all his expectations, his heart was most violently wounded when he heard that even more violent disputes had arisen among them than those remaining to be suppressed in Africa, and that those through whom he had wanted to heal others were themselves in need of a greater healing. Since he had meanwhile informed himself of the beginning and origin of this business, the whole matter appeared to him to be of a nature that was trivial and unworthy of such a dispute. He was writing this letter to both of them to restore harmony between them, stepping between them as a peacemaker. He would have hoped to accomplish this even if the matter they fought over were of more weight; but he hoped for it all the more now, since it was of little significance.

He then presents the origin of these disputes, just as he had learned of them.

> You, **Alexander** <note>Bishop Alexander of Alexandria, the superior of Arius who first condemned his teachings</note>, he says, have asked your elders what each of them thought about a certain passage of the Law <note>In this context, the "Law" refers to the Holy Scriptures or divine doctrine</note>, or rather, about a portion of a vain question. You, **Arius**, have incautiously voiced that which you either should never have thought, or if you had thought it, should have kept silent. In this way, disunity has arisen among you, unity has been abolished, the most holy people torn into two parties, and separated from the harmony of the common body. Let one forgive the other, and accept the fair counsel of your fellow servant <note>Constantine frequently referred to himself as a "fellow servant" of the bishops to emphasize his role as a Christian brother rather than just a monarch</note>. It was neither proper from the beginning to raise such questions, nor to answer them. For such investigations, which the Law does not present as necessary, but which are produced by the litigiousness of an idle leisure—even if they are only undertaken for practice—we must keep to ourselves, not immediately bring them into public assemblies, nor thoughtlessly entrust them to the ears of the people. For how many can truly perceive the full power of such great and difficult matters, and explain them worthily enough?

<meta>catchword: Might</meta>

<page-num>247</page-num>

> But should anyone believe that he could easily do this, how many of the people will he be able to persuade? Or who, in the investigation of such questions, will be able to sufficiently guard himself against a dangerous fall? In such things, one must therefore restrain one's talkativeness, so that the people—either because we cannot explain what is presented due to natural weakness, or because the listeners cannot grasp what is said due to their dullness of understanding—are not necessarily led by one of the two into blasphemy or separation. Let one therefore forgive the other for his thoughtless questioning and his unintelligent answering. For the dispute among you has not arisen over a primary matter of the Law. Nor has any new heresy <note>original: *Kezzerey*</note> concerning the worship of God been introduced by you. In this, you are both of one mind, and can therefore easily come together again in fellowship.

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>250</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

be that as it may, it nevertheless remains a document that does great honor to its author.

The Emperor shows himself in this letter to be entirely impartial; he brings their unchristian bickering to the attention of both sides without distinction, and exhorts them in the most touching way to practice that high duty which stands at the very top of all Christian duties. His indifference, with which he so often speaks of the subject of the dispute, has been primarily criticized and largely attributed to his ignorance in matters of religion, as he was only a <term>catechumen</term> <note>A person receiving basic instruction in the Christian-faith before being baptized</note> at that time. However, Christians had until then almost always thought similarly about such investigations—or rather, such broodings. And even if the matter had been far more important than the Emperor held it to be, the question still remains whether the hard-won victory of such a dogma from speculative theology could be compared with the damage caused by this conflict?

The Emperor sent **Hosius**, Bishop of **Corduba** <note>Modern-day Córdoba, Spain</note>, to bring this letter to Alexandria to investigate the matter and settle the disputes. **Hosius** also had a commission from the Emperor to mediate the dispute regarding the celebration of Easter, about which conflicts had previously arisen, and regarding which the mutual tolerance previously observed seems to have fallen into oblivion.

---

<page-num>251</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

**Hosius** therefore held a council in Alexandria. The circumstances of this meeting are unknown; it is also not known which bishops, or how many of them, were present. But one can see enough to know that this council did not turn out in favor of **Arius**, and this is confirmed by the scenes at this council that took place between the two parties.

**Philostorgius** <note>An 4th-century church historian who often favored the Arian perspective</note> says that **Hosius** and the other bishops excommunicated **Arius**, and that it was established that the Son is of the same essence as the Father. But he also says at the same time that **Alexander** came to **Nicomedia** and persuaded **Hosius** and the bishops to reach this conclusion. This is unfounded, and one therefore cannot consider that report of the council's conclusion to be completely reliable (62). Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century historian</note> says only that at this council the **Sabellian** error <note>The belief that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not distinct persons, but merely different "modes" or masks of a single God</note> was contradicted, and the question of **substance** and **hypostasis** <note>Technical philosophical terms used to debate whether God and Jesus were made of the same "stuff" or were distinct "realities"</note> was investigated (63).

The mildest thing that can be said is that the Arian disputes were not settled or resolved at this council. The steps taken by both parties afterward clearly prove that the proceedings of this assembly had so little calmed the spirits—

(62) Philostorgius, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, number 7.

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>252</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

appeased, but rather the evil became even more intense.

**Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century historian and bishop often called the "Father of Church History"</note> says that bishops rose up against bishops, and one part of the congregations fought against the other. This led to formal riots, and the mob went so far in its fury that they even laid hands on the statues of the Emperor and defiled them (64).

It is commonly alleged that **Arius**, after the synod held in Alexandria, turned to the Emperor to complain about the injustice done to him there and that he was being driven away from everywhere. It is said that he simultaneously presented the Emperor with a very crafty confession of faith, while citing the great number of his followers—whom he primarily had in Libya—and finally asked the Emperor for permission to hold religious services once more, should **Alexander** <note>Bishop Alexander of Alexandria, the primary opponent of Arius</note> persist in excluding him from church communion (65).

This alleged letter from **Arius** is based on nothing other than a very doubtful letter from Emperor **Constantine** to **Arius** and the Arian party, which **Epiphanius** <note>Epiphanius of Salamis, a bishop known for his fierce opposition to heresies</note> mentions, and which—

---

<columns>2</columns>
(64) Eusebius in *Life of Constantine the Great*, Book III, Chapter 4.
<column-break/>
(65) Tillemont, *Ecclesiastical Memoirs*, Volume VI, p. 112.

<meta>catchword: which—</meta>

<page-num>253</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

which **Gelasius of Cyzicus** and after him **Baronius** have provided, and with which a passage in **Socrates** <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian</note> is compared (66).

But besides the fact that the context in which **Epiphanius** speaks of this letter makes it exceedingly doubtful at what time it was written, it also contains so many polemical attacks, insults, wretched wordplay on the name of **Arius** <note>Opponents often linked the name Arius to the Greek "ares," suggesting a "martial" or "furious" nature</note>, and contradictions, that one can hardly consider it authentic. Even if one were to claim, along with some modern scholars, that it was only written after the **Council of Nicaea**, it would still be entirely unworthy of **Constantine the Great** (67).

Since this is the case with this letter, one can easily see what to think of the story that **Arius** wrote to the Emperor and also had a personal hearing with him, which is likewise based on this letter. It is just as unreliable as what **Epiphanius** relates: that **Alexander**, following the investigation held by **Hosius** in Alexandria, turned to the Emperor.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the growing ecclesiastical crisis in the early 4th century. Following the previous page's discussion of Bishop Alexander's opposition to Arius and the failed mediation efforts of Hosius of Corduba, the text now focuses on Emperor Constantine's increasing alarm at the civil unrest caused by these theological disputes and his decision to convene a universal council.</meta>

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>254</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

However, it is nothing other than highly probable that both parties were dissatisfied with the negotiations of **Hosius** <note>Hosius of Corduba, a trusted advisor to Constantine sent to mediate the Arian crisis</note> during his stay in Alexandria. For each of the warring parties wanted their own opinion declared right and the opposing view condemned; and since this did not happen—as Hosius, it seems, was only commissioned to reconcile their spirits—it was natural that **Arius** and **Alexander** were equally dissatisfied with Hosius's investigations. Consequently, the disputes were continued by both parties with just as much heat as had occurred previously.

**Constantine** had already experienced, through the example of the **Donatists** <note>A schismatic group in North Africa that caused significant civil disruption over issues of church purity and the validity of sacraments</note> in Africa, the disadvantageous consequences such religious disputes could have for the peace of the state. The Arian disputes, which were already degenerating into formal riots, did not look any better; rather, it was even to be feared that they would extend much further. This was because both parties, through their letters written to all places, had drawn the bishops and congregations of the entire Christian world into their interests, and the dispute concerned a subject that was considered far more important by Christians everywhere than that over which the Donatists had fought.

<margin>Cited in the *Annals* of **Baronius** <note>original: "Annales Baronii"</note>, p. 143, and **H. D. Walch** in his *History of Heresies* <note>original: "Gesch. der Kezzereyen"</note>, Part 2, p. 465 ff.</margin>
<meta>catchword: Die (The)</meta>

---

<page-num>255</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

The **Meletian affairs** <note>A schism in Egypt involving Meletius of Lycopolis, focusing on how to treat Christians who had lapsed during persecutions</note> likewise still continued, and because of the celebration of the **Easter festival** <note>A major controversy regarding the correct date to observe Easter, which varied between different regions</note>, the disputes had also become more lively than before. All these circumstances taken together moved the Emperor to think of a general church assembly, in order to remedy all these evils therein.

If **Constantine** looked back at the benefit that the assemblies held in Africa regarding the Donatists had produced, he could hardly expect anything advantageous from this one. But the Emperor’s conceptions of such assemblies were already so favorable that he viewed what was decided at them as the special will and pronouncement of God (68). These favorable conceptions, however, had to be even greater regarding such an assembly at which all the bishops of the entire Christian world would appear and voice their opinions. If these men were united, **Constantine** undoubtedly believed, an end would be made to all discord among Christians.

Perhaps even the warring parties themselves had set their sights on a general church assembly. This is likely, even if one considers the report of **Epiphanius** <note>Bishop of Salamis and a prolific writer against heresies</note> that such a request came from Alexander—

<margin>(68) **Eusebius**, *On the Life of Constantine the Great* <note>original: "de Vita Const. M."</note>, Book III, chapter 20.</margin>

...refuses to accept, yet it is probable of **Hosius** <note>Hosius of Corduba, the Emperor’s advisor on ecclesiastical matters</note>, as **Sulpicius** <note>Sulpicius Severus, a Christian historian</note> assures us of it (69).

In addition to these good predispositions and intentions, political considerations and the Emperor's inclination toward splendor also likely played no small part in the convening of this synod.

<margin>Year of Christ 325</margin> This was therefore the first **ecumenical** <term>universal or worldwide council</term> or general church assembly, which was summoned by **Emperor Constantine** to **Nicaea** <note>modern-day İznik, Turkey</note> in **Bithynia** for the following year of 325, in order to settle all errors prevailing among Christians at that time, and primarily to resolve the recently arisen **Arian disputes** <note>the theological conflict over whether Jesus Christ was co-eternal with God the Father</note>.

### V.

### Council of Nicaea in Bithynia.

When all previously employed means to restore peace among the contending parties had been in vain, Emperor Constantine summoned the famous assembly to Nicaea in Bithynia, to which all bishops from both the East and West were called together, and which actually began in June of the <margin>Year of Christ 325</margin> 325th year. It held its sessions in a hall of the imperial palace.

Constantine wanted to have an assembly at which, if not all bishops of the entire world, then certainly the bishops of the whole empire in both the East and West should be present. But this objective was already not achieved.

To increase the prestige of this assembly, the number of bishops has been reported as very high—even as many as 2,048 (70)—but this claim refutes itself. **Athanasius** <note>a leading opponent of Arius and later Bishop of Alexandria</note>, who was present at the synod, sets the number now at approximately three hundred, now at
<meta>catchword: upon</meta>

(70) <note>original: "Eutychii Annales Alexandr. p. 440."</note> Eutychius, *Annals of Alexandria*, page 440.

...to exactly three hundred, and elsewhere to three hundred and eighteen (71). This latter number has received particularly high acclaim, so much so that even the chronicle of **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century historian and bishop</note> was altered for its sake. For some found in it a mystical meaning, where the symbol for the cross represented 300 and the first letters of the name of **Jesus** indicated the number 18; others found in it a parallel to the 318 servants of **Abraham** (72) <note>This refers to the Greek numbering system where T equals 300 and IH equals 18; in Genesis 14:14, Abraham's 318 men were seen by early Christians as a prophetic sign of the Council</note>. Eusebius sets the number of assembled bishops at 250; **Eustathius of Antioch** <note>a prominent opponent of Arianism</note> at approximately 270 (73). These two numbers agree most closely with the first figure given by **Athanasius** <note>Athanasius of Alexandria, the chief defender of Trinitarianism</note>.

From this small number of bishops, one can already see that this assembly was anything but an **ecumenical council** <term>a worldwide meeting of all church leaders</term>, however great and universal its authority subsequently became. Most of the bishops were from the **Orient** <note>the Eastern part of the Roman Empire</note>. ...of Rome, others to **Hosius**, Bishop of Corduba, others to **Eusebius of Caesarea**, and others to **Eustathius**, Bishop of Antioch.

**Tillemont** <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a renowned French ecclesiastical historian</note>, who cites these various opinions, has the strange idea that this honor actually belonged to Bishop **Alexander of Alexandria** (74). Partiality could certainly not have been taken further than if the very man had presided over that assembly which was to determine whether his opinion, or that of his opponent, was the true one? It was perhaps already too much that everything that happened at this Council occurred under his direction and participation, as is stated in the **synodal letter** <term>an official letter sent by a church council to communicate its decisions</term> itself (75). Perhaps the suspicion of a more recent scholar is the most correct: that no one held the presidency, but **Constantine** himself was the head of the assembly (76).

Various preliminary meetings were also held before the opening of the Council, in which the disputed matter was investigated beforehand.

**Arius** was present at these and presented his opinion. Some of the bishops were of the opinion that nothing should be changed in the faith as it had been taught from the beginning, and that one should not engage in any curious investigations into it. Others, however, maintained that the opinions of the ancients should not be accepted without prior investigation. **Sozomen** <note>Salaminus Hermias Sozomenus, a 5th-century historian of the early Church</note> adds on this occasion that many of the bishops and the clergy accompanying them were great masters of disputation, and became known to the Emperor and his entourage on this occasion, and that **Athanasius** was also among them (78).

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>262</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

Arius, as mentioned, presented his opinion in these private conferences, which was supported by his friends and, conversely, contested by **Athanasius** <note>The chief theological opponent of Arianism, later Bishop of Alexandria</note> and **Marcellus of Ancyra**. The only real benefit of these preliminary discussions was likely that one could gauge in advance which way the matter might lean at the Council itself.

During these gatherings, pagan philosophers also appeared, believing this to be the best opportunity to confuse the Christians.

Two peculiar stories are told that are said to have occurred on such an occasion: namely, that a **confessor** <term>a title for someone who maintained their Christian faith under persecution but did not suffer martyrdom</term>, through a simple, artless recitation of the Christian faith, brought an eloquent scoffer to the faith; and that another bishop, **Alexander** (likely the one from Constantinople), imposed silence upon another philosopher in the name of Jesus Christ (79).

However certain it is that often the plainest and simplest presentation is far more penetrating and convincing than a host of rational conclusions, these two little stories might well belong in the catalog of "wonderful things" invented for the greater prestige of the holy men assembled at this Council. If these miracles truly happened, it is surprising that the assembled fathers were not moved by them to first cease the ungodly philosophical school-squabbling over things that lay so far outside their circle of vision, to embrace one another like brothers, and—to keep silent.

These discussions were also continued the following day when the Emperor arrived, opened the Council, and the Arian controversy, as the main reason for the assembly, was taken up first.

The diversity of opinions that **Constantine** perceived among the bishops during these talks filled him with quite extraordinary amazement. **Eutychius** <note>Eutychius of Alexandria, a 10th-century patriarch and historian known for providing details that differ from earlier sources</note> says that 2048 bishops were present

present, and all were of different opinions (80).

From this, much must certainly be discounted. However, the great diversity of opinions among the bishops is quite plausible, especially when one considers that both unlearned and learned men from various philosophical systems had come together there, and that, in addition, other parties such as the **Mariamnites** <note>A sect accused of worshipping Mary as a goddess</note>, **Sabellians** <note>Followers of Sabellius who believed that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were merely different "modes" or faces of one person</note>, and other so-called **heretics** and **schismatics** <term>those who create a division or "schism" in the church</term> had appeared. Among the **Orthodox** <note>The "right-believing" party sanctioned by the church</note> themselves, there were people of the strangest opinions, such as those who believed that Mary had conceived the Lord Christ through her ear (81) <note>This was a literal interpretation of the idea that the "Word" entered Mary upon hearing the angel's greeting</note>.

Arius had the freedom here, as in the conferences held previously, to present his opinion, which was supported by **Eusebius of Nicomedia** and others of his friends, but was contested by the friends of the opposing party, among whom **Athanasius** and **Marcellus** distinguished themselves.

It is easily to be expected that matters here, on both sides, did not proceed with the necessary cool-headedness and gentleness.

---

(81) <note>original French: "Beausobre Hist. des Manichéens, l. p. 532—543."</note> *Beausobre, History of the Manicheans, Vol. 1, pages 532–543.*
