The Emperor gave quite extraordinary proof of his patience and indulgence during these disputes. He not only listened to the heated reproaches that both parties made against one another with total composure; but he also exhorted them to moderation and harmony, employing sometimes pleas and sometimes reasons. And where they seemed to lose themselves too deeply in their philosophical speculations, seeking to determine the truth of their opinions by them, he asked them to keep to the clear pronouncements of the Prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles, and to decide according to them (82). This was a conduct that certainly reflects as much credit upon the Emperor as it must surely have shamed those who required such exhortations.

<margin>X</margin>
During all such discussions among the contending bishops, it was finally seen toward which side the scales would tip. The friends of Arius, among whom Eusebius of Nicomedia stood at the head, sought to ward off this blow by presenting a **creed** <term>a formal statement of Christian belief</term> to the assembly.

Whether this was the creed that **Eusebius of Caesarea** <note>The famous church historian and bishop</note> had drafted, or whether it was the—

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>266</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

<margin>(83) See Mr. Dr. Walch's *History of Heresies*, Part 2, p. 477.</margin>
<margin>(84) Theodoret, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 6.</margin>
Whether there was a letter of which **Ambrose** <note>A prominent 4th-century bishop of Milan and influential theologian</note> takes note, and in which the *consubstantiality* <note>original: ὁμοούσιος (homoousios) – the concept that the Son is of the same essence or substance as the Father</note> was refuted, has been doubted by some (83). This latter writing was, in all probability, the one in question. But that it was a direct refutation of the **Homoousios** <term>the doctrine that the Father and Son are of the same substance</term> is hardly believable, since the matter itself had already been sufficiently contested in previous discussions, yet there was no mention of this specific distinguishing term at that time. It seems, rather, to have been a confession drafted in such expressions that it appeared to draw closer to the opinion of the opposing party, while still allowing one to think whatever they wished. But as soon as this writing was read aloud in the assembly, the bishops of the opposing party tore it to pieces with great tumult (84).

This single trait is already sufficient proof of how much passion and violence were at work in this Council. One clearly sees here the manner of thinking and acting within both parties. Among the Arians, there was indeed moderation, but it was joined with cunning; among their opponents, however, there was passion and impetuousness.

Finally, a conclusion was reached in which the opinion of **Arius** was condemned as incorrect, and

<column-break/>

<page-num>267</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
and instead it was asserted that the Son was not brought forth out of nothing, but was from God, from the substance of the Father.

<margin>(85) Athanasius, *On the Decrees of the Council of Nicaea*, p. 267. 268. *Letter to the African Bishops*, p. 935. 936.</margin>
**Athanasius** <note>The chief architect of the Nicene Creed and the most formidable opponent of Arianism</note> says that the **Eusebians** <note>The faction following Eusebius of Nicomedia, who were sympathetic to Arius</note> on this occasion wanted to align themselves with the orthodox to a certain extent, and had signaled to one another that one could always say this of the Son, as he would thereby possess nothing that he did not have in common with all other creatures (85).

<margin>X</margin>
If this is accurate—and it is not improbable—then one sees here people who adopted the system of emanation <note>The idea that all things "flow out" from a divine source in a hierarchy of being</note>, and according to it, derived the existence of all spiritual natures from God. They gave the Son only the mere privilege of being the firstborn and coming directly from God, whereas other natures were produced only indirectly—namely, through the Son. This was an opinion held by several Greek Church Fathers.

Now, in order to prevent the misinterpretations that could still be made by **Arius**, his friends, and followers, they sought an expression by which the followers of the opposing party could distinguish themselves precisely from the Arians—

<summary>This page describes the dramatic moment at the Council of Nicaea when a compromise document was torn up by angry bishops, leading to the formal condemnation of Arius and the assertion that the Son is of the Father's substance. It highlights the political maneuvering of the "Eusebian" faction and the Council's search for a single, unambiguous term to define orthodox belief.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Council of Nicaea (325 AD). The previous page ended mid-sentence describing the "passion and" violence of the debates. # Attempt at a History
<page-num>268</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>

<margin>86-88</margin>...[dis]tinguish themselves, and for that end the expression was chosen: **of the same essence** <note>original: ὁμοούσιος (*homoousios*) — the controversial term stating that the Father and Son are made of the exact same divine substance</note>, in order to determine thereby that the Son was of the same essence with the Father.

This expression was in many respects unsuitable. It had already been used previously by heretics, and namely by the **Gnostics** <note>Early mystical religious groups whose ideas the mainstream church rejected</note>, in which sense the author of the *Questions to the Greeks* had also already spoken of **angels and demons of the same essence** <note>original: ὁμοούσιος ἀγγέλους καὶ δαίμονας</note> (86). Nevertheless, **Dionysius of Alexandria** <note>A 3rd-century bishop and theologian</note> had already said of the Son that he was **of the same essence as the Father** <note>original: ὁμοούσιος τῷ πατρί</note>, and that he could not possibly be of a foreign essence, because he was the Son and the Word of the Father (87).

However, because this very expression appeared suspicious to many from the perspective that it could easily favor <term>Sabellianism</term> <note>The belief, named after Sabellius, that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not three distinct persons but merely three "masks" or modes of one single person</note>, the most lively disputes arose again among the assembled bishops for this reason. For while some believed that in this way one would easily abolish the personal distinction between the Father and the Son, others feared that the Son would thereby be represented as a broken-off part, a <term>Probole</term> <note>original: προβολή — literally a "projection" or "offshoot," like a branch from a tree</note>, or an offspring of the Father (88).

<margin>
(86) St. Justin Martyr’s *Questions*, p. 297.
(87) Athanasius’s *Works*, Vol. I, p. 243.
(88) Socrates’s *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, p. 23.
</margin>

<meta>Catchword: Since</meta>

<page-num>269</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

Since there were quarrels over this, <term>Eusebius of Caesarea</term> <note>A prominent bishop and the first great historian of the Christian Church</note> presumably drafted the <term>Creed</term> <note>A formal statement of Christian belief</note> which he preserved in the letter to his congregation, and which might have had the intention, among others, of coming as close as possible to the doctrine of the Alexandrians without, however, making use of that specific word which was intolerable to the <term>Arians</term> <note>Followers of Arius, who believed that Jesus was a created being and not eternal like God the Father</note>, and against which many of the so-called "orthodox" themselves had no less to object.

> We believe, he says, in one God, the Father Almighty, the Creator of all things visible and invisible. — And in one Lord, Jesus Christ, the Word of God, God from God, Light from Light, Life from Life, the Only-begotten Son, the Firstborn before all creation, who was begotten from God the Father before all **aeons** (ages), through whom all things were made, who for the sake of our salvation became flesh, lived among men, suffered, rose again on the third day, ascended to his Father, and will come again in glory to judge the living and the dead. — We also believe in a Holy Spirit. — We believe that each of these is, and exists for Himself, so that the Father truly—

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>270</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: found,</meta>

...truly Father, the Son truly Son, and the Holy Spirit truly Holy Spirit, as our Lord said when he sent his disciples out to preach: Go forth, etc. <note>A reference to the "Great Commission" in Matthew 28:19: "Go therefore and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit."</note> (89)

If one examines this creed impartially, it contains nothing—even if the word <term>Homousios</term> <note>From the Greek *homoousios*, meaning "of the same substance" or "of one essence." This was the most contentious word of the era.</note> is missing from it—that would not have been perfectly consistent with the opinion declared orthodox at that time.

This confession was also approved by various parties as orthodox and as something that would remove all occasions for dispute.

Emperor Constantine likewise approved it and admitted that he himself had always thought this way; he therefore exhorted the bishops to agree with this and to sign this <term>symbolum</term> <note>A "symbolum" or "symbol" is a formal statement of faith or a creed.</note>, if only the expression <term>Homousios</term> were inserted, of which he himself gave an explanation (90).

But even this was rejected. — Was it that this symbol was truly not found specific enough

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>272</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: ches</meta>

[al]mighty God, who was begotten, not made, of one essence with the Father, through whom all things were made, that are in heaven and on earth; who for us men and for our salvation came down, and took on flesh, and became man: who suffered, and on the third day rose again, and ascended back into heaven; who shall come again to judge the living and the dead. — And in the Holy Spirit. —

Following this, however, comes the actual condemnation of the opinion of Arius in the following addition:

> But those who say: there was a time when he was not, and he was not before he was begotten, and that he came into being out of nothing, or say that he came into being from another substance or essence, or that the Son of God is created, mutable, and changeable—these the Catholic and apostolic Church (93) <term>anathematizes</term> <note>The original German uses "verflucht" (curses/condemns). In a church context, this refers to an "anathema," a formal curse by a council excommunicating a person or denouncing a doctrine.</note>.

Such was the nature of the <term>Symbolum</term> <note>A "symbol" or "creed"—a formal statement of faith.</note> with the formula of condemnation attached to it, which

<note>(93) Socrates, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, Chapter 8. Theodoret, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, Chapter 11. <note>original: "Socratis Hist. eccles. Lib. I. cap. 8. Theodoretus Hist. eccles. Lib. I. cap. 11."</note></note>

---

<page-num>273</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
<sig>Second Part.</sig>
<sig>S</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

after such long and often very unworthy disputes at this Synod, was established as the rule of true faith and as a distinguishing mark between the orthodox and the heretics. One can see clearly enough from the philosophical terminology used within it that the entire dispute, from which it was generated and brought into the world, concerned a purely philosophical subject that did not belong at all to the true religion of Jesus.

The expressions of Scripture—that which the Lord Jesus and his disciples had said of him themselves—were sufficient to recognize what one ought to think regarding the person of the Son of God. And to exchange these simple expressions of Scripture for philosophical technical terms—which also had their own difficulties and through which it was likewise impossible to cut off or avoid every opportunity for dispute—was, at its core, nothing other than declaring the revelation of Scripture to be obscure and insufficient.

Both parties, the Alexandrians <note>Followers of Alexander of Alexandria and Athanasius, who championed the "of one essence" (homoousios) view.</note> and the Arians, were certainly under the same condemnation here; both had distanced themselves from the path of Scripture and had wandered off into philosophizing. But these philosophical decisions and definitions did not belong in a <term>Symbolum</term>, which was meant to serve as a guide not just for the learned, but also for the common man.

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>274</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: same</meta>

The formula of condemnation attached to the <term>Symbolum</term> <note>The Nicene Creed or formal statement of faith.</note> was entirely unworthy of the spirit of the religion of Jesus. It was the most powerful means that could be devised by the enemy of all that is good to introduce hatred, discord, and unchristian persecutions among Christians. It was the beginning of that terrible despotism which, from this time forward and under the cloak of religion, has endeavored to bring the human intellect under its yoke.

Hardly had this Creed been read aloud when several of those present declared themselves against it and refused to sign it. <term>Sozomen</term> <note>Salminius Hermias Sozomenus (c. 400–450 AD), a historian of the early Christian Church.</note> says that seventeen individuals primarily declared their opposition (94). Among these was also Eusebius of Caesarea.

However, when they began to conduct closer investigations into the expressions used in the Creed, a large part of them realized that not everything had been so tightly locked down that a way out might not still remain open for those who wished to navigate between the opinions of Arius and the Alexandrians.

Eusebius of Caesarea himself provides the following explanation on this matter. He says that they accepted the term <term>ὁμοούσιος</term> <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος" (homoousios); meaning "of the same essence" or "consubstantial." This was the most controversial word at the Council of Nicaea.</note>,

---

(94) <note>original: "Sozomen. Hist. eccles. Lib. I. cap. 20."</note> *Sozomen, Ecclesiastical History, Book I, Chapter 20.*

---

<page-num>275</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
<sig>S 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

of the same essence, for the sake of peace and so as not to miss the true understanding. This expression, he argues, should not be understood in a physical manner, as with animals, nor as a division or separation from the essence of the Father. Rather, it actually indicates that the Son has no similarity to created beings, but is instead like the Father, from whose substance—and from no other—he was begotten. In the same way, one must understand the passage in the Creed where it is said of the Son that he was "begotten and not made." This word "made" properly belongs to other creatures who were made through the Son; he is not like them, but of a better essence. However, the manner in which the Son was begotten of the Father can neither be expressed in words nor comprehended. For his part, he had no hesitation in condemning the phrases stating that "the Son was made out of nothing" and "there was a time when he was not"; for these expressions are not found in the Scriptures. For this reason, he also condemned the sentence: "the Son was not before he was begotten," for the Son of God certainly existed before his <term>incarnation</term> <note>The original "Menschwerdung" refers to God becoming human in the person of Jesus.</note>. —

Of course, the question was not really about the pre-existence of the Son before his incarnation, but rather about his existence as the Son of God, and whether one could say that he existed as a Son according to his divine nature

<page-num>276</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

been, before he was begotten? But on this matter Emperor Constantine himself provided the explanation in his own person, as <term>Eusebius</term> <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the court historian and bishop.</note> notes: that the Son had also existed before all ages in this regard. For before he was begotten in reality, he was already in the Father according to potential, without being begotten; since the Father was always Father, just as the Son is always King and Savior, and everything according to potential (95).

Whether this explanation by the Emperor was quite to the taste of the strict orthodox among the Nicene Fathers, I shall leave undecided. Suffice it to say that it had the desired effect, as did the other explanations that the doubters made either for themselves or to one another: those who had previously refused to sign went over to the larger party. Only five remained, namely Eusebius of Nicomedia, Theognis, Bishop of Nicaea, Maris of Chalcedon, Theonas of Marmarica, and Secundus, Bishop of Ptolemais.

<term>Socrates</term> <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380 – after 439 AD), a Greek Christian church historian.</note> says that they primarily made a mockery of the expression: <term>consubstantial</term> <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος" (homoousios); the controversial term meaning "of the same substance," used to describe the relationship between God the Father and Jesus.</note>, as it could only be used for things that arise from another by outflow, division, or breaking off—like children among humans,

<meta>catchword: from</meta>

---

(95) <note>original: "Theodoret. Hist. eccles. Lib. I. cap. 11."</note> *Theodoret, Ecclesiastical History, Book I, Chapter 11.*

<page-num>277</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

from plants that shoot up from a root, or when two or more bowls are fashioned from a mass of gold (96).

But Emperor Constantine soon put an end to this resistance as well by declaring that he would punish with exile those who still hesitated in the slightest to sign the <term>Symbolum</term> <note>The Nicene Creed.</note>. This was a declaration that certainly demonstrated the Emperor's zeal and did him credit, but surely not his wisdom, since convictions are not won by banishments, but by reasons. In later times, however, once this path had been started, it was continued in the same way.

Faced with such a threat from the Emperor, Eusebius of Nicomedia, Theognis of Nicaea, and Maris of Chalcedon finally brought themselves to sign.

<term>Philostorgius</term> <note>An Anomoean (radical Arian) church historian of the 4th and 5th centuries.</note> reports to us that these bishops decided to sign at the persuasion of the Emperor's sister, <term>Constantia</term> <note>Flavia Julia Constantia, the sister of Constantine the Great and widow of Licinius; she was known to have Arian sympathies.</note>. This is probable, as Eusebius of Nicomedia had already been held in high regard by her for several years. He even claims that it happened on her advice...

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>278</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

it is said to have happened that these bishops, instead of the word **consubstantial** <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος" (homoousios); meaning 'of the same substance,' the orthodox position.</note>, inserted the word **of similar substance** <note>original: "ὁμοιούσιος" (homoiousios); the compromise position favored by many semi-Arians.</note> into their signatures. This is not so probable: for in the case of such a fraud, if it had actually occurred, Eusebius and Theognis would not have found it necessary to bribe the notary who held the council acts in custody to strike out their signatures, as <term>Sozomen</term> <note>Salminius Hermias Sozomenus (c. 400 – c. 450), a historian of the Christian Church.</note> relates. Nor should they have had to regret their accession to the Alexandrian doctrine in the signature if they had truly not joined it through such a signature (97). However it may have happened with this signature, such hypocrisy was always the most natural consequence that could be expected from such a coercion of conscience.

Theonas of Marmarica, on the other hand, and Secundus of Ptolemais could be moved by nothing to co-sign the **Creed** <note>original: "Symbolum"; the formal statement of faith drafted at Nicaea.</note>, and they therefore shared the same fate as Arius and his followers (98).

<page-num>279</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
§ 4

Now that the doctrinal concept had been established in such a way after long mutual strife, a formula of faith drafted, and the signatures for it brought together, the Council finally proceeded to the formal condemnation of Arius, his followers, and their doctrines and writings. He was formally declared a heretic by the assembly, along with the two bishops Theonas and Secundus and his other followers, excluded from the communion of the church, and his dogmas and writings—among these primarily his book <term>Thaleia</term> <note>original: "Thaleia" (The Banquet); a work by Arius written in verse to make his theological ideas accessible to common people through song.</note>—were cursed (99).

The Emperor thereupon undertook it himself to bring this conclusion to full execution through his power. Arius, along with the priests adhering to him, was banished into misery in Illyria <note>A Roman province in the western Balkans.</note> by imperial command. The same happened to the two bishops Theonas and Secundus (100).

But this report does not fit with the rest of history at all and is presumably to be attributed to an error by Jerome. See Dr. Walch’s *History of Heresies*, Part II, p. 480.

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>280</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

Philostorgius relates of the latter <note>Referring to Secundus of Ptolemais, mentioned on the previous page.</note> that he reproached <term>Eusebius of Nicomedia</term> <note>A powerful bishop and ally of Arius who ultimately signed the Nicene Creed to avoid exile, though he later returned to his Arian views.</note>, as he was being led away, for having signed out of fear of being sent into misery, and at the same time prophesied to him that he would also be exiled within a year’s time. A prophecy that indeed came true, but, like many others of that kind, may only have been invented after the fact, so that this side too would not lack for prophets and prophecies.

Furthermore, the Emperor decreed that the writings of <term>Arius</term> and his followers should be surrendered and burned, and he imposed the death penalty on anyone found to have hidden such works and not surrendered them.

At the same time, he commanded that the followers of <term>Arius</term> should henceforth be called <term>Porphyrians</term> <note>A derogatory label designed to link the Arians to the pagan philosopher Porphyry, making them enemies of the state and church.</note>, because Arius, just like the godless philosopher <term>Porphyry</term> <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234–305 AD), a Neoplatonic philosopher who wrote a famous treatise against Christianity.</note>, had sought to ruin Christianity (1).

# Attempt at a History
<page-num>282</page-num>

had begun to persecute one another through slanders (2).

In order, moreover, to put a stop to the evil where it had first broken out—namely at <term>Alexandria</term>, where <term>Arius</term> still had many friends and followers—Emperor <term>Constantine</term> issued a special letter to the Alexandrians. In it, he exhorted them to reject the error which the enemy of truth had scattered among them, to return to the communion of their brothers, and to accept as the truth that which had been recognized and established at the Council as the right doctrine; for that which three hundred bishops had agreed upon was to be regarded as nothing other than a <note>original: "Gottes Ausspruch"</note> divine pronouncement (3).

I shall pass over the other conclusions and decrees made at this Council, as they do not belong here.

What may still have some relationship to this matter is the decree made regarding the <term>Meletians</term> <note>A group of Christians in Egypt, led by Meletius of Lycopolis, who had broken away from the mainstream church over how to handle those who had renounced their faith during Roman persecutions.</note>. One of the primary reasons might perhaps have been that they intended to weaken the party of <term>Arius</term> in this way, by uniting with those who—without necessarily agreeing with Arius in his opinion on the person of Christ—had nonetheless given no small support to his faction (4).

that his godless teaching, speeches, and titles, and the blasphemies—wherein he said the Son of God was brought forth from nothing, that there was a time when he was not yet, that he was capable of both virtue and evil, and that he was made and a creature—should be placed under the ban <note>This "ban" refers to excommunication, a formal exclusion from the Christian community and its sacraments.</note>. Therefore, the holy <term>synod</term> <note>synod: a formal meeting of church leaders to decide on matters of doctrine or administration</note> cursed these blasphemies. What else had happened with him, they had partly already learned, and partly it would still become known to them. He had received the reward of his unrighteousness. But his godlessness had possessed so much power that he even dragged <term>Theonas of Marmarica</term> and <term>Secundus of Ptolemais</term> <note>These two Libyan bishops were Arius's most loyal supporters and refused to sign the Nicene Creed, leading to their exile.</note> into ruin along with him, who were thus also condemned with him.—

<margin>(6) <note>original: "Socratis Hist. eccles. Lib. I. c. 9."</note> Socrates, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 9.</margin>
The two letters of the Emperor, which <term>Socrates</term> <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century historian whose "Ecclesiastical History" is a primary source for the early church.</note> has likewise preserved for us, are written in a similar tone and even, in a certain sense, with greater intensity. The first of these is addressed to the Alexandrians alone, while the other is directed to all bishops and to the people (6).

The Emperor now also confirmed the conclusions made at <term>Nicaea</term>, and in a sense made them into imperial laws, so that anyone who acted contrary to them had to be viewed at the same time as a criminal against the state.

<meta>Catchword: On</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>285</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
On such a way, the Arian <term>heresy</term> <note>heresy: a belief or opinion contrary to established religious doctrine</note> seemed completely suppressed, and everything appeared to be applied against it in such a way that it would never again be in a position to rise once more from its fall. But its subsequent fates have shown the opposite and confirmed the truth that violent suppressions are a far more effective means of preserving and elevating heresies than of exterminating them.

->VI.<-

## Considerations regarding the opinions of Alexander and Arius, and regarding the Nicene Creed.

Here, where the first main period of Arianism closes with its suppression at the Council of Nicaea, will be the most suitable place to make some observations about the ideas of the contending parties and the <term>confession of faith</term> <note>also known as the "Symbolum" or Creed</note> drawn up at Nicaea. This will not only enable us to judge the parties that arose within Arianism itself shortly after these times, but it is also all the more necessary because, in the future, this entire dispute degenerated and passed over into an actually political struggle.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the theological disputes following the Council of Nicaea, specifically focusing on how the primary combatants—Alexander of Alexandria and Arius—interpreted (and perhaps misinterpreted) the earlier teachings of Church Fathers like Origen regarding the eternal nature of the Son.</meta>

<page-num>286</page-num>
# Attempt at a History
<meta>catchword: the,</meta>

It is easy to expect that both conflicting parties did not understand each other, and that once they had clashed, they blamed one another for much and, through their own deductions, attributed teachings to the opponent of which he had never thought. But it is just as certain that both have rarely been judged correctly, and that even if the Nicene faith <note>The creed established at the Council of Nicaea in 325 AD, which defined the orthodox relationship between God the Father and Jesus the Son.</note> was the ancient and originally correct doctrine of the church, it was nevertheless departed from by Alexander <note>Alexander of Alexandria, the bishop who first opposed Arius.</note> not much less than by Arius, and various matters were left entirely untouched which nonetheless later came into play during this dispute, and were then counted toward the Nicene doctrine by the opponents of the Arians.

Alexander said of Arius and his followers that they taught that God had not always been Father, nor had the Son of God always existed; rather, God, who always is, made him out of nothing (7).

Just as the doctrine of Arius is presented here, it was the direct opposite of that which Origen <note>A hugely influential 3rd-century theologian whose complex writings were used as ammunition by both sides of the Arian controversy.</note> had previously stated. "We do not claim," he says, "what the heretics assert: that a part of the divine substance was transformed into the Son, or that the Son was brought forth by the Father out of nothing (<term>from what is not</term> <note>original: "ἐξ οὐκ ὄντων"</note>), that is, outside of His essence—

---

(7) <note>original: "Socrates Hist. eccles. Lib. I. c. 6."</note> Socrates, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 6.

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

—and that there was a time when he was not; but, setting aside all physical conceptions, we say that the Son and Wisdom were begotten from the Invisible and Incorporeal, just as the will comes from the mind. — John says of God that He is a light, and Paul says of the Son that he is the radiance of the eternal light. Now, as the light cannot be without radiance, so also the Son cannot be conceived of without the Father. — How then can one say that there was a time when the Son was not? That is just the same as if one were to say there was a time when Wisdom, Truth, and Life did not exist: for in all of these, the substance of the Father is perfect. — This can never be separated from the Father. — What one says of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit goes beyond all time and eternity" (8).

It may be that much from Rufinus <note>Rufinus of Aquileia, who translated Origen's works from Greek to Latin, often "correcting" them to sound more orthodox, which makes it hard to know Origen's original words.</note> has flowed into this; meanwhile, Origen says this very thing in the preceding passage, where it is written: "One cannot say that God the Father was ever without the begetting of this Wisdom. — Otherwise, one would have to say that God could not beget Wisdom before He actually begat it—or that He was indeed able, but did not wish to. — Both—

<margin>This was also perfectly identical to what those among the Church Fathers—who assumed an <term>internal Word</term> <note>original: "Λόγος ἐνδιάθετος" (Logos endiathetos); the Word as it exists within the mind of God before creation.</note> and an <term>uttered Word</term> <note>original: "προφορικὸς" (prophorikos); the Word spoken aloud or sent forth to create the world.</note>—taught regarding the origin of the Son of God. If Arius therefore said that the Son did not exist before He was begotten, and that the Father had not always been Father, then he agreed—concerning the start of the emergence of the Son's generation—with what respected teachers of the Church in the East and West had already said before him.</margin>

What is most striking, and also most severely censured by Alexander, is the expression that the Son was brought forth <term>from what is not</term> <note>original: "ἐξ οὐκ ὄντων" (ex ouk ontōn); often translated as "out of nothing."</note>. This expression allowed for a milder interpretation, just as the phrase "from what is not" was also used regarding the bringing forth of things by those who did not claim that the world was formed out of nothing, but rather out of an eternal matter.

Alexander found two points here that offended him. First, in his opinion, the phrase "from what is not" presupposed an interval of time between the Father and the Son, but such an <term>interval</term> <note>original: "Διάστημα" (diastēma); a gap or distance in time or space.</note> was unthinkable; and secondly, he attributed to the Son an origin other than from the essence and substance of the Father. This is, in my opinion, the main point in this matter. Arius passes over this point quite easily when he says: that we say the Son is <term>from things that are not</term> <note>original: "ἐξ οὐκ ὄντων" (ex ouk ontōn); often translated as "from nothing," meaning he was not made from any pre-existing material or God's own substance.</note> because he is neither a part of God nor does he have his existence from any pre-existing matter.

Examined closely, this idea—and this expression—likewise contained nothing that degraded the Son to the level of common creatures: for there always remained the important distinction between him and the creatures, namely that they had their existence through him, while he received his directly from the Father. At the very least, the conception held by those among the older Church Fathers—who considered the entire spirit world to be outpourings of God and assigned the Son no other advantage over the remaining <term>Aeons</term> <note>In Gnostic and early Christian thought, Aeons were various levels of divine beings or emanations from the source of all life.</note> than that he was the first and oldest of them and the producer of the others—was no more "orthodox" and did not place the Son any higher than the aforementioned conception held by Arius.

Thus, one and the same proposition could emerge from two principles that stood far apart from each other. Arius does not say in his letter that he believes the Son to be of another

The reason for this assertion lay, indisputably, in the fact that <term>unbegottenness</term> <note>The state of having no origin or beginning; being "self-existent."</note> was considered an essential requirement of the divine nature and the divine essence, as Clement of Alexandria said: there is only one unbegotten being, God the Almighty, and one first-born being, through whom all things were made (15). Therefore, Arius reasoned, if one wanted to derive the existence of the Son from the essence of the Father, one would also have to attribute <term>unbegottenness</term> <note>original: "ἀγεννησίαν" (agennēsian); the quality of not having been brought into being.</note> to him at the same time, which would be impossible without destroying the unity of God. Eusebius therefore also says that the Son does not participate in the unbegotten nature.

as this thought had indeed actually occurred to some of the ancient Church Fathers. For example, among many of those who used the expression <term>emanation</term> <note>original: "προβολὴ" (probolē); a term used by early theologians to describe how the Son "came forth" from the Father, though it later fell out of favor due to its use in Gnostic systems.</note> regarding Christ, and likewise in Tertullian, who said of Christ that only a portion of the divine substance had fallen to his lot.

Here they found no other way out than to claim of the Son that he has his existence <term>from things that are not</term> <note>original: "ἐξ οὐκ ὄντων" (ex ouk ontōn); a key Arian phrase asserting that the Son was created out of nothing, rather than being part of God's own substance.</note>. And indeed, the Church Fathers of earlier times had always expressed themselves so precariously on this point that, even if they had never said the Son was brought forth from nothing, the fact that he was the "Only-begotten" and "First-born" could always refer more to his close and immediate relationship with the Father than to an existence derived from the Father’s very essence. For the Son alone had his existence immediately from the Father, whereas the creatures only received theirs from the Son.

Alexander says at the same time that Arius reckons the Son among created things, or—as it is expressed even more strongly in the letter to Alexander of Constantinople—that he belongs among the rational and irrational things that God created, and is only improperly called the Word and the Wisdom of God; rather, he is a creature of the Wisdom dwelling within God. —

Actually, even this expression in itself could not be considered heretical, since various orthodox teachers had used it regarding Christ, and moreover, it appears in Proverbs 8:22, a passage which at that time was commonly cited in reference to the Son of God <note>Proverbs 8:22 in the Greek Septuagint reads: "The Lord created me at the beginning of his work," which Arians used to prove the Son was a created being.</note>. Even if one must maintain that Arius’s conception was inherently incorrect—that the Son was made from nothing and was not the essential Son of God—the consequences drawn from this were at least not those admitted by Arius and his followers: namely, that the Son belonged among the rational and irrational creatures and, by nature, had no advantage over the other "sons of God." For Eusebius says that the Son was formed according to the most exact likeness of the unspeakable nature of his Creator, and that all other creatures were entirely unlike him in nature (17).

Alexander finally also blames Arius for ascribing a changeable nature to the Son of God, according to which he was capable of and receptive to both good and evil. — These consequences were easy to draw, and the fact that they were primarily logical deductions seems to be indicated by the expression <term>consequently</term> <note>original: "ἀκολόθως" (akolouthos); used here to suggest Alexander was inferring a result Arius did not explicitly state.</note>. (18). — Two clear pronouncements here stand in opposition to one another, between which it is difficult to decide.

If one were to say hereafter that Alexander had gone too far in his zeal; that he had imprudently attacked expressions which others had already used before Arius; that he had drawn too many disadvantageous consequences from Arius's words which were not admitted by him or his followers; and that he had undeservedly equated Arius with the Jews, the Pagans, and other enemies of Christ; then this cannot be entirely denied. But it is also certain that Arius, despite all outward appearance of gentleness, did not treat his opponent much better.

He blamed Alexander for saying that the Son is always God, always the Son, and that the Father and Son always exist together. Since Arius himself said that the Son had existed before all times and eternities, this entire accusation was certainly nothing other than a descent into <term>metaphysical hair-splitting</term> <note>original: "metaphysische Spizfündigkeiten"; refers to overly pedantic or trivial logical distinctions.</note>, and it was absurd to engage in the determination of a specific point in time when speaking of eternity alone. <meta>This page continues the analysis of Bishop Alexander’s theological position against Arius. It focuses on the subtle distinction Alexander made between the Father and the Son, arguing for the Son's eternal existence while maintaining the Father's role as the ultimate source of that existence.</meta>

<page-num>298</page-num>

who had no one as the author of his existence, but derived the existence of the Son from the Father. If Alexander said that the Father had always been Father, and the Son always Son, then this was very much the same situation as with those among the older church teachers who indeed held the world to be eternal—because God could never have been called the All-Ruler if nothing had existed over which he ruled—but who nevertheless derived the origin of the world from God.

As precisely as Alexander connects the existence of the Son with the existence of the Father, so that no interval could be thought between them; so he nonetheless by no means abolishes the distinction that is found between the Father and the Son. Rather, he says that the Son is not the Unbegotten; this pertains to the Father alone. He thoroughly denies that which Arius derived from the words "he was always from eternity": namely, that the eternal existence belonging to the Father alone would thereby be attributed by the Father himself to the Son (19).

According to Arius, Alexander was further alleged to have claimed that the Son was "always born" and "unbegottenly begotten." If this is correct, it certainly belonged among the gibberish phrases <note>German: "kauderwelschen Redensarten"; meaning jargon or nonsensical terminology.</note> that the dear

(19) <note>original: "Alexander in Ep. Synod. ap. Theodoreturn Lib. I. c. 3."</note> Alexander in the Synodical Letter, as found in Theodoret, Book I, Chapter 3.

<meta>catchword: Chur-</meta>

<column-break/>

<header>of Arianism.</header>
<page-num>299</page-num>

Church Fathers have held to their credit; as for example in Tertullian (20), and it was to be expected from such times, when people were already beginning to shroud the plain and comprehensible teaching of Jesus in all sorts of riddles.

Moreover, if Alexander held the opinion of an <term>internal Word</term> <note>original: "Λόγος ἐνδιάθετος" (logos endiathetos); the concept that the "Word" or "Reason" of God existed within Him before being expressed externally.</note>, then according to the concepts associated with it, it was not absurd to say the Son was <term>unbegotten-begotten</term> <note>original: "ἀγεννητογενὴς" (agennetogenes)</note>, and that he existed with the Father "unbegottenly"—namely, not as a second being originating from himself, but as the internal Reason of the Father, <term>co-unbegotten</term> <note>original: "συνάγεννητος" (synagennetos)</note> with the Unbegotten, as the <term>internal Word</term>, but "begotten" insofar as he received his own personality from the Father. In this, then, there would at least be nothing that has not also been said by orthodox teachers of the church. But it is not probable that Alexander was a follower of this opinion regarding an eternal <term>internal Word</term> dwelling within God, which later received its own personality; otherwise, Arius and his followers would not have let this latter point pass without criticism, as it would have been something advantageous to them. Rather, Arius himself says in his letter to Alexander that he rejected this proposition (21).

Since Alexander, in his letter to the bishops and to Alexander of Constantinople, expressly

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Bishop Alexander’s theological position against Arius. It focuses on the logical consequences Arius drew from Alexander's claims, specifically regarding whether the Son is unbegotten or a division of the Father's essence.</meta>

<page-num>300</page-num>
<header># Attempt at a History</header>

explicitly denies it, saying that he does not attribute "unbegottenness" to Christ—which belongs to the Father alone—and since Arius himself admits this in the letter addressed to him; one can and must consider this allegation to be nothing other than a regrettable consequence that Arius drew from Alexander's assertion that no moment could be conceived in which the Son was not begotten by the Father. It was a consequence that was just as unreasonable as the one Alexander drew from Arius's own propositions.

From Alexander's assertion that the Son is "from God" and "from the essence of the Father," Arius drew the disadvantageous conclusion that the former must either abolish the personal distinction between the Father and the Son or assume a division of the divine essence. The former was probable, as Alexander retained the concept of the <term>Logos</term> <note>The Word</note> and <term>Sophia</term> <note>Wisdom</note> that the Alexandrian teachers from the school of Origen had frequently adopted, through which personal distinction was largely abolished. This was not remedied by the word <term>hypostasis</term> <note>original: "ὑπόστασις"; in this era, the term for "person" or "individual existence" had not yet reached its later technical precision.</note> which Alexander used, as in those times it did not yet have the same definiteness as it did later. The latter conclusion was probable because Arius, in his letter to Eusebius, says that among the Eastern bishops upon whom Alexander had pronounced an anathema <note>A formal curse or excommunication by the church.</note>, those were excepted who called the Son an "outcast" and "offshoot" of the Father.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>301</page-num>
<header># of Arianism.</header>

The former accusation and the suspicion of <term>Sabellianism</term> <note>A heresy teaching that Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are merely different "modes" or masks of one person, rather than three distinct persons.</note> are addressed by Alexander in his letter to Alexander <note>Likely referring to Alexander of Constantinople or a general circular letter.</note> by saying, regarding the passage John 10:30, that Christ does not call himself the Father, nor does he say that the two <term>natures</term> <note>original: "ουσιας" (ousias); here Alexander uses the term to refer to 'persons' or 'substances' which are two according to their distinct existence.</note> are merely one. Likewise, Alexander also explicitly contradicts the latter accusation when he says that the Son was not begotten of the Father through division or "outflow" in a physical manner.

However high Alexander placed the Son, and as closely as he connected him to the Father on one hand—claiming he had his existence from the essence of the Father and that no moment could be thought when the Son was not also present with the Father—he nevertheless made just as great a distinction on the other hand between the Father and the Son. For he explicitly declares that there is a great difference between the unbegotten Father and the things created by him out of nothing, and says that between these two, the "only-begotten nature" of the Son stands in the middle.

Thus, according to his conception, the nature of the Son was a "middle thing" <note>An intermediary or bridge.</note> between God and created beings.

> (22) Theodoret, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, Chapter 3.

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the theology of Bishop Alexander of Alexandria and the priest Arius. <header>302 Attempt at a History</header>

creatures. To be sure, Alexander's opinion here is not that the Son is as fundamentally different from the Father as the creatures are; this would be too striking a contradiction, as he immediately adds that the Son is equal to the Father in all things, lacking only the single quality of being unbegotten. Rather, Alexander imagines three categories of things: namely, God, who exists of Himself; the creatures brought forth from nothing; and the <term>mediating nature</term> <note>original Greek: μεσιτευουσαν φυσιν (mesiteuousan physin)</note> of the Son, who is distinguished from the Father by being begotten, and from the creatures by the fact that He was not, like them, brought forth from nothing. Nevertheless, this thought remains very paradoxical and was presumably not denounced as a <term>heresy</term> <note>original: "Kezzerey"</note> by the Arians because it was, to some extent, favorable to them.

->Now, comparing the views of Arius and Alexander, it was claimed that<-

1. Alexander maintained that the Son is so eternal that one cannot conceive of even a moment in which He was not begotten by the Father, and in which the Father was not Father. —

> Arius admittedly granted that the Son received His existence before all times and eternities; but he claimed, on the other hand, that one must nevertheless assume a preceding existence

must take to be true of the Father, because the Son received His existence from Him.

2. Alexander maintained that the Son is true and perfect God in the same sense as the Father, because His worship is commanded in Holy Scripture, and the name "God" bestowed upon Him is not a mere gift of grace. —

> Arius, on the other hand, maintained the latter, and denied that Christ was God in the same sense as the Father, because otherwise He would also have to possess "unbegottenness."

3. Alexander maintained that the Son was necessarily and naturally begotten from the essence of the Father, and that He is therefore of one and the same essence and nature as the Father. —

> Arius, conversely, considered the bringing forth of the Son to be an act resulting solely from the free will and power of God; he called this bringing forth a "creation," viewed the name "Son" only as a title of grace, denied that He was begotten from the essence of the Father, but said instead that He was of a different essence and

<meta>This page continues the point-by-point comparison between the theological positions of Bishop Alexander of Alexandria and the priest Arius, specifically focusing on the Son’s relationship to the Father and the subsequent rulings of the Council of Nicaea.</meta>

<page-num>304</page-num>

and nature, and brought forth from nothing. <note>This fragment completes the description of created beings from the previous page.</note>

**4.** Alexander said that the Son was the true and natural Son of God, begotten in a manner that surpasses all understanding. —

Arius, on the other hand, held this sonship to be an adoption as a child, and derived it purely from the free will and grace of the Father.

**5.** According to Alexander, the Son was a <term>mediating nature</term> <note>original: "mittlere Natur"</note> between the unbegotten Father and the creatures brought forth from nothing; He was distinguished from the creatures by His essence and nature, and by the fact that He had been begotten by the Father, but distinguished from the Father in turn by the fact that He did not have His existence of Himself, but from the Father.

According to Arius’s conception, however, the Son was indeed highly exalted above all creatures, as the one through whom they were called into existence and formed according to the most perfect likeness of his Creator; but He was nonetheless a creature like them, and brought forth from nothing.

<meta>catchword: 6. From</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>305</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>

**6.** From these propositions, Alexander concluded that the Son is by nature unchangeable and necessarily holy, and that He knows the Father most perfectly.

From Arius’s claims, on the other hand, it followed that although the Son had always been holy, He was—like all creatures—nonetheless susceptible to evil as well as good, and that the essence of the Father was incomprehensible to Him. However, Arius at least did not always admit to these consequences.

Since Arius’s opinions were condemned at the <term>Council of Nicaea</term> <note>The first ecumenical council of the Christian Church, held in 325 AD to resolve the Arian controversy.</note>, it is almost universally believed that Alexander’s ideas agree in every respect with what was established at Nicaea, and regarding the actual main point, this cannot be denied. Nevertheless, upon closer examination, a significant difference might still be found, especially when one considers that Alexander made the Son into a <term>middle thing</term> <note>original: "Mittelding"</note> that stands in the middle between the Father and the creatures.

The first thing that the <term>Synod</term> <note>Another term for a council of church leaders.</note> of Nicaea established as the orthodox doctrine against Arius was this: that the Son is truly begotten of the Father, having His essence from the essence of the Father, and having it by necessity,

<sig>Second Part. U</sig>
<meta>catchword: having,</meta>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the theological debate between Bishop Alexander and Arius, specifically examining the introduction of the term "consubstantial" (homoousios) at the Council of Nicaea.</meta>

<page-num>306</page-num>
<header>Attempt at a History</header>
<meta>catchword: of a</meta>

[the Son] was not brought forth from nothing, and does not owe His existence to the mere will of God. And this thought was expressed through the word **consubstantial** <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος" (*homoousios*), meaning "of the same substance" or "of one essence."</note>.

If the account of Philostorgius <note>A 4th-century church historian who often wrote from a pro-Arian perspective.</note> is correct—that Alexander had previously conferred with Hosius <note>Hosius of Cordoba, a key advisor to Emperor Constantine.</note> and that the word **consubstantial** had already been decided upon—then the suspicion is not entirely unfounded that the opinion concerning the **Word** <note>original: "λόγος" (*logos*)</note> and **Wisdom** <note>original: "σοφία" (*sophia*)</note> (which had become quite dominant among the Alexandrians since Origen’s time and which largely did away with personal distinctions) was at work here.

<margin>
(23) Eusebius of Caesarea in Socrates’ *Ecclesiastical History*, Book I, chapter 8. Athanasius, *Epistle to the Africans*, number 9, and *On the Decrees of the Nicene Council*, number 20.
</margin>

The main intent behind this word, as Eusebius and Athanasius say, was to show that the Son has no similarity at all to created things, but only to the Father, with whom He constitutes one essence (23). But since they also wanted to remove all concepts arising from sensory perceptions (as Athanasius says) or concerning bodily things (as Eusebius puts it), which might be attributed to this word according to common usage, the word was to be understood such that the Son did not possess a **specific unity** <note>original: "unitatem specificam," referring to the unity shared by different individuals of the same species or category.</note>, according to which several things of one and the same genus

---

<page-num>307</page-num>
<header>of Arianism.</header>
<sig>U 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

are of one essence and nature with the Father, but rather a **numerical unity** <note>original: "unitatem numericam," meaning that the Father and Son are not just similar, but are literally one and the same singular being.</note> with Him, having one and the same essence.

Nothing is clearer than that the Fathers of Nicaea were caught here in a bind between the so-called Sabellian opinion <note>A heresy that claimed the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were merely different "modes" or masks of a single person, rather than three distinct persons.</note> and that explanation of the word **consubstantial** which had been rejected at the Council of Antioch; and one should not be at all surprised when Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, the church historian.</note> says that they did not understand one another at all.

That this definition was grasped by very few is shown by the claim of the Orthodox in the following century, where it was stated as Nicene doctrine that Christ was **consubstantial with the Father** <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος πατρὶ" (*homoousios patri*)</note> according to His divinity, but **consubstantial with us** <note>original: "ὁμοούσιος ἡμῖν" (*homoousios hēmin*)</note> according to His humanity. If this latter could not possibly be understood as a **numerical unity** (since Christ and all humans are not a single being), and if this meaning—according to this definition—also applied to the divine nature, then the idea of a **division** <note>original: "διαιρέσεως" (*diaireseōs*)</note> or an **emanation** <note>original: "προβολῆς" (*probolēs*)</note> was always present, even though they denied accepting such a thing.

Therefore, whichever way one turns here, Alexander was in no less of a bind than Arius—at least regarding his opinion of the Son as a "middle thing" between the Father and the creatures.

<summary>This page analyzes the theological difficulties surrounding the term "homoousios" (consubstantial) at the Council of Nicaea, noting that the bishops struggled to define whether the Father and Son shared a "specific" or "numerical" unity. The author suggests that the terminology remained ambiguous and was interpreted inconsistently in the years following the council.</summary>

<keywords>Council of Nicaea, homoousios, consubstantial, Alexander of Alexandria, Hosius of Cordoba, Athanasius, Eusebius of Caesarea, Sabellianism, numerical unity, Christology, logos, sophia</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Council of Nicaea (325 AD), specifically moving from the term "consubstantial" to the doctrine of the eternal generation of the Son and the varying interpretations held by the Church Fathers and Emperor Constantine.</meta>

<page-num>308</page-num>
->### Attempt at a History<-

The second point established at the aforementioned synod was the **eternal generation of the Son**: that He is as eternal as the Father, and that no moment can be imagined in which the Father existed before the Son.

Alexander had certainly asserted this in the strictest terms. However, that not everyone thought exactly as he did can be seen from the approval found among the Council Fathers for Constantine’s explanation: that the Son, even without being begotten, had always existed within the Father in terms of **potentiality** before His actual generation. This was an explanation that any Arian could willingly sign, as they did not doubt the eternal existence of the Son in terms of possibility, but denied His eternal existence in terms of **actuality**.

<margin>The internal Word <note>original: "Λόγος ἐνδιάθετος" (*Logos endiathetos*). This Stoic philosophical term refers to a thought or reason held within the mind before it is spoken aloud.</note>.</margin>
This bordered closely on the opinion of the **internal Word**. In this regard, the Son would have had no advantage over created beings—at least according to the concepts of those Church Fathers who considered the spirit world to be an **emanation** <note>The idea that all things flow out from the essence of God.</note> from God. For these, too, existed within God in terms of potentiality from eternity before they began to actually exist. If this was not Alexander’s opinion, how could he approve of it? According to his entire system, it could not be his view. If it were, it deserved to be condemned no less than Arianism itself.

<meta>catchword: How</meta>

---

<page-num>309</page-num>
->### of Arianism.<-

How much, however, still remained to be decided and investigated regarding this entire question that was not determined or discussed at this Council! Here, a fruitful seed was sown for even greater disputes, quarrels, and schisms, just as there was no lack of different parties on both sides in the periods that followed. These consequences were not considered at the time, either because people were afraid to delve deeper into the details of this difficult matter, or because the main objective—namely the formal condemnation of Arius and his followers, which was closest to the heart of the Alexandrian party—had now been achieved and settled.

When one examines the doctrine as it was established at the Synod of Nicaea, it cannot be denied that it already had its predecessors among the orthodox teachers of the church. This is true even if one cannot agree with Bull (24) <note>George Bull (1634–1710), an English bishop and theologian known for defending the Nicene Creed.</note> that the Nicene conception was held by all preceding church teachers without doing the greatest violence to their writings. But it is also undeniable that the ideas of Arius and Alexander both had their adherents among the orthodox teachers. There were few who shared Arius's opinion completely; indeed, even among those who took Arius's side, most were of the group later called **Semi-**

(25) Bull, *Defense of the Nicene Faith* <note>original Latin: "Defensio Fidei Nicaenae."</note>.

held the Arian opinion; so too were there certainly uncommonly few among the preceding teachers of the Church who understood <term>consubstantiality</term> <note>original: "Homousie" (*homoousia*). The doctrine that the Son is of the "same substance" as the Father.</note> in the way it was established by the Fathers at Nicaea. (25).

It would not have been difficult, moreover, to unite the disputing parties for the honor of religion and civil happiness. However, since each of them—and especially the Alexandrian faction—regarded and portrayed the opposing view as blasphemy, a union was impossible. Furthermore, the force with which the opposing party was suppressed at Nicaea—to the point that those who thought differently were even considered and punished as rebels and criminals of <term>lèse-majesté</term> <note>The crime of violating majesty, or an offense against the dignity of the sovereign; here, dissent is treated as a state crime.</note>—could result in nothing other than the unhappy consequences that the continuation of the Arian history will teach.
